> Thus, of those very things which are perceived by sight, no other animal perceives beauty, grace, or the symmetry of parts. Nature and reason, transferring this analogy from the eyes to the mind, think that beauty, consistency, and order should be even more strictly maintained in our decisions and deeds. From these elements is forged and fashioned that "moral excellence" <term>original: honestum; refers to honorable or virtuous conduct</term> which we seek; which, even if it were not celebrated, would still be honorable; and which, even if praised by no one, is by its very nature praiseworthy. You see, indeed, the very form and face of moral excellence; if it could be seen with the eyes, it would awaken a marvelous love for wisdom.
->*Cicero, On Duties, Book 1, Chapter 4.* <note>original: Cic. de Off. lib. 1. c. 4.</note><-

It frames the study of ethics and aesthetics as an inquiry into innate human perceptions, supported by classical Stoic philosophy.</summary>

> Therefore, of those very things which are perceived by sight, no other animal perceives beauty, grace, and the symmetry of parts. Nature and reason, transferring this analogy from the eyes to the mind, think that beauty, consistency, and order are even more to be preserved in our decisions and actions. Reason is careful not to do anything unseemly or effeminate, and in all opinions and deeds, it avoids doing or thinking anything lustful. From these things is forged and accomplished that "Honorable" quality which we seek; which, even if it were not celebrated, would still be honorable, and which we truly say is praiseworthy by its own nature, even if it were praised by no one. <note>original: "Itaque eorum ipsorum, quæ adspectu sentiuntur, nullum aliud animal pulchritudinem, venustatem, convenientiam partium sentit: quam similitudinem natura ratioque ab oculis ad animum transferens, multo etiam magis pulchritudinem, constantiam, ordinem in consiliis factisque conservandam putat, cavetque ne quid indecore effeminateve faciat; tum in omnibus et opinionibus et factis ne quid libidinose aut faciat aut cogitet. Quibus ex rebus conflatur et efficitur id, quod quærimus, honestum; quod, etiam si nobilitatum non sit, tamen honestum sit, quodque vere dicimus, etiam si a nullo laudetur, natura esse laudabile."</note> **Cicero, *On Duties*, Book 1, Chapter 4.**

<meta>This page begins the preface, establishing the philosophical framework for the work by defining the relationship between human nature, reason, and the pursuit of happiness.</meta>

## THE
# PREFACE.

There is no part of Philosophy of more importance than a just knowledge of human nature and its various *powers and dispositions. Our recent inquiries have been very much employed regarding our understanding and the several methods of obtaining truth. We generally acknowledge that the importance of any truth is nothing other than its moment <note>original: "moment"; here meaning its weight or significance</note>, or efficacy to make men happy, or to give them the greatest and most lasting pleasure; and wisdom denotes only a capacity of pursuing this end by the best means. It must surely then be of the greatest importance to have distinct conceptions of this end itself, as well as of the means necessary to obtain it; so that we may find out which are the greatest and most lasting pleasures, and not employ our reason, after all our laborious improvements of it, in trifling pursuits. It is to be feared, indeed, that most of our studies without this inquiry will be of very little use to us; for they seem to have scarce*

<summary>The author argues that understanding human nature and the sources of lasting happiness is the most vital branch of philosophy, as reason is wasted if directed toward trivial goals.</summary>
<keywords>human nature, philosophy, happiness, wisdom, reason, pursuit of pleasure</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of existing philosophical frameworks regarding human pleasure and knowledge, transitioning from the limitations of "speculative knowledge" to a deeper inquiry into human nature.</meta>

<header>The PREFACE.</header>

any other *tendency than to lead us into speculative knowledge <note>original: "speculative Knowledge it self"; knowledge gained through theoretical contemplation rather than practical application</note> itself. Nor are we distinctly told how it is that knowledge or truth is pleasant to us.*

This *consideration set the author of the following papers about inquiring into the various pleasures which human nature is capable of receiving. We shall generally find in our modern philosophical writings nothing further on this head than some bare division of them into "sensible" and "rational," and some trite, commonplace arguments to prove the latter to be more valuable than the former. Our sensible pleasures are slightly passed over and explained only by some instances in tastes, smells, sounds, or such like—which men of any tolerable reflection generally look upon as very trifling satisfactions. Our rational pleasures have had much the same kind of treatment. We are seldom taught any other notion of rational pleasure than that which we have upon reflecting on our possession of, or claim to, those objects which may be occasions of pleasure. Such objects we call advantageous. But advantage, or interest, cannot be distinctly conceived until we know what these pleasures are which advantageous objects are apt to excite, and what senses or powers of perception we have regarding such objects. We may perhaps see reason to imagine such an inqui-*

<summary>The author argues that contemporary philosophy fails to adequately explain why truth is pleasing or to distinguish between different types of pleasure beyond basic categories. He proposes a deeper investigation into the human "powers of perception" to better understand what makes objects advantageous or satisfying.</summary>

<keywords>speculative knowledge, human nature, sensible pleasure, rational pleasure, advantage, interest, powers of perception, philosophy of emotion</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---

# The PREFACE.

<margin>The importance of proving that virtue is the agent's greatest happiness.</margin>
It is of more importance in morals to prove what we call the *reality* of *virtue*—or that it is the *surest happiness* of the *person acting*—than one would at first imagine.

In reflecting upon our external *senses*, we plainly see that our *perceptions* of *pleasure* or *pain* do not depend *directly* on our *will*. *Objects* do not please us simply because we want them to. The presence of some *objects* necessarily pleases us, and the presence of others just as necessarily displeases us. Nor can we, by our *will*, procure *pleasure* or avoid *pain* in any other way than by seeking the former kind of *objects* and avoiding the latter. By the very frame of our nature, the one is made the occasion of *delight*, and the other of *dissatisfaction*.

The same *observation* holds true for all our other *pleasures* and *pains*; for there are many other sorts of *objects* which please or displease us as necessarily as physical *objects* do when they act upon our *organs of sense* <note>original: "Organs of Sense"; referring to the eyes, ears, nose, etc.</note>. There is scarcely any *object* that our *minds* are occupied with which is not constituted as the necessary cause of some *pleasure* or *pain*. Thus, we find ourselves pleased with a regular *form*, a piece of *architecture* or *painting*, a *musical composition* <note>original: "Composition of Notes"</note>, a mathematical *theorem*, an *action*, an *affection* <note>original: "Affection"; here meaning a person's disposition or emotional state</note>, or a *character*.

<summary>The author argues that pleasure and pain are not chosen by the will but are necessary responses dictated by human nature, applying this logic to both physical sensations and the appreciation of art, logic, and morality.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, happiness, senses, will, aesthetics, moral philosophy, perception</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory argument regarding the origins of human pleasure and the definition of our internal perceptions.</meta>

vi
# The PREFACE.

*And we are aware that this pleasure necessarily arises from the contemplation of the idea, which is then present to our minds with all its circumstances, although some of these ideas have nothing of what we call sensory perception in them. In those ideas that do involve the senses, the pleasure arises from some uniformity, order, arrangement, or imitation, and not from the simple ideas of color, sound, or a specific shape considered separately.*

These inclinations to be pleased with any forms or ideas that we observe, the author chooses to call **SENSES**. He distinguishes them from the powers that commonly go by that name by calling our power of perceiving the beauty of regularity, order, and harmony an **INTERNAL SENSE**. Furthermore, that inclination to be pleased by contemplating the affections, actions, or characters of rational beings—which we call virtuous—he labels the **MORAL SENSE**.

His principal design is to show:
> "That human nature was not left entirely 
> indifferent to the matter of virtue, as if 
> it had to form for itself observations 
> concerning the advantage or disadvantage 
> of actions and regulate its conduct 
> accordingly."

The weakness of our reason, and the distractions arising from the infirmity <note>original: "avocations arising from the Infirmity"</note>

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence with the catchword "mity" for "infirmity," to be continued on the following page.</meta>

<summary>The author defines the "Internal Sense" (appreciation of beauty and order) and the "Moral Sense" (appreciation of virtue), arguing that humans possess an innate blueprint for morality rather than relying solely on calculated reason.</summary>
<keywords>Internal Sense, Moral Sense, Virtue, Aesthetics, Human Nature, Reason vs. Instinct, Francis Hutcheson</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the origins of human morality, specifically addressing the limitations of pure reason in guiding conduct.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>

# The PREFACE.

The infirmities and necessities of our nature are so great that very few of mankind could have formed those long deductions of reason which might show some actions to be, on the whole, advantageous to the agent and their contraries pernicious. The **AUTHOR of Nature** <note>A common Enlightenment term for God as the creator of the natural order.</note> has much better furnished us for a virtuous conduct than our moralists seem to imagine. He has done this by providing almost as quick and powerful instructions as we have for the preservation of our bodies. He has made Virtue a lovely form to excite our pursuit of it, and has given us strong affections to be the springs of each virtuous action.

**THIS** moral sense of beauty in actions and affections may appear strange at first view. Some of our moralists themselves are offended at it in my **LORD SHAFTESBURY** <note>Anthony Ashley-Cooper, 3rd Earl of Shaftesbury (1671–1713), a philosopher who argued that humans have an innate "moral sense."</note>; they are so accustomed to deduce every approval or aversion from rational views of self-interest (except perhaps in the simple ideas of the external senses). They also have such a horror of "innate ideas" <note>The philosophical concept that the mind is born with existing knowledge, a theory famously rejected by John Locke.</note>, which they imagine this theory borders upon. But this moral sense has no relation to innate ideas, as will appear in the second treatise. Our gentlemen of good taste can tell us of a great many senses, tastes, and relishes for beauty, harmony, and imitation in painting and poetry; and may we not also find in—

<summary>The author argues that God provided humans with a "moral sense" and natural affections to guide virtuous behavior, rather than forcing us to rely solely on complex rational calculations of self-interest.</summary>
<keywords>Moral Sense, Virtue, Lord Shaftesbury, Innate Ideas, Ethics, Aesthetics, Human Nature</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory argument regarding the natural human capacity for aesthetic and moral judgment, contrasting modern academic stiffness with the grace of ancient philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
# The PREFACE.

*Does mankind have a relish for beauty in characters and in manners? I fear we have made philosophy, as well as religion—through our foolish management of it—into such an austere and awkward form that a gentleman cannot easily bring himself to like it. Those who are strangers to it can scarcely bear to hear our description of it. It is so much changed from what was once the delight of the finest gentlemen among the ancients, and their recreation after the hurry of public affairs!*

In the first treatise, the author perhaps in some instances has gone too far in supposing a greater agreement among mankind in their sense of beauty than experience will perhaps confirm. However, all he is concerned about is to show "that there is some sense of beauty natural to men." We find just as great an agreement among men in their tastes for forms as we do in their external senses <note>The five physical senses: sight, hearing, touch, taste, and smell.</note>, which everyone agrees are natural; and we find that pleasure or pain, delight or aversion, are naturally joined to their perceptions. If the reader is convinced that the mind is determined <note>original: "Determinations of the Mind"; meaning the mind's natural tendency or inclination.</note> to be pleased with forms, proportions, resemblances, and theorems, it will be no difficult matter to understand another superior sense natural to men, which inclines them to be pleased with actions, characters, and affections. This is the **moral sense**,

<sig>A 2</sig>

<summary>The author argues that philosophy has become unnecessarily grim and asserts that humans possess a natural "sense of beauty" for physical forms, which serves as evidence for a corresponding "moral sense" regarding human conduct.</summary>
<keywords>Aesthetics, Moral Sense, Ancient Philosophy, Sense of Beauty, Human Nature, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory remarks regarding the origins of our aesthetic and moral judgments, transitioning from the general definition of the "internal senses" to their development in the human mind.</meta>

# The PREFACE.

*which makes the subject of the second treatise.*

The proper occasions for perception through our external senses occur to us as soon as we come into the world; for this reason, perhaps, we easily consider these senses to be natural. However, the objects of the superior senses—those of beauty and virtue—generally do not appear so early. It is probably some time before children reflect, or at least let us know that they reflect, upon proportion and similarity, or upon affections, characters, and temperaments. It also takes time for them to recognize the external actions which are evidence of these internal qualities. Because of this delay, we imagine that their sense of beauty and their moral sentiments regarding actions must be entirely due to instruction and education. In reality, it is just as easy to conceive how a character or a temperament—as soon as they are observed—may be established by NATURE as a necessary cause of pleasure or an object of approval, just like a taste or a sound; even though it takes some time before these objects are presented to our observation.

If the author were not so hesitant about his own work—considering it too insignificant to have any great names mentioned within it—he would have publicly acknowledged his obligations to a certain LORD <note>This likely refers to Lord Molesworth or Lord Shaftesbury, who influenced Hutcheson's theories on the "moral sense."</note> (whose name would have carried no small authority with

<summary>The author argues that while the senses of beauty and virtue develop later than physical senses, they are nonetheless innate "natural" faculties rather than products of mere education. He also begins a humble acknowledgment of an unnamed noble patron's influence on his work.</summary>

<keywords>innate ideas, moral sense, aesthetics, nature vs nurture, Francis Hutcheson, perception, virtue, beauty</keywords>

WHATEVER faults the *ingenious* <note>original: "Ingenious" — here meaning clever or intellectually minded readers</note> may find with the *author's performance* <note>original: "Performance" — referring to the work or the writing itself</note>, he hopes nobody will find anything in it contrary to *religion* or *good manners* <note>original: "good Manners" — meaning social ethics or moral conduct</note>; and he shall be well pleased if he gives the *learned world* an occasion to examine these *subjects* more thoroughly, which are, he presumes, of *very considerable importance*. The chief *ground* of his *assurance* that his *opinions* in the main are just, is this: that as he took the first *hints* of them from some of the *greatest writers of antiquity* <note>original: "Antiquity" — referring to the classical authors of ancient Greece and Rome</note>, so the *more he has conversed with them* <note>original: "convers'd with them" — meaning to have studied or read their works deeply</note>, *he finds his illustrations the more conformable to their sentiments.*

-> # AN INQUIRY CONCERNING BEAUTY, ORDER, etc. <-

---

-> ## SECTION I. <-

### *Concerning some Powers of Perception, distinct from what is generally understood by Sensation.*

To make the following observations understood, it may be necessary to state beforehand <note>original: "premise"</note> some definitions and observations—either universally acknowledged or sufficiently proved by many writers, both ancient and modern—concerning our perceptions called sensations and the actions of the mind that result from <note>original: "consequent upon"</note> them.

*Article* 1. Those *ideas* that are raised in the mind upon the presence of external objects <note>Completing "Ob-" with the catchword "jects"</note>...

<summary>This page introduces the first section of the inquiry, which focuses on defining human perception and sensations as a foundation for understanding beauty and order.</summary>
<keywords>perception, sensation, ideas, beauty, order, definitions, philosophy of mind</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions started on the previous page, specifically concluding the definition of "objects" and moving into the categorization of the senses and the powers of the mind.</meta>

-> # An INQUIRY concerning <-

1. objects, and their acting upon our bodies, are called *sensations*. We find that the mind in such cases is passive and has no power to directly prevent the perception or idea, or to change <note>original: "vary"</note> it at its reception, as long as we keep our bodies in a state fit to be acted upon by the external object.

2. When two perceptions are entirely different from each other, or agree in nothing but the general idea of sensation, we call the powers of receiving those different perceptions *different senses*. Thus, seeing and hearing denote the different powers of receiving the ideas of colors and sounds. And although colors have vast differences among themselves, as do sounds, yet there is a greater agreement among the most opposite colors than between any color and a sound; therefore <note>original: "Hence"</note>, we call all colors perceptions of the *same sense*. All the various senses seem to have their distinct organs, except touch <note>original: "Feeling"</note>, which is to some degree diffused over the whole body.

3. The mind has a power of combining <note>original: "compounding"</note> ideas that were received separately, of comparing their objects by means of the ideas, and of observing their relations and proportions; of enlarging and diminishing its ideas at pleasure, or in any certain ratio,

<summary>This page defines sensations as passive perceptions of external objects and explains how we categorize different senses based on the types of ideas (like color or sound) they receive. It also outlines the mind's ability to combine, compare, and scale these ideas once they are received.</summary>
<keywords>sensations, perceptions, different senses, organs of sense, compounding ideas, mental powers, philosophy of perception</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mind's powers, moving from the ability to compare and scale ideas to the concept of abstraction and the definition of substances through sensory data.</meta>

-> # BEAUTY, ORDER, etc. <-

<margin>Sect. 1.</margin> or degree; and of considering separately each of the simple ideas, which might perhaps have been impressed jointly in the sensation. This last operation we commonly call *abstraction*.

4. The ideas of *substances* <note>In this context, a substance refers to a physical object or material entity.</note> are compounded of the various simple ideas that were jointly impressed upon us when they presented themselves to our senses. We define substances only by enumerating <note>listing or counting</note> these sensible ideas. Such definitions may create an idea clear enough of the substance in the mind of someone who has never immediately perceived that substance, provided they have separately received through their senses all the simple ideas that make up the composition of the complex idea of the defined substance. But if they have not received any of these ideas, or lack the senses necessary for the perception of them, no definition can ever create in them any idea of that sense in which they are deficient.

5. Many of our sensory perceptions <note>original: "sensitive Perceptions"</note> are immediately pleasant or painful, and that without any knowledge of the cause of this pleasure or pain, or how the objects excite it, or are the occasions of it; or without seeing to what further advantage or detriment the use of such objects might tend. Nor would the most accurate knowledge of these things change <note>original: "vary"</note> either the pleasure or pain of the perception,

6. 8. The only sensory pleasure that our philosophers seem to consider is that which accompanies the simple ideas of sensation <note>original: "simple Ideas of Sensation"; referring to basic sensory inputs like a single color or a single sound</note>. But there are vastly greater pleasures in those complex ideas of objects which receive the names of "Beautiful," "Regular," or "Harmonious." One is more pleased with a view <note>original: "Prospect"</note> of the sun rising among settled clouds and coloring their edges, with a starry sky <note>original: "Hemisphere"</note>, a fine landscape <note>original: "Landskip"</note>, or a symmetrical <note>original: "regular"</note> building, than with a clear blue sky, a smooth sea, or a large open plain that is not diversified by woods, hills, waters, or buildings. So in music, the pleasure of a fine composition is incomparably greater than that of any single note, no matter how sweet, full, or resonant <note>original: "swelling"</note> it may be.

**Harmony** also refers to our pleasant ideas arising from the composition of sounds; and a "good ear" (as it is generally understood) refers to the power of perceiving this pleasure. In the following sections, an attempt is made to discover the immediate cause of these pleasant ideas, or what real quality in objects normally triggers them.

10. **It** is of no consequence whether we call these ideas of beauty and harmony "perceptions of the external senses" of sight and hearing, or not. I would prefer to call our power of perceiving these ideas an *internal sense*, if only for the convenience of distinguishing them from other sensations of seeing and hearing which people may experience without any perception of beauty or harmony. Experience clearly shows that many people possess the senses of sight and hearing perfectly well in the common sense of the words. They can identify individual notes as being higher, lower, sharper, or flatter when played separately. 13. This superior power of perception is rightly called *a sense* because of its **affinity** <note>original: "Affinity"; meaning a natural similarity or connection</note> to the other senses. This is because the pleasure does not arise from any knowledge of principles, proportions, causes, or the usefulness of the object; instead, it strikes us immediately with the idea of beauty. Furthermore, even the most accurate knowledge does not increase this specific pleasure of beauty, though it may add <note>original: "super-add"</note> a separate, rational pleasure based on prospects of personal advantage or from the increase of knowledge itself.

14. Additionally, the ideas of beauty and harmony—like other ideas perceived through the senses—are necessarily pleasant to us, as well as being immediately so. Neither our own willpower <note>original: "Resolution"</note> nor any prospect of advantage or disadvantage can change the beauty or ugliness <note>original: "Deformity"</note> of an object. **Section 1.** The *Sense of Beauty* derived from *objects*, which makes them seem "good" to us, is very distinct from our *desire* for them once they are perceived that way. Our *desire for beauty* may be balanced against *rewards* or *threats*, but our *sense* of it can never be; just as the *fear of death* or the *love of life* might make us choose and desire a bitter *medicine* <note>original: "potion"</note>, or reject those *foods* <note>original: "meats"</note> that our *sense of taste* would normally recommend as pleasant. And yet, no prospect of *benefit* <note>original: "advantage"</note> or *fear of harm* <note>original: "evil"</note> can make that medicine taste agreeable to the sense, or make those foods taste disagreeable, if they were not already so before that prospect existed. It is exactly the same regarding the *sense of beauty and harmony*. The fact that the pursuit of such *objects* is frequently neglected due to prospects of *profit*, an *aversion to labor*, or any other *motive of self-interest* <note>original: "self-love"</note> does not prove that we have no sense of beauty; it only proves that our *desire* for it may be outweighed by a stronger desire. In the same way, the fact that *gold* is heavier than *silver* is never used as proof that silver lacks *weight* <note>original: "gravity"</note>.

By * **Absolute Beauty**, we mean only that beauty which we perceive in objects without comparing them to anything external that the object is supposed to imitate or represent. Examples include the beauty perceived in the works of nature, man-made forms, geometric figures, and mathematical theorems. **Comparative** or **Relative Beauty** is that which we perceive in objects commonly considered to be imitations or resemblances of something else. These two kinds of beauty occupy the following three sections.

### *Of Original or Absolute Beauty.*

1. And let it be observed here that our inquiry concerns only the qualities that are beautiful to humans, or the foundation of their sense of beauty. For, as was hinted above, beauty always relates to the perception of some mind; and when we later show how generally the objects we encounter are beautiful, we mean they are agreeable to the human senses. There are many objects which seem in no way beautiful to humans, yet we see a variety of other animals who seem delighted with them. They may have senses constructed differently than those of humans, and may have ideas of beauty triggered by objects of a completely different form. We see animals adapted for every place; and what appears crude <note>original: "rude"</note> and shapeless, or loathsome to humans, may be a paradise to them.

2. To more clearly discover the general foundation or cause of the ideas of beauty among humans, it will be necessary to consider it first in its simpler forms, such as those we find in regular figures. We may perhaps find that the same foundation extends to all the more complex species of beauty.

3. The figures that trigger the ideas of beauty in us seem to be those in which there is *Uniformity amidst Variety*. There

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

Section 2. There are many conceptions of objects that are agreeable for other reasons, such as grandeur, novelty, sanctity, and some others that shall be mentioned <note>original: "touched at"</note> later.* But what we call beautiful in objects—to speak in a mathematical style—seems to exist in a compound ratio of uniformity and variety. So, where the uniformity of bodies is equal, the beauty increases according to the variety; and where the variety is equal, the beauty increases according to the uniformity. This will be clear from examples.

**First**, variety increases beauty when uniformity is equal. The beauty of an equilateral triangle is less than that of a square, which is less than that of a pentagon <note>a five-sided polygon</note>; and this, in turn, is surpassed by the hexagon. Indeed, when the number of sides is greatly increased, the proportion of the sides to the radius or diameter of the figure is so easily lost to our observation that beauty does not always increase with the number of sides. Additionally, the lack of parallelism in the sides of heptagons <note>a seven-sided polygon</note> and other figures with an odd number of sides may also diminish their beauty.

Similarly, in solids <note>three-dimensional shapes</note>, the icosahedron <note>original: "Eicosiedron"; a 20-faced solid</note> surpasses the dodecahedron <note>a 12-faced solid</note>, and this surpasses the octahedron <note>an 8-faced solid</note>, which is still more beautiful than the cube; and this, again, surpasses the regular pyramid <note>likely referring to a tetrahedron, a 4-faced solid</note>. The obvious

<summary>The author explains his mathematical formula for beauty: it is a "compound ratio" of uniformity and variety. He illustrates this by showing how geometric shapes become more beautiful as they gain more sides, provided they remain regular and balanced.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 16 regarding the "compound ratio" of beauty, specifically how increasing variety or uniformity affects our perception of geometric shapes.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>BEAUTY, ORDER, &c.</header>

The basis <note>original: "Ground"</note> of this is that greater *variety* enhances beauty when there is equal *uniformity*.

<margin>Section 2.</margin>
Greater *uniformity* increases *beauty* when the *variety* is equal, as seen in these examples: an *equilateral triangle*, or even an *isosceles triangle*, surpasses the *scalene* <note>original: "Scalenum"</note>; a *square* surpasses the *rhombus* or *lozenge*, and this in turn surpasses the *rhomboid* <note>original: "Rhomboides"</note>, which is still more beautiful than the *trapezium* or any figure with irregularly curved sides. Similarly, the regular solids vastly surpass all other solids with an equal number of flat <note>original: "plain"</note> surfaces. This is observable not only in the five perfectly regular solids <note>the Platonic solids: tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron</note>, but in all those that have significant *uniformity*, such as *cylinders*, *prisms*, *pyramids*, and *obelisks*. These please every eye more than any rough <note>original: "rude"</note> figures where there is no *unity* or resemblance among the parts.

We have examples <note>original: "INSTANCES"</note> of the compound *ratio* when comparing *circles* or *spheres* with *ellipses* or *spheroids* that are not very elongated <note>original: "eccentric"</note>. We also see it when comparing compound solids—such as the *exoctahedron* <note>a 14-faced Archimedean solid; original: "Exoctaedron"</note> and the *icosidodecahedron* <note>a 32-faced Archimedean solid; original: "Eicosidodecaedron"</note>—with the perfectly regular solids from which they are composed. We shall find that the lack of that most perfect *uniformity* seen in the latter is balanced <note>original: "compensated"</note> by the greater *variety* in the former, so that their *beauty* is nearly equal.

4. **These** observations would probably hold true for the most part and could be confirmed by the judgment of children regarding simpler shapes <note>original: "Figures"</note>, where the *variety* is not too great for them to understand. However uncertain some of these specific examples may seem, we can constantly observe <note>original: "perpetually to be observ'd"</note> that children are fond of all regular shapes in their little games <note>original: "Diversions"</note>, even though those shapes are no more convenient or useful to them than the shapes of ordinary pebbles. We see how early they display a *taste* or sense of *beauty* by their desire to see buildings, regular gardens, or even depictions of them in paintings of any kind.

5. **It** is this same foundation that supports our sense of *beauty* in the works of NATURE. In every part of the world that we call *beautiful*, there is vast *uniformity* amidst almost infinite *variety*. Many parts of the universe do not seem to be designed for the use of humans at all; indeed, we are only acquainted with a very small part of it. The shapes and movements of the great celestial bodies <note>original: "great Bodys," referring to planets and stars</note> are not obvious to our senses but are discovered through reasoning and reflection based on many long observations. Yet, as far as we can discover through our senses, or expand our knowledge and imagination through reasoning, we generally find the

...structure, order, and motion align with our sense of *beauty*. Not every individual object in *nature* appears *beautiful* to us; however, there is a vast abundance of *beauty* across most of the objects that we encounter through our senses or through reasoning based on observation. For instance, without even mentioning the apparent position of the celestial bodies <note>original: "heavenly Bodys"</note> on the surface of a vast sphere—which is entirely caused by the limitations of our sight in judging distances—the shapes of all the great bodies in the universe are nearly spherical. The paths of their movements <note>original: "Orbits of their Revolutions"</note> are generally elliptical <note>original: "Elliptick"</note> and do not have great eccentricity <term>eccentricity: the degree to which an orbit deviates from a perfect circle</term> in those that we constantly observe. These are shapes of great *uniformity* and are therefore pleasing to us.

Furthermore, moving past the less obvious *uniformity* found in the proportions of their mass <note>original: "Quantitys of Matter"</note>, distances, and times of revolution relative to one another; what could provide a better example of *uniformity amidst variety* than the constant pattern <note>original: "Tenour"</note> of revolutions over nearly equal periods of time? Each *planet* rotates on its axis and orbits the central fire, the SUN, throughout all the ages for which we have records, and stays in nearly the same orbit. Because of this, after certain periods, all the same appearances are renewed again. 6. FURTHERMORE <note>original: "AGAIN"</note>, regarding the dry part of the surface of our globe, much of which is covered with a very pleasant, mild <note>original: "inoffensive"</note> color <note>The author likely refers to the green of grass and foliage, which was historically considered restful and "inoffensive" to the eyes compared to the glare of the sun or sand.</note>, how *beautifully* it is diversified with various degrees of *light* and *shade*! ...uniformity is not only observable in the overall form; indeed, it is not even most exact in that regard. Rather, it is found in the structure of their smallest <note>original: "minutest"</note> parts, which no eye without the help of a microscope <note>original: "unassisted with Glasses"</note> can see. In the almost infinite number of *leaves, fruit, seeds,* and *flowers* of any one species, we see an exact *uniformity* in the structure and arrangement <note>original: "Situation"</note> of the smallest fibers. This is the *beauty* that delights a skilled <note>original: "ingenious"</note> *botanist*. Indeed, what vast *uniformity* and regularity of shape <note>original: "Figure"</note> is found in every individual *plant*, *leaf*, or *flower*! In all trees and most smaller plants, the stalks or trunks are nearly *cylinders* or regular *prisms*. The branches are similar to their respective trunks and emerge at nearly regular distances, provided no accidents hinder their natural growth. In one *species*, the branches grow in pairs on opposite sides; the vertical plane <note>original: "perpendicular Plain"</note> of the pair directly above intersects the plane of the pair below it at nearly right angles. In another *species*, the branches spring out individually and alternately all around the trunk at nearly equal distances. In yet other *species*, the branches sprout in clusters <note>original: "Knots"</note> around the trunk, one cluster for each year. In every *species*, all the branches in the first shoots maintain the same angles relative to their trunk; these, in turn, sprout into smaller branches in exactly the same way as the trunks. Nor should we...

Section 2. I will pass over the great *unity* <note>original: "Unity"</note> of colors in all the flowers of the same plant or tree, and often across a whole *species*. There is also an exact *consistency* <note>original: "Agreement"</note> in the many shaded *transitions* between opposite colors, which generally match in all flowers of the same plant, and often even within the entire *species*.

8. AGAIN, regarding the *beauty* of *animals*—whether in their internal structure (which we learn about through *experiment* and long observation) or their outward form—we find vast *uniformity* among all known species in the structure of those parts upon which life most immediately depends. And how amazing is this *unity* of *mechanism* <note>original: "Mechanism"; here meaning the biological "machinery" of the body</note>! We find that an almost infinite diversity of motions—including *walking*, *running*, *flying*, and *swimming*; all their serious efforts for *self-preservation*; and all their playful *contortions* when they are lively and sporting—are performed in all their various limbs by one simple *design* <note>original: "Contrivance"</note>: a contracting *muscle*. This single mechanism is applied with inconceivable variety to achieve all these different goals. Various other mechanisms <note>original: "Engines"</note> might have achieved the same ends, but then there would have been less *uniformity*. The *beauty* of *animal systems* and of individual animals would have been much less if this surprising *unity* of *mechanism* were removed from them.

9. AMONG animals of the same species, the *unity* <note>original: "Unity"</note> is very obvious, and this resemblance is the very basis for our ranking them into such classes or species, despite the great diversities in size, color, and shape which are observed even in those called the same species. And then, within each individual, what vast *beauty* arises from the exact resemblance of all the external symmetrical limbs <note>original: "double Members"</note> to each other! This seems to be the universal intention of NATURE when no accident or injury prevents it. We see that a lack of this resemblance never fails to be seen as an imperfection and a lack of *beauty*, even if no other practical disadvantage <note>original: "Inconvenience"</note> follows; such as when the eyes are not exactly alike, or when one arm or leg is a little shorter or smaller than its counterpart.

10. The sculptor knows what proportion of each part of the face to the whole face is most pleasing, and can tell us the same regarding the proportion of the face to the body (or any of its parts), and between the widths <note>original: "Diameters"</note> and lengths of each limb. 12. If our reasoning about the *nature* of *fluids* is accurate, the vast stores of *water* provide an example of *uniformity* in *nature* beyond imagination. This is clear when we reflect upon the almost infinite multitude of small, polished, smooth spheres which must be assumed to be formed in all parts of this globe. The same *uniformity* probably exists among the parts of other *fluids* as well as water; and the same must be observed in several other natural bodies, such as *salts*, *sulfurs* <note>original: "Sulphurs"; here referring to crystalline or mineral substances</note>, and the like. Their uniform properties probably depend upon a *uniformity* in the shapes <note>original: "Figures"</note> of their individual parts.

13. Under the heading of *Original Beauty* <note>The author uses this term to describe "absolute" beauty found in an object's own form, rather than "relative" beauty which comes from imitating something else.</note>, we may include *harmony*, or the *beauty of sound* (if that expression can be allowed). We include it here because *harmony* is not usually conceived as an imitation of anything else. *Harmony* often creates pleasure in those who do not know the cause of it; and yet, the foundation of this pleasure is known to be a sort of *uniformity*. When the various vibrations of one note regularly coincide with the vibrations of another, they make an agreeable composition; and such notes are called *chords*. Thus the vibrations of a—

[A]ny one note coincides in time with every second vibration of its *octave*; and two vibrations of any note coincide with three of its *fifth*; and so on for the rest of the *chords* <note>original: "Chords"; here referring to musical intervals or harmonies</note>. Now, good compositions—besides the frequent use of these *chords*—must maintain a general *unity* of key, and an *uniformity* among the parts in *bars, risings, fallings, and endings* <note>original: "Closes"</note>. The necessity of this will appear by observing the *dissonance* which would arise from joining parts of different tunes together as if they were one, even though both were separately pleasant. A greater *uniformity* is also observable among the *basses, tenors, and trebles* of the same tune. Some other powers of *music* may be considered later <note>original: "*" refers to a footnote: "See Sect. 6. Art. 12."</note>.

14. BUT in all these instances of *beauty*, let it be observed that the pleasure is communicated to those who have never reflected on this general foundation. All that is claimed here is this: that the pleasant sensation arises only from objects in which there is *uniformity amidst variety*. We may have the sensation without knowing what causes it, just as a man’s taste may suggest ideas of sweets, acids, and bitters, even though he is ignorant of the shapes of the small bodies <note>original: "small Bodys"; referring to the corpuscular theory of matter where taste was thought to be caused by the physical shape of particles</note>, or their motions, which trigger these perceptions in him.

## SECTION III.

->*On the Beauty of Theorems.*<-

1. THE *Beauty* of *Theorems* <note>original: "Theorems"; here meaning established mathematical or philosophical principles</note>, or universal *Truths* that have been proven, deserves its own separate consideration. This is because it is quite different in nature from the types of *Beauty* mentioned previously. And yet, there is no other area where we see such an amazing *Variety* combined with *Uniformity*. From this combination arises a very great *Pleasure*, which is distinct from any expectation of further *Advantage* or practical gain.

2. FOR in a single *Theorem*, we may find included—with the most exact *Agreement*—an infinite multitude of particular *Truths*; in fact, we often find an *Infinity* of *Infinities*. It is true that the necessity of forming abstract *Ideas* and universal *Theorems* perhaps arises from the limitations of our minds, which cannot hold an infinite multitude of individual *Ideas* or *Judgments* all at once. Yet, this power of abstraction provides evidence that the human *Capacity* is much larger than our *Imagination* alone would suggest. Thus, for instance, the 47th *Proposition* of the first Book of **EUCLID'S** *Elements* <note>original: "47th Proposition of the first Book of EUCLID'S Elements"; this is the Pythagorean theorem, which states that in a right-angled triangle, the square of the hypotenuse is equal to the sum of the squares of the other two sides ($a^2 + b^2 = c^2$)</note> contains an infinite multitude of *Truths* concerning the infinite possible sizes of right-angled *Triangles* as you make the *Area* greater or less. Within each of these sizes, you may also find an infinite multitude of differently shaped *Triangles* as you
<meta>The text continues on the next page, likely explaining how varying the proportions of the triangle's sides still adheres to the same universal rule.</meta>

<summary>This page explores the aesthetic beauty of mathematical theorems, arguing that they provide intellectual pleasure because a single universal rule (uniformity) accounts for an infinite number of specific cases (variety). The author uses the Pythagorean theorem as a primary example of this "infinity of infinities" contained within one proof.</summary>
<keywords>mathematical beauty, theorems, Euclid, Pythagorean theorem, uniformity, variety, abstract ideas, geometry, aesthetics</keywords>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

...vary the proportion of the *base* to the *height* <note>original: "Perpendicular"</note>; all of these *infinities* of *infinites* agree with the general *theorem*. In the same way, a single *calculus calculation* <note>original: "Fluxional Calculation"; refers to the method of fluxions, Sir Isaac Newton's form of calculus</note> can determine the *tangents* of all *algebraic curves*. There are infinite *orders* and *types* <note>original: "Species"</note> of these *curves* possible; within each *type* there are infinite *sizes* or *magnitudes* of *areas*; for each *size*, infinite *individuals*; and for each *individual curve*, an *infinity* of *points* from which *tangents* may be drawn. Yet all these *infinities* of *infinites* are exactly contained within the general *theorem*, which determines the lengths of the *subtangents* <note>the portion of the x-axis between the point where the tangent crosses it and the point directly below the point of tangency</note> or their proportion to the *abscissa* <note>the x-coordinate, or the horizontal distance of a point from the vertical axis</note>.

Section 3
</margin>

3. SO THAT we may better perceive how this *agreement* or *unity* of an *infinity* of *objects* within a general *theorem* serves as the *foundation* for the *beauty* or *pleasure* we feel when discovering them, let us compare the *satisfaction* we find in such *discoveries* to the uneasy state of mind we experience when we can only measure *lines* or *surfaces* using a *scale*, or when we conduct *experiments* that we cannot simplify into any general *rule* <note>original: "Canon"</note>. In such a state, we are merely piling up a *multitude* of specific, disconnected <note>original: "incoherent"</note> *observations*. While each of these *trials* reveals a new *truth*, they offer no *pleasure* or *beauty* despite their *variety*, until we can find some sort of *unity* or organize them into a general *rule*.

<meta>4. AGAIN,</meta>

<summary>This page argues that mathematical beauty arises from "unity amidst variety," where a single general theorem explains an infinite number of specific cases. The author contrasts the intellectual pleasure of discovering these universal laws with the tedious dissatisfaction of collecting disconnected data points.</summary>
<keywords>mathematical beauty, calculus, theorems, tangents, unity amidst variety, scientific method, geometry</keywords>

<header>BEAUTY, ORDER, &c. Section 3.</header>

4. AGAIN, let us take a *metaphysical* axiom, such as this: *Every whole is greater than its part*. We shall find no *beauty* in contemplating it. Although this proposition contains an infinity of particular truths, the *unity* is insignificant <note>original: "inconsiderable"</note> because they all agree only in a vague, undetermined concept of "whole" and "part," and in an indefinite excess of the former over the latter—which is sometimes great and sometimes small. However, if we should hear that a *cylinder* is greater than its inscribed *sphere*, and that the sphere is again greater than a *cone* of the same height <note>original: "Altitude"</note> and base diameter, we shall find no pleasure in this knowledge of a general relationship of "greater" and "less" without any precise difference or proportion. But when we see the universal, exact agreement of all possible sizes of such systems of solids—that they preserve to each other the constant *ratio* of 3, 2, 1 <note>This refers to Archimedes' famous discovery regarding the volumes of a cylinder, sphere, and cone</note>—how beautiful the *theorem* is, and how we are delighted <note>original: "ravish'd"</note> with its first *discovery*!

5. THERE is another *beauty* in propositions which cannot be omitted: when one *theorem* contains a vast multitude of *corollaries* <note>propositions that follow logically and easily from one already proved</note> easily deducible from it. Thus, that *theorem* which gives us the *equation* of a *curve*, from which perhaps most of its *properties* may be deduced,

<meta>catchword: deduced,</meta>

<keywords>metaphysics, geometry, Archimedes, mathematical beauty, theorems, corollaries, ratios, cylinder, sphere, cone</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical beauty, focusing on how complex properties in geometry, physics, and morality can be derived from single, universal principles.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>Section 3.</margin>
deduced, does in some way please and satisfy our mind more than any other proposition. Such a *theorem* is also the 35th of the first book of **EUCLID** <note>original: "35th of the 1st Book of EUCLID"; this theorem states that parallelograms on the same base and between the same parallels are equal in area</note>, from which the entire art of measuring straight-lined <note>original: "right-lin'd"</note> areas is deduced by resolution into *triangles*. These triangles are the halves of various *parallelograms*, and each of these is respectively equal to a *rectangle* formed by the *base* and the perpendicular *height* <note>original: "Altitude"</note>. The 47th theorem of the first book <note>The Pythagorean theorem, which relates the sides of a right-angled triangle</note> is another of similar *beauty*, as are many others. In the search for *nature*, there is a similar *beauty* in the knowledge of certain great principles or universal forces from which innumerable effects flow. Such is *gravitation* in **Sir ISAAC NEWTON’S** system <note>original: "Scheme"</note>; such also is the knowledge of the origin of *rights*—*perfect*, *imperfect*, and *external*; alienable and unalienable—along with the manner of their transfer <note>original: "Translations"; in this context, the legal or moral transfer of a right from one person to another</note>. From these, the greatest part of moral duties may be deduced across the various relations of human life.

It is easy to see how people are charmed with the *beauty* of such knowledge, quite apart from its usefulness. This beauty encourages them to deduce the *properties* of every geometric figure from a single origin <note>original: "Genesis"; the method by which a geometric figure is generated</note>, and to demonstrate mechanical forces from one *theorem* regarding the composition of motion <note>The principle that a single motion can be the result of two or more combined forces</note>—even after they already have sufficient knowledge and certainty of all these truths through distinct, independent demonstrations. And this pleasure we enjoy

even

<summary>Hutcheson explains that beauty in knowledge arises from "unity in variety," where a single theorem (like those of Euclid or Newton's law of gravity) explains a vast multitude of specific phenomena.</summary>

Nor could the love of *fame* drive us to such organized methods of *reasoning* if we were not aware that people are naturally pleased by them through this *internal sense* of their *beauty*.

It is just as easy to see the absurd whims <note>original: "Whimsys"</note> men have been led into by this sense of *beauty*, and by a foolish attempt to find it in other sciences just as they do in *mathematics*. This was probably what motivated **Descartes** to undertake that ambitious project of deriving all human knowledge from a single proposition: *I think, therefore I am* <note>original: "Cogito, ergo sum"</note>. Meanwhile, others with just as little sense argued that the principle, "It is impossible for the same thing to be and not to be at the same time" <note>original: "Impossibile est idem simul esse & non esse"</note>, had a much better claim to the title of the *absolutely first principle of human knowledge* <note>original: "Principium humanæ Cognitionis absolutè primum"</note>. Mr. **Leibniz** <note>original: "Leibnitz"</note> had a similar fondness for his favorite principle—that there is a *sufficient reason* for everything in *nature*. He bragged to Dr. **Clarke** about the wonders he had achieved in the intellectual world with its help; however, his learned opponent <note>original: "Antagonist"</note> seems to think Leibniz did not have "sufficient reason" for his boasting. If we look at specific sciences, we can see in the systems created by learned men the disadvantages caused by this love of *uni-* <note>catchword: formity. The word continues on the next page as "uniformity."</note>

6. This delight which accompanies the sciences, or universal *theorems*, may really be called a kind of *sensation*. As there is a vast diversity of possible proportions and different kinds of *uniformity*, there is enough room for that diversity of tastes <note>original: "Fancys"</note> observed in *architecture*, *gardening*, and similar *arts* across different *nations*. They all may have *uniformity*, even though the parts in one may differ from those in another: *Chinese* or *Persian* buildings are not like *Grecian* and *Roman* ones, and yet the former has its own *uniformity* of the various parts to each other and to the whole, just as the latter does. In that kind of *architecture* which EUROPEANS call *regular*, the *uniformity* of parts is very obvious: the several parts are *regular figures*, and are either equal or at least similar...

There is the same proportion observed everywhere in the same row between the *diameters* of pillars and their *heights*, their *capitals* <note>The decorative head or top part of a column.</note>, the *diameters* of *arches*, the *heights* of the *pedestals*, the *projections* of the *cornice* <note>The decorative horizontal molding that crowns a building or furniture.</note>, and all ornaments in each of our five *orders* <note>The five classical styles of architecture: Doric, Ionic, Corinthian, Tuscan, and Composite.</note>. And though other countries do not follow the *Greek* or *Roman* proportions, they still maintain a consistent proportion, a *uniformity*, and a *resemblance* of corresponding figures. Every deviation in one part from the proportion observed in the rest of the building is displeasing to every eye and destroys—or at least diminishes—the *beauty* of the whole.

7. The same might be observed through all other works of *art*, even down to the lowliest <note>original: "meanest"</note> *utensil*. We shall always find that the *beauty* of every one of these has the same foundation: *uniformity amidst variety*. Without this, they appear base, irregular, and deformed.

## S E C T. IV.

<image-desc>typographic ornament consisting of two stacked horizontal wavy lines</image-desc>

### Of Relative or Comparative Beauty.

1. If the preceding thoughts concerning the foundation of *absolute beauty* <note>The beauty inherent in an object itself, such as a geometric shape or a flower, independent of any external reference.</note> are correct, we may easily understand what *relative beauty* consists of. All *beauty* is relative to the sense of some mind perceiving it; but what we call *relative* is that which is apprehended in any *object* commonly considered as an *imitation* of some original. This *beauty* is founded on a *conformity*, or a kind of *unity*, between the original and the copy. The original may be either some object in *nature* or some *established idea*; for if there is any known *idea* used as a standard, and rules to fix this image or *idea* by, we may create a *beautiful imitation*. 

Thus a *sculptor* <note>original: "statuary"</note>, *painter*, or *poet* may please us with a depiction of HERCULES if his piece retains that *grandeur* and those marks of *strength* and *courage* which we imagine in that hero. Furthermore, to achieve *comparative beauty* alone, it is not necessary for there to be any *beauty* in the original; the imitation of *absolute beauty* may indeed make a more lovely piece overall, and yet an exact imitation shall still be *beautiful* even if the original were entirely void of it. Thus, the *deformities* <note>original: "defarmitys"</note> of old *age* in a *picture*, the rudest

2. The same observation holds true in the descriptions of poets, regarding either *natural objects* or *people*. This *relative beauty* is what they should principally endeavor to obtain as the unique *beauty* of their works. By the *stories of manners* <note>original Latin: *Moratæ Fabulæ*</note>, or the *characters* <note>original Greek: ἤθη (*ethe*)</note> of ARISTOTLE, we are not to understand "virtuous manners" in a moral sense, but rather a *just representation* of manners or characters as they exist in *nature*. This requires that the *actions* and *sentiments* be suited to the characters of the *people* to whom they are ascribed in epic and dramatic poetry. 

Perhaps very good reasons may be suggested from the nature of our *passions* <note>In the 18th century, "passions" referred to the whole range of human emotions and psychological drives.</note> to prove that a poet should not, by choice, draw the *finest characters* possible for *virtue*. These characters, indeed, considered abstractly, might give more pleasure and have more *beauty* than the *imperfect* ones which occur in life with a mixture of good and evil. However, it may suffice for now to argue against this choice by noting that we have more lively ideas of *imperfect men* with all their passions than of *morally perfect heroes*. And further, through consciousness of our own state, we are more nearly touched and affected by *imperfect characters*; since in them we see represented, in the persons of others, the *contrasts* of inclinations and the *struggles* between the passions of *self-love* and those of *honor* and *virtue*, which we often feel in our own breasts. This is the perfection of *beauty* for which **Homer** is justly admired, as well as for the *variety* of his *characters*. <note>Homer’s characters, such as Achilles or Agamemnon, are famously complex and flawed rather than purely "good" or "evil," making them more relatable to the human experience.</note>

3. **Many** other beauties of poetry may be reduced under this class of *relative beauty*: *Probability* <note>In 18th-century aesthetics, "probability" refers to verisimilitude—making the story seem believable or true to life so the reader can engage with it.</note> is absolutely necessary to make us imagine *resemblance*; it is by *resemblance* that *similitudes*, *metaphors*, and *allegories* are made beautiful, whether the subject itself or the thing compared to it has *beauty* or not. The *beauty* indeed is greater when both have some original *beauty* or *dignity* as well as *resemblance*; and this is the foundation of the rule of studying *decency* <note>original: "decency"; here meaning appropriateness or fitness of style to the subject matter.</note> in *metaphors* and *similes* as well as *likeness*. The *measures* and *cadence* <note>This refers to poetic meter and the rhythmic flow of language.</note> are instances of *harmony* and come under the head of *absolute beauty*.

An **aged oak** in the **mountains** shall represent an **old empire**, and a **flame** seizing a **wood** shall represent a **war**. In short, everything in **nature**, due to our strange inclination to find **resemblances**, is made to represent other things, even those most remote from it—especially the **passions** and circumstances of **human nature** in which we are more personally concerned. To confirm this and provide examples, one only needs to look into **Homer** or **Virgil**. A productive **imagination** could find in a **grove** or a **forest** an emblem for every **character in a society** <note>original: "commonwealth"; referring to the various roles and personalities found within a state or organized community.</note>, and every shift of temperament or station in life.

Concerning that kind of *comparative beauty* <note>Hutcheson distinguishes between "original" beauty (inherent in the form itself) and "comparative" beauty (which comes from the object's relationship to something else it represents or imitates).</note> which has a necessary relation to some established *idea*, we may observe that some *works* of *art* acquire a *distinct beauty* by their *correspondence* to some universally supposed *intention* in the *artist* <note>original: "Artificer"; meaning the craftsman or creator.</note>, or the persons who employed him. To obtain this *beauty*, they sometimes do not form their works to achieve the highest perfection of *original beauty* considered on its own; this is because a composition of this *relative beauty*, combined with some degree of the *original kind*, may give more *pleasure* than a more *perfect original beauty* would separately. 

Thus, we see that strict *regularity* in the layout of *gardens*—such as *parterres* <note>A level space in a garden occupied by an ornamental arrangement of flowerbeds.</note>, *vistas*, and *parallel walks*—is often neglected in order to obtain an *imitation* of *nature*, even in some of its *wildness*. We are more pleased with this *imitation*, especially when the *scene* is large and spacious, than with the more confined *exactness* of *regular work*. 

So, likewise, in the *monuments* erected in honor of deceased *heroes*: although a *cylinder*, a *prism*, or a *regular solid* may have more *original beauty* than a very sharp *pyramid* or *obelisk*, the latter pleases more by better fulfilling the supposed *intentions* of *stability* and being conspicuous <note>In this context, being easily seen or prominent.</note>. For the same reason, *cubes* or *square prisms* are generally chosen for the *pedestals* of *statues*, and not

7. This *beauty* arising from *correspondence* to *intention* would open a new scene of *beauty* in the works of NATURE to curious observers. This is done by considering how the *mechanical structure* of the various parts known to us seems adapted to the perfection of that specific part, and yet remains subordinate to the good of some *system* or *whole*. We generally suppose the good of the *greatest whole*, or of all beings, to have been the *intention* of the AUTHOR of *nature* <note>original: "AUTHOR of Nature"; an Enlightenment-era term for God, viewed as a rational designer or architect of the universe.</note>; and we cannot avoid being pleased when we see any part of this *design* executed in the *systems* we are acquainted with.

The observations already made on this subject are widely known, being found in the treatises of our recent contributors to *mechanical philosophy* <term>mechanical philosophy: a scientific theory popular in the 17th and 18th centuries that viewed the universe and living things as complex machines operating under mathematical laws.</term>. We need only note here the pleasure that any person receives from seeing a design well-executed through ingenious *mechanism*, even when their own personal benefit is not involved in any way. We also experience a pleasant sensation when we discover the purpose for which a complex *machine* is adapted—especially if we previously had a general knowledge of the *machine* without seeing how its parts were suited to carry out that specific design.

The arguments by which we prove *reason* and *design* in a creator <note>original: "Cause"; here referring to the "First Cause" or God.</note> based on the *beauty* of the *effects* are so frequently used in some of the most important subjects <note>Hutcheson refers here to "natural theology," the practice of using reason and observation of the natural world to prove the existence of God.</note> that it is necessary to examine them more closely. We must see how far these arguments remain valid and what degree of evidence they provide.

### SECTION V.

->*Concerning our Reasonings about Design and Wisdom in the First Cause, Based on the Beauty or Regularity of the Effects.*<-

1. There seems to be no necessary connection between our pleasing ideas of *beauty* and the *uniformity* or *regularity* of objects based on the *nature* of things themselves, prior to the specific *constitution* <term>constitution: the inherent physical or mental makeup of a being.</term> given to us by the AUTHOR of our nature, which has made such forms pleasant to us. Other *minds* might possibly be designed in such a way that they receive no *pleasure* from *uniformity*; and we actually find that the same regular forms do not seem to please all known animals equally, as will likely be shown later. 

Therefore, let us make the most difficult *supposition* <note>original: "unfavourable Supposition"; Hutcheson is setting up a "worst-case scenario" for his argument to see if it still holds true.</note> possible for the current argument: suppose that the way our senses are built to approve of *uniformity* is merely arbitrary on the part of the AUTHOR of our nature, and that there are an infinite number of possible *tastes* or *relishes* for *beauty*. Under this assumption, it would be impossible to throw together fifty or a hundred pebbles without them forming an agreeable home for some animal or other and appearing beautiful to it. If that were the case, it is clear that from the *perception* of *beauty* in

<summary>Hutcheson argues that our appreciation for beauty and uniformity is not an inherent necessity of nature, but a specific trait given to us by a Creator. He begins a "thought experiment" to test whether we can still infer a designer even if we assume that any random arrangement of matter could be considered "beautiful" by some creature.</summary>
<keywords>beauty, uniformity, design, Author of Nature, perception, aesthetic taste, Francis Hutcheson, teleology</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues Section 5, which explores how we can infer a wise Creator (the "First Cause") by looking at the beauty and regularity of the world. Hutcheson is currently responding to the argument that beauty might just be a lucky accident.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>BEAUTY, ORDER, &c.</header>

<margin>Sect. 5.</margin>
...in any one *Effect*, we should have no reason to conclude that there was *Design* in the *Cause*. A *Sense* of beauty could, after all, be constructed in such a way as to be pleased with the kind of *Irregularity* that results from an *undirected Force*. However, consider that there are an *infinite* number of possible *Forms* into which any *System* of matter might be organized, an *infinite* number of *Places* where *Animals* might live, and an *infinite* number of possible *Relishes* or *Senses* that could exist. Given the vastness of space, the probability that any one *Animal* would, by *Chance*, be placed in an environment that matches its specific *Taste* must be at least as improbable as *infinite* to *one*. It is even more unreasonable to expect *Chance* to ensure that a multitude of *Animals* who share the same *Sense* of *Beauty* would all find *agreeable Places* to live.

2. It is also certain that in any one *System* of *Matter*, an *Undirected Force* has the same *Probability* of producing a *regular Form* as it does of producing any one specific *irregular* form—

---

\* This *Expression* is taken from the *Cartesian Scheme* <note>The "Cartesian Scheme" refers to the physics of René Descartes, who attempted to explain the universe through mechanical laws of motion.</note>, in which the *Author* of *Nature* is supposed to have intentionally impressed a general *Force* or "striving toward motion" <note>original: *Conatus ad motum*</note> upon the *Mass* of *Matter*, without giving it any specific *Direction* whatsoever. This nonsensical *Notion* became so prevalent—and people have so many confused *Conceptions* of *Nature* and *Chance* as if they were real *Beings* operating without *Wisdom* or *Design*—that it may be useful to show that their very absurd *assumption* <note>original: *Postulatum*</note> is completely insufficient to explain the *Regularity* we see in the *World*, even if we granted them their premise. This is what is attempted in the first fourteen Articles of this Section.

<summary>Hutcheson argues that the mathematical probability of animals finding environments that match their sense of beauty by mere chance is effectively zero. He also begins to critique the Cartesian idea that random force could explain the orderly appearance of the universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Section 5, demonstrating that the mathematical probability of "chance" or an undirected force creating a regular, beautiful universe is effectively zero. Hutcheson is using geometry to show how irregular forms always vastly outnumber regular ones.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>Section 5.</margin>one specific irregular form of the same degree of complexity <note>original: Complication</note>. 

But still, the *irregular forms* into which any system may be arranged exceed the *regular* ones in number, just as *infinity* exceeds *one* <note>original: Unity</note>. What holds true in *one* small system will hold true in a *thousand*, a *million*, or a *universe*, with even greater certainty: namely, that the possible *irregular forms* are infinitely more numerous than the *regular* ones. 

For instance, the *area* of a square inch is capable of containing an infinity of *regular forms*, such as the *equilateral triangle*, the *square*, the *pentagon*, *hexagon*, *heptagon*, and so on. But for every one regular form, there are an infinity of irregular ones. There are an infinity of *scalene triangles* <note>original: *Scalena*; a triangle with three unequal sides</note> for the one equilateral triangle; an infinity of *trapezoids* <note>original: *Trapezia*; four-sided shapes with no parallel sides</note> for the one square; an infinity of irregular *pentagons* for the one regular one; and so on. 

Therefore, assuming any one system is moved by an *undirected* <note>original: undesigning</note> force, it is infinitely more probable that it will organize itself into an *irregular form* than a *regular* one. Thus, the chance that a system of *six* parts, when moved, will *not* take the shape of a regular *hexagon* is at least *infinite* to *one*. The more complex we make the system, the greater the improbability <note>original: hazard</note> becomes, for a very obvious reason. 

We see this confirmed by our constant experience: *regularity* never arises from any *undirected* force of ours. From this, we conclude that wherever there is any *regularity* in the arrangement of a system capable of many other positions, there must have been *design* in the cause.

<summary>Hutcheson argues that because irregular geometric forms infinitely outnumber regular ones, the existence of order in the universe cannot be an accident; it must be the result of intentional design.</summary>

And the force of this evidence increases according to the multiplicity <note>original: Multiplicity; a large number or variety</note> of parts employed.

But this conclusion is too hasty unless further proof is introduced. What leads us to it is this: people who have a sense of *beauty* in *regularity* are generally led, in all their arrangements of bodies, to study some kind of *regularity* and almost never design *irregularity*. Because of this, we judge other beings the same way—assuming that they also study *regularity*. We presume there is an *intention* in the *cause* <note>original: Intention in the Cause; a conscious purpose behind the creator or force that made something</note> wherever we see order, while always viewing *irregularity* as a sign of a lack of *design*. 

However, if other agents <note>original: Agents; those capable of action or creation</note> have different senses of *beauty*, or if they have no sense of it at all, *irregularity* might be designed just as intentionally as *regularity*. In that case, observe that there would be just as much reason to conclude there was *design* in the *cause* from an irregular effect as from a regular one. Since there are an infinity of other *forms* possible besides the specific irregular one produced, and since to a being who lacks a sense of *beauty*, all forms are as...

"...internal structure, and often—nay, always—in their external appearance." To remove this objection, it will be sufficient to show "that the multitude of cases where *perceivable dissimilarity* could have happened is still infinitely more than all the cases in which *perceivable similarity* might occur." Therefore, the same reasoning holds for *perceivable similarity* as it does for *mathematically exact* similarity. And again, "the cases of *obvious dissimilarity* exceed the possible cases of *obvious similarity* in the same way that *infinity exceeds one*."

13. To prove both of these assertions, let us consider a simple instance: a *trapezium* <note>original: "Trapezium"; a four-sided shape where no sides are parallel.</note> with an *area* of one square foot shall appear *similar* to another as long as no *side* differs by more than 1/10th of an inch, or no *angle* in one exceeds the corresponding angle in the other by more than perhaps ten minutes <note>A "minute" is a unit of angular measurement equal to 1/60th of a degree.</note>. Now, this tenth of an inch is *infinitely* divisible, as are the ten minutes; therefore, the cases of *unnoticeable dissimilarity* occurring under the guise of *apparent similarity* are truly *infinite*. But then it is also plain that there are an *infinity* of different, noticeably dissimilar *trapezia*—even of the same *area*—created by varying a *side* by one-tenth, two-tenths, three-tenths, and so on, and varying the *angles* and another *side* so as to keep the *area* equal. Now, in each of these infinite *degrees* of *perceivable dissimilarity*, the several tenths are

Considering this, how much vastly greater must the number of all possible *perceivable dissimilarities* be in such complex *bodies* as *legs, arms, eyes, arteries, veins, and skeletons?*

14. Regarding the *dissimilarities* between animals of the same species: it is similarly obvious that the possible cases of *obvious dissimilarity* <note>original: "gross Dissimilarity"</note> are infinite. Moreover, every single case of *obvious dissimilarity* also contains within it all the possible cases of *unnoticeable dissimilarity*. For example, if we consider all animals of a species to be *obviously similar* as long as no limb exceeded the standard shape in length or diameter by more than a third of the head's size, it is clear that there are an infinite number of possible *obvious dissimilarities*. Furthermore, in each of these cases of *obvious dissimilarity*, there are an infinite number of cases of *finer dissimilarity*, since one-third of a head's length can be infinitely divided. To use a simple example: two *cockle shells* <note>A cockle is a heart-shaped bivalve shell often found in pairs that fit together.</note> that fit together naturally may have an infinite number of *unnoticeable differences*; however, there are still an infinite number of possible *perceivable differences*. Since the addition of every member or part to a complex machine <note>In the 18th century, living organisms were often compared to "machines" to describe their functional parts.</note> creates a new infinity of cases in which *noticeable dissimilarity* might occur, this infinity—combined with the infinite cases of the previous parts—raises the odds against chance by a power of infinity.

This should sufficiently show us the absurdity of the *Cartesian* or *Epicurean theories* <note>The "Cartesian" (after René Descartes) and "Epicurean" (after Epicurus) hypotheses suggested that the universe could have formed through the mechanical motion of matter without direct divine intervention.</note>, even if we grant them their *postulate* <note>original: "Postulatum"; an assumed starting point for an argument.</note> of *random force* applied to *infinite matter*. This seems to be almost a mathematical demonstration of *purposeful design* in the *universe*.
