**WISDOM** denotes the *pursuing* of the *best* goals <note>original: "Ends"</note> by the *best* means; and therefore, before we can use any *effect* to prove that its *cause* is *wise*, we must know what that *cause* or *agent* <note>A being capable of acting or exerting power.</note> considers to be "best." Among men who find pleasure in contemplating *uniformity* <note>Consistency and regularity in design.</note>, the *beauty* of the results is an argument for wisdom because this beauty is "good" to them. But the same argument would not hold true for a *being* who lacks this *sense* of *beauty*. Therefore, the *beauty* apparent to us in *nature* will not, on its own, prove *wisdom* in its cause, unless this cause—or **AUTHOR** of nature—is assumed to be **BENEVOLENT**. If the Author is benevolent, then the happiness of mankind is desirable, or "good," to the **SUPREME CAUSE**; and the specific form of nature that pleases us becomes an argument for his *wisdom*.

The strength of this argument increases in proportion to the degree of *beauty* produced in *nature* and exposed to the view of any rational being. Since we assume a *benevolent* **DEITY**, all the apparent *beauty* produced is evidence of the execution of a *benevolent design* intended to give us the pleasures of *beauty*. But what more immediately proves *wisdom* is this: when we see any *machine* with a vast *complication* of parts actually achieving a *purpose* <note>original: "End"</note>, we justly conclude:

> “ been the effect of *chance*, it must have
> “ been intended for that *purpose*, which is ob-

17. **There** is also another kind of *beauty* that is pleasing to our *senses*, from which we conclude there is *wisdom* in the *cause* as well as *design*; and that is when we see many useful or beautiful *effects arising from one general cause.* There is a very good reason for people to reach this conclusion. Self-interest must lead *beings* of limited power—who are incapable of a great variety of operations and are distracted by them—to choose this *frugal economy* <note>original: "frugal Oeconomy"; the efficient management of resources or forces to achieve maximum results with minimum effort.</note> of their forces, and to look upon such management as evidence of *wisdom* in other *beings* like themselves.

The uniform principle of *Gravity* simultaneously preserves the *planets* in their orbits, gives *cohesion* to the parts of each globe, and *stability* to mountains, hills, and *artificial structures*. It raises the sea in tides and sinks them again, restraining them within their channels. It drains the earth of its superfluous moisture through *rivers*; it raises *vapors* by its influence on the *air* and brings them down again in *rains*. It gives a uniform *pressure* to our *atmosphere*, which is necessary to our bodies in general, and more especially to *inspiration* <term>the act of drawing breath into the lungs</term> in breathing. Finally, it furnishes us with a universal *movement* <note>original: "Movement," referring here to a source of mechanical power, like a falling weight or water.</note> capable of being applied in innumerable engines. 

How incomparably more *beautiful* is this structure than if we supposed so many *distinct volitions* <note>individual acts of will or separate decisions</note> in the <term>DEITY</term>, producing every particular effect and preventing some of the accidental evils which naturally <note>original: "casually flow," meaning occurring as a side effect of the law rather than by specific intent</note> flow from the *general law*! Since any deviation from **general laws**, unless for very extraordinary occasions, must be seen as evidence of **inconstancy** and **weakness** rather than of steady **wisdom** and **power**, such deviations would actually weaken the best arguments we have for the wisdom and power of the **universal MIND**."

## SECTION VI.

### On the Universality of the Sense of Beauty among Men.

1. WE previously suggested <note>original: "insinuated"</note> that "**Beauty** has a relation to **some perceiving power**." Consequently, since we do not know the **variety** of senses that may exist among animals, there is no form in **Nature** about which we can say, "That it has no **Beauty**," for it may still please some **perceiving power**. But our **inquiry** is confined to human beings. Before we examine the **universality** of this **sense of beauty**, or our shared agreement in approving of **uniformity**, it may be proper to consider whether this **sense of beauty** makes some objects disagreeable or painful to us, just as the other **senses** which give us pleasure also give us pain. It is obvious that many objects give no pleasure to our **sense**; many are certainly void of **beauty**. However, there is no form that seems necessarily disagreeable in itself, as long as we do not fear any other evil from it and do not compare it with anything better of its kind. Many objects are naturally dis-

Section 6 [continued]. ...pleasing and distasteful to our external **senses**, as well as others that are pleasing and agreeable, such as **smells**, **tastes**, and some individual **sounds**. But regarding our **sense** of **beauty**, no **composition** of **objects** (provided they do not produce unpleasant simple ideas <note>In the philosophy of John Locke, "simple ideas" are the most basic units of perception, like a single color or a specific sound.</note>) seems inherently unpleasant or painful in itself, if we had never observed anything better of that kind.

**Deformity** is only the **absence** of **beauty**, or a **deficiency** in the **beauty** expected in any **species**. For example, **bad music** pleases **peasants** <note>original: "Rusticks"</note> who have never heard anything better. Even the **finest ear** is not offended by the **tuning** of **instruments** (as long as it is not too tedious) because no **harmony** is expected; yet a much smaller **dissonance** shall be offensive during a performance where **harmony** is expected. A **rough heap** of **stones** is in no way offensive to someone who would be displeased by **irregularity** in **architecture**, where **beauty** was expected. 

And if there had been a species of the form that we now call **ugly** or **deformed**, and we had never seen or expected greater **beauty**, we would have felt no disgust toward it—although the pleasure from this form would not have been as great as from those we now admire. Our **sense** of **beauty** seems designed to give us positive pleasure, but not positive pain or disgust, beyond what arises from disappointment.

### Section 6.

3. We shall see later <note>See articles 11 and 12 of this section below.</note> that **associations of ideas** make objects appear pleasant and delightful even when they do not naturally provide such pleasure. In the same way, **random connections of ideas** can cause disgust even when there is nothing inherently disagreeable in the form itself. This is the cause of many **irrational** <note>original: "fantastick"</note> aversions to the appearance of certain animals and other shapes. For instance, **pigs** <note>original: "swine"</note>, **snakes** <note>original: "serpents"</note> of all kinds, and some **insects** that are actually quite beautiful, are viewed with hatred by many people who have developed **accidental associations** with them. There is no other explanation for these types of dislikes.

4. But regarding the **universal agreement** of humanity in their **sense of beauty** (derived from **uniformity within variety**), we must look to experience. We acknowledge that all people possess **reason**, since everyone is capable of understanding simple arguments, even if few are capable of following complex **logical proofs** <note>original: "demonstrations"</note>. Similarly, in this case, it is enough to prove that this **sense of beauty is universal** if all people are more pleased with **uniformity** in simple examples than with the opposite—even when they see no practical advantage in it. <margin>Section 6.</margin>

> "As people's capacity for understanding grows, allowing them to receive and compare more complex ideas, they further expand their delight in *Uniformity*. They become pleased with its more complex forms, both *Original* <note>Beauty found in nature or inherent shapes.</note> and *Relative* <note>Beauty found in how well something represents or relates to something else.</note>."

Now let us consider if any person was ever completely lacking in *this sense* [of beauty] in simpler instances. Few tests have been conducted in the simplest examples of *Harmony* <note>In this context, musical or auditory beauty.</note>. This is because as soon as we find that someone's *ear* is not capable of enjoying complex musical compositions, such as our *tunes*, no further effort is spent studying their abilities. 

But regarding *Shapes* <note>original: "Figures"</note>, has any man ever chosen a *trapezium* <note>A four-sided shape with no parallel sides.</note> or any irregular *curve* for the *floor plan* <note>original: "Ichnography"</note> of his house without a specific necessity or some great reason of convenience? Or has anyone ever chosen to make the opposite walls *not parallel* or *unequal* in height? Have *trapeziums*, irregular *polygons*, or *curves* ever been chosen for the shapes of *doors* or *windows*? These shapes might have served their purposes just as well, and choosing them would have often saved much of the time, labor, and expense for the workmen, which is currently spent on fitting stones and timber to *regular forms*. 

Among all the strange <note>original: "fantastick"</note> fashions of *dress*, none was ever completely devoid of *Uniformity*. This is seen, at the very least, in the *symmetry* <note>original: "resemblance"</note> of the two sides of the same *robe*, and in *some general fittingness* to the human form; the...

A GOTH <note>In the 18th century, "Goth" or "Gothic" often referred to the medieval architectural style, which was then considered less "refined" than the classical Roman style.</note>, for instance, is mistaken when, because of his upbringing, he imagines the *Architecture* of his country to be the most perfect. Their *arches* are not one single uniform curve, but they are segments of *similar curves*, and are generally equal within the same rows. Even *Indian buildings* possess some kind of *uniformity*; and many of the EASTERN NATIONS, though they differ much from us, still have great regularity and *beauty* in their style, just as the ROMANS had in theirs. 7. What has been said will probably be agreed upon if we always remember, in our inquiries into the **universal nature** of the **sense of beauty**, "that there may be **real beauty** where there is not the **greatest beauty**; and that there are an infinite number of different forms which all possess some **unity**, and yet differ from each other." Thus, people may have different tastes in **beauty**, and yet **uniformity** remains the **universal** foundation for our approval of any form whatsoever as **beautiful**. We shall find that this is true in the **architecture**, **gardening**, **fashion** <note>original: "Dress"</note>, **carriages** <term>Equipage: a carriage and horses with attendants, or more generally, the fine equipment and accessories belonging to a person of rank.</term>, and **furniture** of houses, even among the most uncultivated nations. In these cases, **uniformity** still pleases, providing no other advantage than the pleasure of contemplating it.

9. The reason for this difference in judgment can be nothing other than this: "that we have distinct names for the **external senses**, but none, or very few, for the **internal** ones." ...of **harmony** <note>original: "Harmony"</note> has been given a name: a "good ear." We are generally led to acknowledge this as a **natural power of perception**, or a **sense** that is somehow distinct from the physical act of **hearing**. Now, it is certain "that there is as necessary a perception of **beauty** upon the presence of **regular objects** as there is of **harmony** upon hearing certain **sounds**."

10. BUT let it be observed here once and for all "that an **internal sense** no more presupposes an **innate idea** <note>an idea present in the mind from birth</note>, or a principle of knowledge, than the **external** senses do." Both are **natural powers of perception**, or **determinations of the mind** to necessarily receive certain ideas from the presence of objects. The **internal sense** is **a passive power of receiving ideas of beauty from all objects in which there is uniformity amidst variety**. Nor does there seem to be anything more difficult in this matter than "that the mind should be always determined to receive the idea of **sweetness** <note>original: "Sweet"</note> when particles of a certain shape enter the pores of the tongue; or to have the idea of **sound** upon any quick **vibration** <note>original: "Undulation"</note> of the air." The physical cause seems to have as little logical connection with its resulting idea in the one case as in the other; and the same Power <note>referring to Nature or a Creator</note> could with equal ease make the former the cause of ideas as the latter.

II. THE **Association of Ideas** <note>referring to the way the mind connects different thoughts or feelings when they are experienced together; see the note at the bottom of the page</note> mentioned earlier is one major cause of the apparent diversity of tastes <note>original: "Fancys"</note> in the **Sense of Beauty**, as well as in the **external senses**. It often causes people to feel an aversion to objects of **beauty**, and a liking for others lacking it <note>original: "void of it"</note>, based on concepts other than those of **beauty** or **ugliness** <note>original: "Deformity"</note>. It may be useful here to give some examples of these **associations**.

The **beauty of trees**, their cool **shades**, and their **fitness** <note>original: "Aptness"</note> for concealment have made **groves** and **woods** the usual retreat for those who love **solitude**—especially the **religious**, the **thoughtful** <note>original: "Pensive"</note>, the **melancholy**, and the **romantic** <note>original: "Amorous"</note>. Do we not find that we have so joined the ideas of these states of mind with those external objects that they always return to our minds together? The cleverness of **pagan priests** <note>original: "Heathen Priests"</note> might have made such dark places the setting for the fictional appearances of their **deities**, and thus we connect ideas of something **divine** to them. We experience a similar effect in our ideas of **churches**, arising from their constant and exclusive use for religious services. The faint light in **Gothic buildings** has produced a similar association with a very foreign

This disagreement happens because many other ideas may either please or displease us depending on the person, their temperament, and their past circumstances. We know how agreeable a very **wild countryside** may be to someone who spent the cheerful days of his youth there, and how disagreeable very **beautiful places** may be if they were the scenes of his misery. This may help us, in many cases, to account for the differences in personal taste without denying the **uniformity** of our internal **sense of beauty**.

## SECTION VII.

### *On the Power of Custom, Education, and Example regarding our Internal Senses.*

1. **Custom**, *education*, and *example* are so often cited in this matter as the cause of our taste <note>original: "Relish"</note> for *beautiful objects*, and for our approval of or delight in a certain *conduct in life* in a *moral sense*, that it is necessary to examine these three specifically. This is to demonstrate:

> “That there is a *natural power* 
> “of *perception*, or a *sense of beauty* in 
> “*objects*, that exists prior to <note>original: "antecedent to"</note> all *custom, education, or example*.”

2. **CUSTOM**, as distinct from the other two, works in this way: regarding actions, it only gives the mind or body a tendency to perform those actions more easily when they have been frequently repeated. However, it never leads us to understand <note>original: "apprehend"</note> them from any perspective other than what we were capable of understanding at first, nor does it give us any new power of perception regarding them. We are naturally capable of feelings of *fear* and *dread* toward any powerful *presence*; and so *custom* may connect the ideas of religious horror <meta>catchword: Horror</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Section 7, where the author begins to argue that the sense of beauty is an innate human faculty rather than a product of social conditioning or habit.</summary>
<keywords>custom, education, example, internal senses, moral sense, perception, innate beauty, Francis Hutcheson</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 79 regarding Section 7, specifically focusing on how "custom" (habit or social conditioning) cannot create new senses or perceptions but only modify existing ones.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>
Section 7.
</margin>
horror of certain buildings; but no amount of **custom** could have made a **being** who was naturally incapable of **fear** experience such ideas. Similarly, if we had no other power of perceiving or forming ideas of actions except for whether they were **advantageous** or **disadvantageous**, then **custom** could only have made us quicker at noticing the **advantage** or **disadvantage** of those actions. But this is not our current focus.

Regarding our approval of, or delight in, external objects: when the **blood** or **spirits** <note>original: "Spirits"; referring to the "animal spirits," a contemporary medical concept thought to be fluids or vapors responsible for sensation and movement</note> that **anatomists** discuss are aroused, quickened, or "fermented" (as they call it) in a pleasant way by medicine or food <note>original: "Nutriment"</note>, or when any **glands** are frequently stimulated to produce secretions, it is certain that to keep the body comfortable, we will find delight in objects of **taste** that are not inherently pleasant on their own. We do this if they promote the agreeable physical state to which the body has become **accustomed**. Furthermore, **custom** will alter the state of the body so much that things which initially caused uncomfortable **sensations** will stop doing so, or perhaps even produce a pleasant idea within that same sense. However, **custom** can never give us an idea from a sense different from those we already had before it. It will never make the **blind** appreciate objects for being **colored**, nor will it make those who have no **taste** appreciate food <note>original: "Meats"</note> as **delicious**, regardless of how much they might like things that proved—

<summary>This page argues that while habit (custom) can influence our physical preferences or tolerance for sensations, it cannot create an entirely new sense or capacity for perception that was not already naturally present.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Section 7 from the previous page, where the author argues that "custom" (habit or social conditioning) cannot create new senses but only refine existing ones. The discussion moves from physical tastes to the aesthetic perception of beauty and harmony.</meta>

strengthening or exhilarating: If our **glands** and the parts around them lacked feeling, and if we perceived no pleasure from certain faster **motions** in the **blood**, then no **habit** <note>original: "Custom"</note> would ever make stimulating or intoxicating fluids or medicines agreeable, provided they were not already pleasing to the **taste**. By the same reasoning, if we did not have a **natural sense** of **beauty** derived from **uniformity**, **habit** could never have led us to imagine any beauty in objects; if we had no **ear**, **habit** could never have given us the pleasures of **harmony**. 

When we possess these **natural senses** beforehand, **habit** may enable us to expand our perspectives further and to receive more complex ideas of **beauty** in bodies, or **harmony** in sounds, by increasing our attention and quickness of **perception**. However, while **habit** may increase our power to receive or compare complex ideas, it seems to weaken rather than strengthen the actual ideas of **beauty**, or the impressions of pleasure we get from regular objects. Otherwise, how would it be possible for anyone to go out into the open air on a sunny day or a clear evening without experiencing the most extravagant raptures—such as **Milton** * describes our **ancestor** <note>Referring to Adam, the first man, in John Milton’s epic poem *Paradise Lost*.</note> experiencing upon his first creation? For certainly, anyone would fall into such a state upon seeing such a scene for the very first time.

<margin>Section 7.</margin>

**Habit** <note>original: "Custom"; in the 18th century, "custom" referred to both personal habit and social conditioning.</note> in a similar way could make it easier for any person to recognize the use of a complex machine and approve of it as beneficial; however, he would never have imagined it to be *beautiful* if he did not possess a *natural sense of beauty*. *Habit* may make us faster at understanding the truth of complex *theorems*, but we all find the pleasure or *beauty of theorems* just as strong at first as we ever do later. *Habit* makes us more capable of remembering and comparing complex ideas, so that we can recognize more complicated *uniformity* <note>The author defines beauty as "uniformity amidst variety."</note> which escapes the observation of *novices* in any *art*. 

But all of this assumes a *natural sense of beauty* in *uniformity*. For if there were nothing in *forms* that was naturally designed to cause pleasure to our senses, no amount of *repetition* of ideas that are neutral—neither pleasurable nor painful, *beautiful* nor *deformed*—could ever have made them become pleasing or displeasing.

3. The effect of **education** is this: that through it, we receive many theoretical <note>original: "speculative"</note> opinions, sometimes true and sometimes false. We are often led to believe that objects may be naturally capable of giving pleasure or pain to our external senses when, in reality, the object has no such qualities. And furthermore, through *education*, there are some strong associations of ideas formed without any logical reason—sometimes by mere accident, as well as

...formed by design, which are very hard for us to break apart later. In this way, aversions are raised toward darkness, to many kinds of food <note>original: "Meats"; in the 18th century, "meat" often referred to food in general, not just animal flesh.</note>, and to certain innocent actions. Unfounded approvals are raised in a similar manner. But in all these instances, *education* never makes us perceive any qualities in objects for which we do not already have naturally capable senses. We know what a sick stomach feels like, and we might believe without reason that very healthy foods will cause it. Through our sight and smell, we receive disagreeable ideas from the food of swine and their pigsties, and perhaps we cannot prevent these ideas from returning to us while we are at the dinner table. However, people who are naturally *blind* were never prejudiced against objects for having a disagreeable color, nor in favor of others for having a beautiful color. They might hear men criticize one color, but they imagine this color to be some completely different quality perceived by their other senses. In the same way, a man naturally lacking a sense of *taste* could never, through any *education*, receive the ideas of flavor or be prejudiced in favor of foods as being delicious. Likewise, if we had no *natural sense of beauty and harmony*, we could never be prejudiced in favor of objects or sounds as being *beautiful* or *harmonious*. *Education* may make an unobservant Goth <note>The author uses "Goth" here as a stereotypical example of someone from a "barbaric" culture with tastes that deviate from classical Greco-Roman standards of beauty.</note> imagine that his countrymen have attained the perfection of *architecture*, and develop an aversion

...toward their enemies the **ROMANS**, may have joined some disagreeable ideas to their very buildings and prompted them to demolish them. However, he would never have formed these prejudices if he were completely lacking a *sense* of *beauty*. Did *blind men* ever debate whether *purple* or *scarlet* was the finer *color*? Or could any *education* bias <note>original: "prejudice"</note> them in favor of either as *colors*?

**THUS**, *education* and *custom* may influence our *internal senses*—where they already exist—by enlarging the capacity of our minds to remember and compare the parts of complex compositions. When the finest objects are presented to us, we become aware of a pleasure far superior to what common works <note>original: "performances"; here referring to artistic or creative works.</note> excite. But all of this assumes that our *sense* of *beauty* is *natural*. 

Instruction in *anatomy*, the observation of *nature*, and the study of those facial *expressions* <note>original: "airs of the countenance"</note> and *postures* <note>original: "attitudes"</note> of the body that accompany any *sentiment*, *action*, or *passion*, may enable us to recognize when there is a faithful imitation. But why should an exact imitation please us when we see it if we did not *naturally* have a *sense* of *beauty* within it? Without that sense, it would be no different than observing the arrangement of fifty or a hundred pebbles thrown at random; no matter how often we directed our attention to them, we would never dream of them becoming *beautiful*.

### Section 7.
<image-desc>A small decorative flourish beneath the section heading.</image-desc>

4. There is an observation worth making regarding how to root out the *prejudices of education* <note>The author refers to biases or irrational beliefs learned during childhood or through cultural conditioning.</note>, which is quite relevant to our current purpose. When a *prejudice* arises from an *association of ideas* that have no natural connection, we must often force ourselves to endure representations of those objects—or use the objects themselves—while they are separated from the disagreeable idea. This may eventually break the unreasonable association, especially if we can link new, agreeable ideas to them. For instance, superstitious opinions are best removed through pleasant conversation with people we respect for their *virtue*, or by seeing such people dismiss those opinions. 

But when a *prejudice* arises from a fear or belief that a *natural evil* <note>In this context, "natural evil" refers to physical harm, pain, or misfortune.</note> is an accompaniment or consequence of an object or action, the remedy depends on the frequency of the perceived threat. If the *evil* is believed to be a constant and immediate accompaniment, then a few trials without suffering any harm will remove the *prejudice*; this is seen in prejudices against certain foods <note>original: "Meats"; in the 18th century, "meat" often referred to food of any kind.</note>. However, when the *evil* is not viewed as a constant companion, but rather as something that might possibly or probably happen at some point, it requires frequent reasoning with ourselves, or a long series of trials without any harm, to remove the *prejudice*. This is the case with our fear of *spirits* in the dark and in churchyards. And when

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>The author explains how to dismantle irrational prejudices, distinguishing between those formed by arbitrary associations and those formed by the fear of potential harm.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to remove irrational prejudices or fears. It explains that when an "evil" consequence is believed to be distant or supernatural, it is much harder to disprove through experience than an immediate physical threat.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>

Section 7.

</margin>
...the *evil* is presented as a consequence perhaps occurring much later, or in a *future state* <note>Referring to the afterlife.</note>, it is then most difficult to remove the *prejudice*. This can only be corrected through the slow processes of reasoning, because in these cases, no practical experiments <note>original: "Trials"</note> can be conducted. This is the situation with *superstitious prejudices* against actions thought to be offensive to **GOD** <note>original: "the DEITY"</note>; this is why such beliefs are so difficult to eradicate.

5. **EXAMPLE** <note>Referring to the influence of observing the behavior and choices of others.</note> seems to work in this way: we are aware that we usually act to gain *pleasure* or our own *private good*, and therefore we naturally imagine that others do the same. Consequently, we conclude there must be some *excellence* <note>original: "Perfection"; in this context, it refers to a desirable quality or value in an object.</note> in the *objects* we see others pursue, and some *evil* in those we see them constantly avoiding.

Alternatively, the *example* of others may serve as a series of experiments for us, helping to remove our fear of *evil* in *objects* we previously disliked. However, all of this depends on our *perception* of *qualities* that our own *senses* can detect. No *example* will ever convince a *blind* or *deaf* person to pursue *objects* for their *colors* or *sounds* <note>original: "Sonorous"</note>. Similarly, *example* could not motivate us to pursue *objects* for being *beautiful* or *harmonious* if we did not already possess a natural *sense* of *beauty* or *harmony*.

<summary>The author explains that superstitious prejudices are hard to break because they involve distant or supernatural consequences that cannot be tested by experience, and argues that observing others' examples only influences us because we share the same underlying natural senses.</summary>

<header>BEAUTY, ORDER, etc.</header>

### Section 7.

**EXAMPLE** <note>In this context, social influence or the habit of following others' opinions.</note> may make us conclude, without any real examination, that our own countrymen have achieved the peak <note>original: "Perfection"</note> of *Beauty* in their *works*. It may lead us to believe there is less *Beauty* in the styles of *Architecture* or *Painting* used in other *nations*, and so we settle for very imperfect forms. Often, the fear of being held in contempt for lacking *taste* or *talent* <note>original: "Genius"</note> makes us join in praising the performances of the famous masters in our own country. This social pressure restrains those who naturally have a fine *talent*, or very sharp *internal senses* <note>The author's term for the innate ability to perceive beauty and harmony.</note>, from studying to reach the greatest perfection. It also makes those with bad *taste* pretend to perceive ideas of *Beauty* even when they do not. 

However, all of this presupposes that we have some *natural power* of receiving ideas of *Beauty* and *Harmony* to begin with. Following the *example* of others cannot achieve anything further, unless it is to lead people to pursue objects through blind faith—seeking some excellence that the pursuer knows they do not actually recognize, or which is perhaps a completely different quality from what is perceived by those who have true *taste* in such matters.

### S E C T. VIII.

#### *Of the Importance of the internal Senses in Life, and the final Causes of them.*
<note>Final causes: The ultimate purpose or goal for which something exists in nature.</note>

1. THE busy part of mankind may look upon these things as airy dreams of an inflamed imagination, which a wise man—who rationally pursues more solid possessions independent of "fancy" <note>original: "Fancy"; here meaning imagination or subjective whims</note>—should despise. However, a little reflection will convince us "that the gratifications of our *internal senses* are as natural, real, and satisfying enjoyments as any 'sensible' <note>original: "sensible"; meaning perceived through the five physical senses</note> pleasure whatsoever;" and "that they are the chief ends for which we commonly pursue *wealth* and *power*." For how is *wealth* or *power* advantageous? How do they make us *happy*, or prove *good* to us? They do so in no other way than as they supply gratifications to our *senses* or faculties of perceiving pleasure.

Now, are these *senses* or faculties only the *external ones*? No. Everyone sees that a small portion of *wealth* or *power* will supply more pleasures of the external *senses* than we can actually enjoy. We know that scarcity often heightens these perceptions more than abundance, which "cloys" <note>original: "cloys"; to sicken or weary with an excess of something, such as food or pleasure</note> the appetite that is necessary for all pleasure in enjoyment.

<summary>This page argues that the pursuit of wealth and power is not an end in itself, but a means to satisfy our "internal senses" of beauty and harmony, which provide more lasting satisfaction than mere physical pleasures.</summary>

In short, the only use of a great *wealth* <note>original: "Fortune"</note>, above a very small one (except for *acts of kindness* <note>original: "good Offices"</note> and *moral pleasures*) must be to supply us with the pleasures of *Beauty, Order*, and *Harmony*.

If their hearts were open to our view, we would see *regularity, propriety* <note>original: "Decency"</note>, and *beauty* as the ultimate goals of their wishes, either for themselves or their descendants. Their imagination is always presenting these things to them as the possible result of their labors; without this, they could never justify their pursuits to themselves.

We shall confine ourselves to a subject where we have some certain foundations to build upon, and only inquire "if we can find any reasons worthy of the great AUTHOR of Nature for making such a connection between regular objects and the pleasure which accompanies our perceptions of them;" or, "what reasons might possibly have influenced him to create the *world* as it currently is, which—as far as we can observe—is everywhere full of *regularity* and *uniformity*."

BUT to return to the questions: what can be found to resolve them may be contained in the following propositions.

1. THE method of gaining knowledge through *universal theorems* <note>original: "universal Theorems"; general mathematical or logical truths that apply across all instances.</note>, and of acting through *universal causes* <note>original: "universal Causes"; the fundamental laws of nature that produce consistent, predictable effects.</note>—so far as we can achieve this—must be most convenient for *beings* of limited understanding and power. This is because it prevents their minds from becoming distracted by a vast number of individual propositions, and saves their physical powers from toil and weariness. Consequently, their *reason*, even without any *sense of beauty*, must approve of such methods when they reflect upon their obvious *advantage*.

2. THOSE objects of contemplation in which there is *uniformity amidst variety* <note>This is the author's core definition of beauty: the balance between a simple, governing rule and a complex set of parts.</note> are more clearly and easily understood and remembered than *irregular objects*. This is because the accurate observation of one or two parts often leads to the knowledge of the whole. Thus, from a *pillar* or two with an intermediate *arch* and *cornice* <term>the decorative, functional molding that crowns a building or wall</term>, we can form a clear idea of a whole regular *building*, if we know of

In contrast, it requires a long period of attention to a vast multiplicity of parts to ascertain or fix the idea of any *irregular form*, or to give any distinct idea of it, or to make us capable of remembering it. This is evident in the forms of *rough rocks*, *pebbles*, and *confused heaps*, even when the number of visible parts is not as great as in *regular forms*. Such *irregular objects* distract the *mind* with *variety*, since we must form a completely different idea for every single visible part.

For if it were not so—if, on the contrary, irregular objects, *particular truths*, and *operations* pleased us—then besides the endless toil this would involve us in, there would arise a perpetual dissatisfaction in all rational agents with themselves. This is because *reason* and *interest* <note>Meaning personal benefit or rational advantage.</note> would lead us toward simple *general causes*, while a contrary *sense* of beauty would make us disapprove of them. 

*Universal theorems* would appear to our understanding as the best means of increasing our knowledge of what might be useful; meanwhile, a contrary *sense* would set us on the search after *singular truths* <note>Individual, isolated facts that do not follow a general rule.</note>. *Thought* and *reflection* would recommend objects with *uniformity amidst variety*, and yet this perverse *instinct* would involve us in labyrinths of confusion and *dissimilarity* <note>original: "Dissimilitude"</note>. And hence

<summary>The author argues that God designed our sense of beauty to find pleasure in 'uniformity amidst variety' so that our natural instincts would align with our rational need for general laws and simple truths. If we were instead attracted to irregularity, our reason and our senses would be in constant, frustrating conflict.</summary>

As to the other question, “What reason might influence the **D**EITY—whom no complexity of tasks could distract or weary—to choose to act by the *simplest means* and *general laws*, and to spread *uniformity*, *proportion*, and *similarity* through all the parts of *nature* which we can observe?” Perhaps there may be some real excellence in this manner of operation, and in these forms, which we do not yet know. reveal himself to them as *Wise* and *Good*, as well as *Powerful*; for in this way, he has given them greater evidence of his wise and good intentions than the whole earth provides. Regarding the operations of the **D**EITY <note>referring to God</note> through *General Laws*, there is a further reason derived from a sense even superior to those already mentioned. This sense conducts even that of *Propriety* <note>original: "Property"; in this context, it refers to the moral fitness or "properties" of an action</note>, or the *Beauty of Action*, which is the foundation of our greatest happiness. For if there were no established rules in the course of nature, there could be no *prudence* or *purposeful design* in men. No practical experience of effects following from causes through regular action could be obtained. Without such regularity, no measure of action could be effective. Since our greatest happiness depends upon our actions—as it may perhaps be shown it does—it follows that the course of nature cannot be governed by a random or unpredictable will. If it were governed by a will and actions of which we could form no expectations, we would be unable to plan our *schemes of action*.

<page-num>101</page-num>
<sig>H 3</sig>

### AN
# INQUIRY
### CONCERNING
## MORAL GOOD *and* EVIL.

---

### INTRODUCTION.

The term **Moral Goodness** refers to our idea of a specific quality perceived in actions that earns approval <note>original: "Approbation"</note> and love toward the person acting from those who receive no personal advantage from the action. **Moral Evil** refers to our idea of an opposite quality, which triggers a feeling of dislike <note>original: "Aversion"</note> and resentment toward the person acting, even among people who are not affected by the action's natural consequences. We must be satisfied with these basic descriptions until we can discover the general foundation for this distinction between actions—why some are seen as morally good and others as evil.

<vocab>Moral Good, Moral Evil, Moral Goodness, Approval, Actor, Resentment, Natural Consequences</vocab>

<term>Moral Goodness</term>: Actions or character traits that society deems virtuous and worthy of praise.
<term>Approbation</term>: Formal approval, praise, or a favorable opinion. Hutcheson uses this to describe the "moral sense" responding to virtue.
<term>Actor</term>: In philosophy, this refers to the "agent" or the person performing the action, rather than a theatrical performer.
<term>Natural Tendency</term>: The inherent likely outcome or consequence of an action in the physical world.

<meta>catchword: THESE</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the core definitions of moral philosophy, defining "Moral Goodness" and "Moral Evil" based on the feelings of approval or dislike they provoke in an observer, regardless of personal gain.</summary>
<keywords>Moral Goodness, Moral Evil, Approbation, Ethics, Human Nature, Moral Sense, Introduction</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the second treatise, expanding on the distinction between "moral" qualities and "natural" qualities of life.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

These descriptions seem to contain a universally acknowledged difference between *moral good* and *evil* and *natural* good and evil. Everyone who speaks of *moral good* acknowledges that it inspires love toward those we perceive to possess it, whereas *natural good* does not. In this matter, people must consult their own hearts <note>original: "Breasts," a common 18th-century metaphor for the inner seat of emotion and conscience</note>. How differently are we affected toward those we believe possess *honesty, faith, generosity, and kindness*—even when we expect no personal benefit from these admired qualities—compared to those who possess *natural goods*, such as *houses, lands, gardens, vineyards, health, strength, or wisdom* <note>original: "Sagacity"</note>? We shall find that we necessarily love and approve of those who have the former qualities; but the possession of the latter inspires no love at all toward the owner, and instead often causes the opposite feelings of *envy* and *hatred*. 

In the same manner, whatever quality we perceive to be *morally evil*—such as *treachery, cruelty, and ingratitude*—raises our hatred toward the person in whom we see it, even when those qualities do not harm us personally. In contrast, we sincerely love, respect, and pity many who are exposed to *natural evils*, such as *pain, poverty, hunger, sickness, and death.*

<sig>H 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Now</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson distinguishes between moral qualities (virtues like honesty) and natural qualities (assets like wealth or health), arguing that only moral qualities inspire love and approval in an observer.</summary>
<keywords>moral good, natural good, virtue, vice, ethics, sentiment, moral sense, honesty, envy, pity</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal inquiry into the origins of our moral ideas by first defining the terminology of "natural good" and "interest" to distinguish them from "moral" concepts.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

Now the first question on this subject is: "From where do these different ideas of actions arise?"

Because we shall afterwards frequently use the words *interest, advantage,* and *natural good*, it is necessary here to fix their meanings <note>original: "Ideas"</note>. The *pleasure* in our sensory perceptions of any kind gives us our first idea of *natural good* or *happiness*; and then all objects which are likely to excite this *pleasure* are called *immediately good*. Those objects which may procure others that are immediately *pleasant* are called *advantageous*: and we pursue both kinds from a view of *interest*, or from *self-love*.

Our *sense of pleasure* exists prior to <note>original: "is antecedent to"</note> *advantage* and *interest* and is the foundation of them. We do not perceive pleasure in objects because it is our *interest* to do so; instead, objects or actions are *advantageous*, and are pursued or undertaken out of *interest*, because we receive pleasure from them. Our perception of pleasure is necessary <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "necessary" often means "involuntary" or "automatic." Hutcheson is arguing that we do not choose what feels good; our nature determines it for us.</note>, and nothing is *advantageous* or *naturally good* to us except what is likely to raise *pleasure* indirectly or directly <note>original: "mediately, or immediately"</note>. Furthermore, those objects which we know—either from the experience of our senses or from reason—to be directly or indirectly *advantageous*, or likely to provide *pleasure*, we pursue from 

<sig>H 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: from</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson defines "natural good" as pleasure and "advantage" as the means to achieve pleasure, arguing that our self-interest is rooted in these involuntary sensations.</summary>
<keywords>natural good, pleasure, happiness, advantage, interest, self-love, perception, reason, ethics, sensory experience</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the motives of human action, specifically contrasting "natural good" (personal pleasure or advantage) with the theories of recent moralists who claim all morality is rooted in self-interest and law.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

...from *self-interest*, when our *intention* is only to enjoy this *pleasure*, which these objects have the *power* of exciting. Thus *food* <note>original: "Meats"</note>, *drink, harmony, fine views* <note>original: "Prospects"</note>, *paintings*, and *statues* are perceived by our *senses* to be *immediately good*. Our *reason* shows *riches* and *power* to be *indirectly so* <note>original: "mediately so"</note>—that is, likely to furnish us with *objects* of *immediate pleasure*. Both *kinds* of these *natural goods* are pursued from *interest* or *self-love*.

Now, the majority of our recent *moralists* <note>Hutcheson is likely referring to "egoist" philosophers like Thomas Hobbes or legalists like Samuel von Pufendorf, who argued that morality is essentially about following rules to gain rewards or avoid punishment.</note> establish it as undeniable "that all *moral qualities* necessarily have some *relation* to the *law* of a *superior* who possesses sufficient *power* to make us *happy* or *miserable*." Since all *laws* operate only by the *sanctions* of *rewards* or *punishments*—which drive us toward obedience through *motives* of *self-interest*—these thinkers suppose "that this is how *laws* make some actions *indirectly good* <note>original: "mediately Good"</note> or *advantageous*, and others *disadvantageous* in the same way."

They say indeed, "that a benevolent *legislator* establishes no actions as *advantageous* to the person acting <note>original: "the Agent"</note> by law, except those which in their own nature tend toward the *natural good* of the *whole* <note>meaning the common good of society</note>, or at least are not inconsistent with it. Therefore, we approve of the *virtue* of others because it has some small tendency toward our own *happiness*, either..."

<summary>Hutcheson explains that while physical pleasures and wealth are pursued out of self-interest, many modern philosophers wrongly argue that even morality is merely a calculation of self-interest based on the rewards and punishments of a powerful legislator.</summary>

> "...from its own *nature*, or from this general *consideration*: that *obedience* to a benevolent *legislator* is, in general, *advantageous* to the *whole* <note>original: "the Whole"; meaning the common good or society at large</note> and to us in particular. They argue that for these reasons alone, we disapprove of the *vices* of others—meaning the prohibited *action*—because it tends toward our own particular *detriment* to some degree." But then they maintain "that we are determined to *obedience* toward *laws*, or deterred from *disobedience*, merely by *motives* of *self-interest*: to obtain either the *natural good* arising from the commanded *action*, or the *rewards* promised by the *legal sanction* <note>original: "Sanction"; the penalty or reward that gives a law its force</note>; or to avoid the natural evil *consequences* of *disobedience*, or at least the *penalties* of the *law*."

**Some** other moralists <note>This refers to the "Moral Sense" school of thought, of which Hutcheson was a primary proponent, arguing against the "Egoist" view that all human action is based on self-interest.</note> suppose "an *immediate natural good* in the *actions* called *virtuous*; that is, that we are determined to perceive some *beauty* in the *actions* of others, and to love the *agent* <note>the person performing the action</note>, even without reflecting upon any *advantage* which could in any way *accrue* <note>original: "redound"</note> to us from the *action*. They also claim that we have a secret sense of *pleasure* accompanying those of our own *actions* that we call *virtuous*, even when we expect no other *advantage* from them." But they allege at the same time, "that we are motivated <note>original: "excited"</note> to perform these *actions* even..."

> “...just as we pursue or purchase *paintings*, *statues*, and *landscapes* out of *self-interest*, in order to obtain the *pleasure* which accompanies the very *action* of viewing them, and which we necessarily enjoy in doing so.”

The purpose of the following sections is to inquire into this matter; and perhaps the reasons to be offered may prove:

> “I. THAT some actions have an *immediate goodness* to human beings; or that by a *superior sense*, which I call a *moral sense*, we perceive pleasure in the contemplation of such *actions* in others, and are determined to love the agent <note>original: "Agent"; the person performing the action</note> (and we perceive much more pleasure in being conscious of having performed such actions ourselves), without any view of further *natural advantage* <note>original: "natural Advantage"; personal benefit such as wealth, safety, or reputation</note> from them.”

II. IT may perhaps also appear, “That what excites us to these actions which we call *virtuous* is not an intention to obtain even this *sensible pleasure* <note>pleasure felt through the senses or emotions</note>; much less is it motivated by future *rewards* from the *sanctions* <note>original: "Sanctions"; the rewards or punishments established by a law to ensure obedience</note> of laws, or any other *natural good* which may be the consequence of the *virtuous action*. Instead, it arises from an entirely different principle of action, distinct from *interest* or *self-love*.”

<summary>The author outlines his primary thesis: that human beings possess an innate "moral sense" that finds immediate pleasure in virtue, independent of self-interest or external rewards.</summary>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. 107 Sect. 1.</header>

## SECTION I.

### *Of the* Moral Sense *by which we perceive* Virtue *and* Vice, *and approve or disapprove them in others.*

I. THAT our perceptions of **moral good and evil** are entirely different from our perceptions of **natural good** (or **personal advantage**), everyone must convince themselves by reflecting on the different ways they are affected when encountering these different things. If we had no **sense** of "good" other than the **advantage** or **interest** that comes from our external senses, or from our perceptions of **beauty** and **harmony**, our admiration and love for a **fruitful field** or a **comfortable home** <note>original: "commodious Habitation"</note> would be much the same as the feelings we have for a **generous friend** or any **noble character**. This would be the case because both are, or can be, beneficial to us. Furthermore, we would no more admire an action or love a person in a distant country or a past age—whose influence could never reach us—than we love the **mountains of Peru** when we have no interest in the **Spanish trade** <note>The "Spanish Trade" refers to the 18th-century commerce involving silver and goods from South American colonies like Peru.</note>. We would have the same feelings and affections for **inanimate objects** <note>original: "inanimate Beings"</note> as we have for **rational agents** <note>original: "rational Agents"; people capable of reason and moral choice</note>; yet every—

one <note>This "one" is a catchword, indicating the first word of the next page.</note>

<summary>The author argues that moral approval is distinct from personal interest; if they were the same, we would feel the same affection for a useful object as we do for a virtuous person.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, virtue vs. vice, natural good, personal advantage, rational agents, benevolence, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that moral approval is distinct from the appreciation of mere utility or personal advantage. The previous page established that we do not value a "generous friend" in the same way we value a "fruitful field," even if both provide us with benefits.</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

Section 1. one knows to be false. When we compare the two, we say, “Why should we admire or love *inanimate beings* with esteem? They have no intention of doing *good* to us; their nature simply makes them suitable for our *uses*, which they neither know about nor strive to serve. But it is not so with *rational agents* <note>original: "rational Agents"; people capable of reason and moral choice</note>: they study our *interest*, delight in our *happiness*, and are *benevolent* toward us.” We are all, therefore, conscious of the difference between that esteem—or perception of *moral excellence*—which *benevolence* inspires toward the person in whom we see it, and that opinion of *natural goodness* <note>original: "natural Goodness"; here meaning the inherent utility or quality of an object</note>, which only creates a desire to possess the good object. 

Now, “what would cause this difference, if all *approval* <note>original: "Approbation"</note>, or *sense of good*, came only from the *prospect of personal advantage*? Do not *inanimate objects* promote our *advantage* just as much as *benevolent persons* who perform acts of *kindness* and *friendship*? Should we not then have the same affectionate feelings for both? Or should we have only the same cold opinion of *advantage* in both cases?” The reason why it is not so must be this: “That we have a distinct perception of *beauty* or *excellence* in the kind affections of *rational agents*; because of this, we are determined to admire and love such *characters* and *persons*.”

<meta>catchword: SUP.</meta>

<summary>The author explains that humans distinguish between "moral excellence" in people and "natural goodness" in objects because people act with benevolent intentions. He concludes that our moral approval stems from a specific sense of beauty we perceive in the kind actions of others, rather than just the personal benefit we receive from them.</summary>
<keywords>moral excellence, natural goodness, rational agents, benevolence, personal advantage, moral sense, utility vs virtue</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of moral approval, specifically arguing that our perception of virtue is a distinct "sense" that operates differently from our perception of personal profit or physical harm.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

### Sect. I.
---

Imagine we receive the same *benefit* <note>original: "Advantage"</note> from two different men. One serves us because he takes *delight* in our happiness and feels *love* toward us; the other serves us out of *self-interest* or because he is *forced* <note>original: "Constraint"</note> to do so. In this case, both are equally beneficial or *advantageous* to us, yet we will have completely different feelings <note>original: "Sentiments"</note> about them. Therefore, we must certainly have perceptions of *moral actions* that are distinct from those of mere *advantage*. This power of receiving these perceptions may be called a **MORAL SENSE**, since it fits the definition: *a tendency of the mind to receive an idea from the presence of an object that occurs to us, regardless of our own will.*

This will perhaps be just as obvious when we consider our ideas of *evil* done to us intentionally by a *rational agent* <note>original: "rational Agent"; a person capable of reason and moral choice</note>. If we only had senses for *natural good* and *evil* <note>original: "natural Good and Evil"; physical benefits or harms—like health, wealth, or property damage—rather than moral ones</note>, we would react to an *assault*, a *blow* <note>original: "Buffet"</note>, or an *insult* from a *neighbor*—or being *cheated* by a *partner* or *trustee*—with the same serenity and composure as we would react to equal damage caused by a falling *beam*, a *roof tile*, or a *storm* <note>original: "Tempest"</note>. We would have the exact same feelings and attitudes toward both. *Villainy*, *treachery*, and *cruelty* would be as mildly received <note>original: "meekly resented"; here, "resented" refers to the emotional reaction or "feeling" one has in response to something</note> as a *blight* <note>original: "Blast"</note>, *mildew*, or an *overflowing stream*. But I imagine everyone is affected very differently on these occasions, though

<meta>catchword: there</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that humans possess a "moral sense" because we react differently to intentional kindness than to selfish help, and to human cruelty than to natural disasters. This suggests we perceive a moral quality in actions that is distinct from their physical benefit or harm.</summary>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

### Sect. I.
---

There may be equal *physical harm* <note>original: "natural Evil"</note> in both cases. In fact, actions that are not at all harmful to us may cause the strongest anger and indignation if they only show weak <note>original: "impotent"</note> hatred or contempt. On the other hand, when we consider *moral concepts* <note>original: "moral Ideas"</note>, we might not hate the person <note>original: "Agent"</note> who causes us the greatest *physical harm*, nor will we judge their action as morally wrong. For example, believing that a legal sentence is *just* <note>original: "Justice"</note> will prevent us from seeing any *moral wrongdoing* <note>original: "moral Evil"</note> in its execution. We will not feel hatred toward the *judge* <note>original: "Magistrate"</note>, even though he is the direct cause of our greatest sufferings.

### II.
---

In our feelings <note>original: "Sentiments"</note> regarding actions done toward ourselves, there is indeed a mixture of ideas about *personal benefit* <note>original: "natural Good"</note>, which requires some focus to separate. But when we reflect on actions that do not affect us, but rather affect other people, we can observe *moral concepts* clearly, unmixed with ideas of *personal benefit* or *physical harm*. 

For let it be noted here that *those senses* by which we experience pleasure from physical objects—which is why we consider them *beneficial* <note>original: "Advantageous"</note>—could never create in us any desire for the *public good*. They only create a desire for what is good for us personally. Nor could those senses ever make us pleased with an action simply because it promotes the *happiness* of others. And yet, as soon as any action is presented to us as arising from

When we observe *love, humanity, gratitude, compassion,* a *concern* <note>original: "Study"</note> for the good of others, and a *delight* in their happiness—even if it were in the most distant part of the world or in some past age—we feel joy within ourselves, admire the lovely *action*, and praise its author. Conversely, every *action* represented as flowing from *hatred, delight* in the misery of others, or *ingratitude* raises feelings of abhorrence and aversion.

It is indeed true that the actions we approve in others are generally imagined to lead to the *physical well-being* <note>original: "natural Good"</note> of mankind, or at least some *part* of it. But where does this *secret chain* between each *individual* and the rest of humanity come from? How is my own *self-interest* <note>original: "Intereſt"</note> connected with the most distant *parts* of the human race? And yet, I must admire kind <note>original: "beneficent"</note> *actions* toward them and love the author of those actions. 

Where do this *love, compassion, indignation,* and *hatred* toward even *fictional characters* <note>original: "feign'd Characters"</note> come from—characters in the most distant ages and nations—based on whether they appear *kind, faithful, compassionate,* or have the opposite *dispositions* toward their imaginary contemporaries? And do not the latter qualities provoke our *hatred*, *contempt*, and *abhorrence*? It would be a tragedy for mankind if the *sense of virtue* were as limited as the capacity for such *metaphysics* <note>original: "Metaphysicks"; referring to complex, abstract philosophical reasoning.</note>.

V. This moral sense—whether regarding our own actions or those of *others*—has this in common with our other senses: although our desire for *virtue* may be counterbalanced by self-*interest*, our sentiment or perception of its *beauty* cannot be. This certainly would be the case if the only ground for our approval were views of personal *advantage*. Let us consider this in relation to both our own actions and the actions of *others*.

A greedy <note>original: "COVETOUS"</note> man will dislike any branch of trade, however useful it may be to the public, if there is no gain for himself in it; here is a dislike based on *interest*. Offer him a sufficient reward, and he shall be the first to set about it, feeling full satisfaction in his own conduct. Now, is it the same way with our *sense of moral actions*? Should anyone advise us to wrong a *minor* <note>original: "Minor"; a child or person under the legal age of adulthood, often an heir to an estate.</note> or *orphan*, or to do an ungrateful action toward a *benefactor*, we abhor it at first glance. Even if they assure us that it will be very advantageous to us, or even propose a reward, our *sense* of the action is not altered. It is true that these motives may make us undertake the task, but they have no more influence in making us approve of it than a physician’s advice has in making a disgusting medicine <note>original: "nauseous Potion"</note> pleasant to the taste, even when we force ourselves to take it for the recovery of health. The second thing to be considered is: "Whether our **sense** of **moral good** or **evil** in the actions of others can be outweighed or bribed by views of **self-interest**." I may easily be capable of wishing that another person would perform an action I abhor as **morally evil** if it were very **advantageous** to me. In that case, **self-interest** <note>original: "Interest"</note> might outweigh my desire for **virtue** in another person. But no amount of **self-interest** will make me truly approve of an action as **good** if, without that personal gain, it would have appeared **morally evil** to me. Our **sense** of **moral good** or **evil** cannot be outweighed by **self-interest**. How ridiculous would it be to try to bribe someone with rewards, or to threaten them, into having a good opinion of an action that was contrary to their **moral notions**? We might produce hypocrisy <note>original: "Dissimulation"</note> by such means, and that is all.

<meta>This page continues Hutcheson’s rebuttal of the cynical "self-interest" theory of virtue (often associated with Bernard Mandeville). Hutcheson is arguing that if morality were only about personal advantage, we would not be able to admire the virtues of our enemies or the self-sacrifice of ancient heroes.</meta>

# An **INQUIRY** concerning

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
> "...very useful for the defense of any state; and therefore, by **panegyrics** <note>original: "Panegyricks"; formal public speeches or writings delivered in high praise of someone.</note> and **statues**, they encourage such **dispositions** <note>original: "Tempers"</note> in others as the most **compliant** <note>original: "tractable"</note> and useful."

Here, first, let us consider: if a **traitor**, who would sell his own **country** to us, might not often be as **advantageous** to us as a **hero** who defends us? And yet, we can love the **treason** while hating the **traitor**. At the same time, we can praise a gallant **enemy** who is very **harmful** <note>original: "pernicious"</note> to us. Is there nothing in all this but a judgment of personal **advantage**? 

Again, according to this **theory** <note>original: "Scheme"</note>, what could a **statue** or **panegyric** achieve? Men love **praise**. They will perform the **actions** that they see are **praised**. To men who have no other idea of **good** but **self-interest**, **praise** is merely the **opinion** that a **nation** or **party** has of a **man** as being **useful** to them. **REGULUS** <note>Marcus Atilius Regulus, a Roman general who chose torture and death over breaking his word to his enemies.</note>, **CATO** <note>Cato the Younger, a Roman statesman known for his moral integrity and opposition to corruption.</note>, or **DECIUS** <note>Publius Decius Mus, a Roman consul who sacrificed his life in battle to ensure victory for his army.</note> gained no **advantage** from the actions that benefited their country; therefore, they themselves could not admire those actions, even if the people who reaped the **advantage** might praise such actions. **REGULUS** or **CATO** could not possibly praise or love another hero for a **virtuous action**, because this would not gain them the **advantage** of **honor**. They would have had to look upon their own actions as the harsh conditions by which honor was to be purchased, without finding anything inherently lovely in the actions themselves...

<summary>Hutcheson argues that moral approval cannot be based solely on self-interest, as we often admire the virtue of enemies who harm us and the self-sacrifice of heroes who gained nothing personally from their deeds.</summary>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues Hutcheson’s critique of the "selfishness" theory of morality. He is specifically addressing the argument (popularized by Bernard Mandeville) that virtue is a myth invented by clever politicians to trick citizens into acting against their own interests for the benefit of the state.</meta>

# MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

<margin>Section 1.</margin>
...in the contemplation or reflection of which they could be pleased. Now, how unlike is this to what even the slightest observation would teach a person concerning such characters?

BUT, says he <note>Referring to Bernard Mandeville, author of *The Fable of the Bees*, who argued that "private vices" create "public benefits."</note>, "These wondrously cunning governors made men believe, through their ***statues*** and ***panegyrics*** <note>original: "Panegyricks"; formal public speeches or writings delivered in high praise of someone.</note>, that there was such a thing as ***public spirit***, and that this was in itself ***excellent***. Because of this, men are led to admire it in others and to imitate it in themselves, forgetting the pursuit of their own ***advantage***." 

It seems like such an easy matter to him for someone to stop judging others by what we feel within ourselves! He suggests it is easy for a person who is entirely ***selfish*** to imagine others to be ***public-spirited***! He thinks it is simple for someone who has no idea of ***good*** except for his own ***advantage*** to be led by the persuasion of others into a conception of ***goodness*** in something that is openly detrimental to himself and profitable to others. He even suggests we do this so completely that we do not thoroughly approve of the ***action*** unless we are conscious that it proceeded from a ***disinterested study*** <note>original: "disinterested Study"; an unbiased or altruistic pursuit of a goal without seeking personal gain.</note> of the ***good*** of others! Yet, it seems ***statues*** and ***panegyrics*** can accomplish this! <note>Hutcheson is being sarcastic here, pointing out how unlikely it is that mere statues could trick people into ignoring their own self-interest so fundamentally.</note>

> ->*There is no pit in the olive, and no shell on the nut!* <note>original: "Nil intra est oleam, nil extra est in nuce duri!" From Horace, Epistles, Book 2, Letter 1, line 31. This is a proverbial way of saying an argument is as absurd as saying olives have no pits or nuts have no shells.</note><-

### Section 1.

It is an easy matter for men to assert anything in words, but our own hearts must decide the matter: whether some **moral actions** do not at first sight appear **amiable** <note>original: "amiable"; in this context, it means admirable, lovable, or morally attractive.</note>, even to those who are unconcerned with their influence. It is true that the actions we approve of are useful to mankind, but they are not always useful to the person who approves of them. It would perhaps be useful to the **whole** of society if all men agreed to perform such actions, for then everyone would have his share of the **advantage**. However, this only proves that **reason** and calm **reflection** may recommend those actions to us based on **self-interest**. In contrast, our **moral sense** leads us to admire these actions at first sight, without considering this **interest**. 

Indeed, our **sense** shall operate even where the **advantage** to ourselves does not exist. We can approve of the justice of a legal sentence against ourselves. A condemned traitor may approve of the vigilance of a **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), a Roman statesman and orator famous for uncovering the Catilinarian conspiracy to overthrow the Roman government.</note> in discovering conspiracies, even though it would have been to the traitor's advantage if there had never been any men of such **sagacity** <note>original: "Sagacity"; keen mental discernment and soundness of judgment.</note> in the world. To say that he may still approve of such conduct as tending toward the **public good** is a joke coming from someone whose only idea of "good" is **self-interest**. Such a person has no desire for the public good except where it tends toward his own **advantage**, which it does not do at all in this case.

<summary>Hutcheson argues that our moral sense operates independently of self-interest, noting that we can admire virtuous actions (like Cicero's vigilance) even when they result in our own personal loss or punishment.</summary>

<keywords>moral sense, self-interest, public good, Cicero, sagacity, virtue, altruism, justice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Hutcheson’s argument that morality is an innate sense rather than a calculated byproduct of self-interest. He argues that education and custom can only direct our existing moral feelings, not create them from scratch.</meta>

-># MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. I.<-

VII. If what has been said makes it appear that we have a perception of actions as **admirable** <note>original: "amiable Idea"; in this context, it refers to something that is morally attractive or worthy of love.</note> that is different from seeing them as **advantageous** to ourselves, we may conclude that "this perception of **moral good** is not derived from **custom, education, example,** or **study**." These things provide us with no new ideas. They might help us see an **advantage** to ourselves in actions whose usefulness was not initially apparent, or they might give us opinions about how certain actions lead to our **harm** <note>original: "Detriment"</note> through subtle logical reasoning or hasty prejudice, even when we would not have noticed such things at first glance. However, they could never have made us perceive actions as **admirable** or **hateful** <note>original: "odious"</note> independently of any consideration of our own **advantage**.

VIII. It remains then, "That just as the **Author of Nature** <note>A common Enlightenment-era term for God as the creator of the natural order.</note> has designed us to receive pleasant or disagreeable ideas of objects through our **external senses**, depending on whether they are useful or harmful to our bodies; and to receive the pleasures of **beauty** and **harmony** from **uniform objects** <note>original: "uniform Objects"; objects that possess symmetry, order, or consistent patterns.</note> to encourage us to pursue **knowledge** and to reward us for it—or to serve as evidence of His **goodness**, just as **uniformity** itself proves His **existence**—whether we had a **sense** of

<summary>Hutcheson argues that our sense of moral good is an innate faculty designed by God, similar to our physical senses, and cannot be explained away as a mere product of education or self-interest.</summary>

# AN INQUIRY CONCERNING

<margin>Section I.</margin>
> "...of **beauty** in **uniformity** <note>original: "Uniformity"; refers to symmetry or consistent patterns.</note> or not; likewise, he has given us a **MORAL SENSE** to direct our actions and to give us even **nobler pleasures**. Thus, while we only intend the **good** of others, we unintentionally promote our own greatest **private good**."

We should not imagine that this **moral sense**, any more than our other **senses**, assumes any **innate ideas**, **knowledge**, or **practical propositions** <note>original: "practical Proposition"; a formal rule of behavior that is supposedly born into the human mind rather than learned.</note>. *By this term, we mean only a natural inclination of our minds to perceive actions as either **admirable** <note>original: "amiable"</note> or **disagreeable** when they occur in our observation*, prior to any opinions of **advantage** or **loss** that might result to ourselves from them. It is just as we are pleased by a **regular form** or an **harmonious composition** without having any **knowledge** of **mathematics**, or seeing any **advantage** in that **form** or **composition** other than the immediate **pleasure** it provides.

## SECTION II.

### *Concerning the Immediate Motive to Virtuous Actions.*

The motives of human actions—or their immediate *causes*—would be best understood after considering the *passions* and *affections* <note>original: "Affections"; in 18th-century philosophy, this refers to an internal desire, emotion, or inclination that moves one to act.</note>. However, here we shall only consider the *origins* <note>original: "Springs"</note> of the actions we call *virtuous*, as far as is necessary to settle the general foundation of the *moral sense*.

I. EVERY action that we perceive as either morally good or evil is always supposed to flow from *some affection* toward *rational beings* <note>original: "rational Agents"</note>. Whatever we call *virtue* or *vice* is either one of these *affections* or an *action* resulting from it. Or it may perhaps be enough to make an action (or the failure to act) appear *wicked* <note>original: "vitious"; modern: vicious. This refers to a moral defect or flaw.</note> if it demonstrates the lack of such affection toward rational beings as we expect in characters considered *morally good*. All actions considered *religious* in any country are supposed, by those who consider them religious, to flow from some affections toward the GOD <note>original: "DEITY"</note>. Furthermore, whatever we call *social virtue*, we still suppose to flow from—

When this kind of *courage* is sometimes admired, it is based on a hidden perception <note>original: "secret Apprehension"</note> of a good intention behind its use. *Prudence*, if it were only used to promote *self-interest*, is never considered to be a *virtue*. And *justice*—or observing strict equality—if it has no regard for the *good of humanity*, the preservation of rights, and the securing of peace, is a quality more appropriate for its usual equipment <note>original: "Gestamen"; Latin for something carried, like an ornament or tool.</note>, the *beam and scales* <note>original: "Beam and Scales"; the weighing tool held by the personification of Justice.</note>, than for a rational being <note>original: "Agent"</note>. # MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

<margin>Section 2.</margin> ...are dispositions universally necessary to promote the public good and denote *affections* <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "affections" refers to a person's inner motives, feelings, or emotional dispositions toward others.</note> toward rational agents; otherwise, no *virtue* would appear in them.

II. Now, if it can be shown that none of these *affections* which we call *virtuous* spring from *self-love* or a *desire* for *private interest*—since all *virtue* consists of either such *affections* or the *actions* that follow from them—it must necessarily follow that “*virtue* is not pursued out of the *interest* or *self-love* of the *pursuer*, or from any *motives* of his own *advantage*.”

The *affections* that are of the greatest importance in *morals* are **LOVE** and **HATRED**. All the others seem to be merely different *modifications* of these two *original affections*. Now, in discussing *love* toward rational agents <note>That is, human beings or any beings capable of reason and moral choice.</note>, we do not need to be warned to exclude the *love* between the sexes; when no other *affections* accompany it, this is only a *desire* for *pleasure* and is never considered a *virtue*. *Love* toward rational agents is subdivided into the *love of complacence* <note>original: "Complacence"; here meaning a feeling of pleasure or satisfaction in the excellence of someone else's character.</note> or *esteem*, and the *love of benevolence* <note>The well-wishing desire for the happiness or good of another person.</note>. And *hatred* is subdivided into the *hatred of displicence* <note>original: "Displicence"; an archaic term for dislike, dissatisfaction, or a feeling of being displeased by someone's character.</note> or *contempt*, and the *hatred of malice*. Concerning each of these separately, we shall consider whe[ther]...
