**LOVE** of *complacence* <note>original: "Complacence"; in this context, it refers to a feeling of pleasure, satisfaction, or approval toward someone's character.</note>, *esteem*, or *good-liking* appears at first glance to be *disinterested* <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "disinterested" means unbiased or not motivated by personal gain.</note>, as does the *hatred* of *displicence* <note>original: "Displicence"; an archaic term for displeasure, dislike, or distaste.</note> or *dislike*. Both are entirely sparked by certain *moral qualities*, whether *good* or *evil*, that we perceive to exist in others. The very *structure* of our *nature* compels us to *love* or *hate*, to approve or disapprove of these qualities, according to the *moral sense* * previously explained. 

Offer a man all the rewards in the world, or threaten him with every possible punishment to force him to *love* with *esteem* and *complacence* a third person who is either entirely *unknown* to him, or who is *known* to be *cruel, treacherous,* or *ungrateful*. You might be able to extort external obedience, helpful actions, or a faked display of love; but no price can purchase *genuine love* based on *esteem*. The same is true for the *hatred* of *contempt*, which no motive of *personal advantage* can prevent. On the contrary, if you describe a *character* as *generous, kind, faithful,* and *humane*—even if they live in the most distant parts of the world—we cannot help but *love* that person with *esteem* and *complacence*. A *bribe* might possibly make us try to ruin such a man, or some strong motive of *private advantage* might drive us to

Indeed, when we examine our own hearts, we will find that we can hardly ever persuade ourselves to attempt any harm against such people out of a motive of *personal gain*, nor can we carry it out without the strongest reluctance and remorse—at least until we have blinded ourselves into forming a negative opinion of that person’s *moral character*.

III. As for the *love* of *benevolence* <note>original: "Benevolence"; the desire to do good for others or an act of kindness.</note>, the very name excludes *self-interest*. We never call a man *benevolent* if he is useful to others but only intends to serve his *own interest*, without any desire for or delight in the *well-being* of *others*. If there is any *benevolence* at all, it must be *disinterested* <note>original: "disinterested"; in 18th-century philosophy, this means acting without seeking personal profit or biased advantage.</note>; for even the most useful action imaginable loses all appearance of *benevolence* as soon as we realize that it flowed only from *self-love* or *private interest*. For example, no human actions were ever more *beneficial* than the inventions of *fire* and *iron*; but if these were accidental, or if the inventor only intended his *own profit* from them, there is nothing in them that can be called *benevolent*. Wherever *benevolence* is assumed to exist, it is imagined to be *disinterested* and intended for the *good* of *others*.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how benevolence and self-love interact to determine the moral value of an action, using a mathematical analogy to calculate the "purity" of a person's motives.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>*An* INQUIRY *concerning*</header>

<margin>Section 2.
~~~</margin>
lence <note>The conclusion of "benevolence" from the previous page.</note>, these two principles may jointly motivate a man to perform the same action. In such cases, they are to be considered like two physical forces pushing the same object into motion. Sometimes they work together, sometimes they are unrelated to each other, and sometimes they are somewhat opposed.

For example, if a man has such strong **benevolence** that he would have performed an action without any consideration of **self-interest**, the fact that he also expects **private advantage** alongside **public good** as a result of his action does not reduce the **benevolence** of that action in any way. 

However, if he would not have produced as much **public good** without the prospect of **self-interest**, then the effect of **self-love** must be subtracted. His **benevolence** is then proportional to the remaining amount of **good** that pure **benevolence** alone would have produced. 

When a man’s **benevolence** causes him personal harm, then **self-love** is opposed to **benevolence**. In this situation, the **benevolence** is proportional to the total **good** produced plus the resistance of **self-love** that he had to overcome <note>original: "surmounted"</note> to achieve it. 

In most cases, it is impossible for people to know exactly how much others are influenced by one principle or the other. Yet, the general truth is certain enough: this is the method by which the **benevolence** of actions is to be calculated <note>original: "computed"; the author is treating morality as a form of "moral arithmetic."</note>.

<summary>Hutcheson explains how to calculate the moral worth of an action by weighing benevolence against self-interest, arguing that true virtue is measured by how much good a person would do even if it cost them personally.</summary>

> "If anyone asks, 'Where does this **Love of Esteem**, or **Benevolence** toward good men, or toward mankind in general, come from, if not from some subtle <note>original: "nice"</note> views of **Self-Interest**? Or, how can we be moved to desire the happiness of others without any view to our own?' It may be answered, 'The same cause that determines us to pursue happiness for ourselves also determines us toward both **Esteem** and **Benevolence** on the proper occasions; namely, the very frame of our nature, or a generous instinct, which shall be explained later.'"

IV. HERE we may observe that just as love of esteem and satisfaction <note>original: "complacence"; here meaning a sense of pleasure or approval</note> is always joined with **benevolence** where there is no strong opposition of **interest**, so **benevolence** seems to presuppose some small degree of **esteem**. This is not necessarily an esteem of **actual** good qualities, for there may be strong **benevolence** even where there is a **hatred** of **contempt** for **actual vices**. For example, a parent may have great benevolence toward a most abandoned child whose behavior <note>original: "manners"</note> he hates with the greatest displeasure <note>original: "displicence"</note>. However, benevolence presupposes a being who is at least *capable* of **virtue**. We judge other rational agents by ourselves. Human nature is a lovely form; we are all conscious of some morally good qualities and inclinations in ourselves, however partial and imperfect they may be; we

A sudden passion may give us a false view of our fellow creatures, representing them as **absolutely evil** for a short time; during this state of imagination, we might perhaps show evidence of **disinterested malice**. But as soon as we reflect upon **human nature** and form accurate conceptions, this **unnatural** passion is calmed <note>original: "allay’d"</note>, and only **self-love** remains. <meta>This page continues Hutcheson’s argument that human hatred is not naturally "disinterested" but is usually a result of conflicting interests. He moves on to discuss whether other virtuous emotions, like reverence, are rooted in self-interest or a genuine appreciation of moral goodness.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>Section 2.</margin> 
<image-desc>A decorative horizontal flourish or swirl ornament is printed in the margin below the section label.</image-desc>
This clearly shows that we rarely ever harbor any *settled malice* <note>original: "sedate Malice"; meaning a calm, persistent ill-will rather than a fleeting burst of anger.</note> against anyone, or take delight in their misery. Our *hatred* arises only from a conflict of *interest*. If we were to feel *settled malice*, it would have to be directed toward a character we perceived as necessarily and unalterably *evil* in a *moral sense*—which is how a sudden fit of passion sometimes makes our enemies appear to us. Yet, perhaps no such *being* exists among the works of a good GOD <note>original: "DEITY"</note>.

V. Having now presented arguments that may prove our *love*—whether based on *esteem* or *benevolence*—is not founded on *self-love* or calculations of *interest*, let us see "if some *other emotions* <note>original: "Affections"</note> in which *virtue* may be found arise from *self-love*," such as *fear* or *reverence* resulting from our perception of *goodness*, *power*, and *justice*. 

For no one perceives any *virtue* in a base *dread* and *servitude* toward a powerful *evil being*; that is, in fact, the most pathetic form of *selfishness*. Now, the same arguments that prove the *love of esteem* is disinterested will also prove that this honorable *reverence* is disinterested as well. This is because reverence clearly arises from perceiving lovable *qualities* in a person and feeling *love* toward them, which creates an *abhorrence* <note>original: "Abhorrence"; a feeling of strong loathing or repungance.</note> of offending them. If we could feel reverence for a *being* simply because it was in our *interest* to do so, then a third party could bribe us into feeling reverence toward a *being* that was neither *good* nor

VIII. THE last and only remaining objection to what has been said is this:
> "Perhaps *virtue* is pursued only because
> of the *pleasure that accompanies it* <note>original: "concomitant Pleasure"</note>."
To this we may answer, first, by observing that this argument clearly assumes a *sense of virtue* exists *before* <note>original: "antecedent to"</note> any ideas of personal advantage. It is upon this prior sense that the advantage itself is built. From the very *structure* <note>original: "Frame"</note> of our *nature*, we are predisposed to find *pleasure* in practicing *virtue* and to approve of it when it is practiced by ourselves or others. But furthermore, might we not rightly question whether every *virtuous act* is pleasant? Or whether we are not driven toward certain admirable *actions* in which we find no pleasure at all? It is true that all passions and emotions <note>original: "Affections"</note> justify themselves; in other words, we approve of being affected in a certain way on a specific occasion, and we condemn a person who reacts differently. # MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

<margin>Section 2.</margin>

Those who are jealous or compassionate think it reasonable that they should feel these emotions upon the various occasions that trigger these passions. However, we should not therefore say that *sorrow*, *anger*, *jealousy*, and *pity* are pleasant, or that we choose to be in these states of passion because of the pleasure that accompanies them. The matter is plainly this: the *structure* <note>original: "Frame"</note> of our *nature* determines that we will be affected in these ways on such occasions, and that we will approve of our being so. In fact, we dislike any person who is not affected this way in such situations, despite the discomfort <note>original: "uneasiness"</note> of these passions. 

Furthermore, this discomfort drives us to try to change the condition of the person or thing <note>original: "Object"</note> causing the feeling; but we do not otherwise try to remove the painful emotion while the cause remains unchanged. This shows that these passions do not arise from *self-love* <note>original: "Self-Love"; meaning acting purely out of self-interest</note>. The actions that these passions motivate us to take generally do result in removing the uncomfortable emotion by changing the state of the person in distress. However, removing our own pain is not our direct intention. If it were, we would simply run away, close our eyes, or distract ourselves from the miserable person to avoid the pain of compassion—which we rarely do. On the contrary, we crowd around such scenes and voluntarily expose ourselves to pain, unless reason and the realization that we cannot help the person in misery countermands our inclination, or if some selfish emotion—such as fear for our own safety—outweighs it.

<summary>The author argues that compassionate and painful "passions" like pity are not driven by self-interest, as we do not seek to avoid the pain they cause but rather seek to help the person in distress.</summary>
<keywords>moral philosophy, compassion, self-love, passions, pity, human nature, ethics, benevolence</keywords>

# AN INQUIRY CONCERNING MORAL GOOD AND EVIL.

<margin>
Section 2.
<image-desc>A decorative horizontal flourish with loops.</image-desc>
</margin>

Now there are several *morally attractive* <note>original: "amiable"; here meaning praiseworthy or inspiring of love</note> actions which flow from these passions that are so uncomfortable; such as attempts to relieve the distressed, to defend the injured, or to *make amends* for wrongs done by ourselves. These actions are often accompanied by no pleasure at the time, nor do they result in any subsequent pleasure, except when they are successful—unless it is the pleasure that may arise from *calm reflection* after the passion has passed, when we consider that we have been in a state of mind <note>original: "Disposition"</note> which appears *lovely* and *good* to our *moral sense*. However, this pleasure is never the intention in the heat of action, nor is it the motive that excites us to do it.

**Furthermore**, in the *pleasant passions*, we do not *love* because it is *pleasant to love*; we do not *choose* this state because it is an *advantageous* or *pleasant state*. This passion necessarily arises from seeing its proper object: a *morally good character*. If we could love whenever we saw it was in our *interest* <note>original: "Interest"; meaning for our own benefit</note> to love, then *love* could be bribed by a third person. Moreover, we could never love people in distress, because in those cases our love causes us pain. The same observation may be extended to all the other *affections* <note>original: "Affections"; the various emotions or inclinations of the mind</note> from which *virtue* is supposed to flow. From all of this, we may conclude that the *virtuous person* <note>original: "virtuous Agent"</note>

IX. HAVING removed these falsely supposed sources <note>original: "Springs"</note> of those actions which are considered *virtuous*, let us next establish the true one: namely, *a certain inclination of our nature to seek the good of others; or an instinct, occurring before any reasoning about self-interest, which influences us to love others.* This is similar to the *moral sense* (mentioned above*) that leads us to approve of actions flowing from *this love*, whether in ourselves or in others. This *disinterested affection* <note>original: "disinterested Affection"; meaning an unselfish love that does not seek personal gain</note> may seem strange to people who are convinced by the idea that *self-love* is the only motive for action—an idea often heard from the pulpit, in schools, in philosophical systems, and in conversations shaped by them. But let us consider this affection in its strongest and simplest forms; once we see that it is possible in these instances, we may easily discover its *universal extent.*

X. And that this is true in practice will appear by considering some more distant attachments. If we observe any neighbors—from whom perhaps we have received no favors <note>original: "good Offices"</note>—forming *friendships*, *families*, and *partnerships*, and assisting each other with honesty and kindness; I ask any person if they would not be better pleased with their *prosperity* (when their interests are in no way inconsistent with their own) than with their *misery* and *ruin*. You shall find a *bond of benevolence* <term>goodwill and a desire for the well-being of others</term> that extends further than a *family* and *children*, although these wider ties are not as strong.

He will find his mind *moved* in their favor. He will find himself *thinking of* *improvements* and *amendments* to their *constitution*, and *regret* any unfortunate part of it that might be harmful <note>original: "pernicious"</note> to their *interests*. He will *bewail* any *disaster* that befalls them and follow all their fortunes with the affection of a *friend*. Now, this suggests a *benevolence* that is, to some degree, extended to all *mankind*, provided there is no *interfering interest* arising from *self-love* that might obstruct it. And if we had any concept of rational *beings* <note>original: "Agents"</note> capable of moral feelings in the *most distant planets*, our good *wishes* would still be with them, and we would *delight* in their *happiness*.

Our *moral sense* leads us to approve of these *lovely dispositions* where we have most clearly observed them, and our *benevolence* <term>goodwill toward others</term> makes us care about the *interests* of the *people* who possess them. When we come to observe the same things as clearly in *another country*, we begin to develop a *national love* toward it as well. Our *own country* has no other preference in our minds, except perhaps through an *association* <term>a mental connection</term> of the *pleasant ideas* of our *youth* with the *buildings, fields*, and *woods* where we experienced them. But where this does not happen, we shall find all *mankind* under its influence,

### SECTION III.

->*The sense of virtue and the various opinions about it can be reduced to one general foundation. The method for calculating the morality of actions.*<-

I. If we examine all the actions that are considered **virtuous** <note>original: "amiable"; here meaning morally attractive or worthy of love</note> anywhere and inquire into the reasons why they are approved, we shall find that, in the opinion of the person who approves them, they always appear as **BENEVOLENT**, or flowing from a love for others and a concern for their happiness. This is true whether the person approving the action is one of the people loved or benefited <note>original: "profited"</note> by it, or not. Therefore, all those kind feelings <term>affections</term> that incline us to make others happy—and all actions thought to flow from such feelings—appear **morally good**, provided that while they are benevolent toward some people, they are not harmful <note>original: "pernicious"</note> to others. Furthermore, we will not find anything attractive in any action whatsoever where no benevolence is perceived, nor in any personality trait <term>disposition</term> or talent <term>capacity</term> that is not thought to be applicable to and intended for benevolent purposes. Indeed, as was observed earlier*,

---

* See Section 2, Article 3, Paragraph 1; and Article 6, Paragraph 2.

<summary>Hutcheson argues that the foundation of all moral approval is benevolence; an action is only considered "good" if it is perceived to flow from a desire for the happiness of others without causing harm elsewhere.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, benevolence, moral goodness, social happiness, moral approval, affections, ethics</keywords>

The actions which in fact are exceedingly useful shall appear void of **moral beauty** if we know that they proceeded from no kind intentions toward others. And yet, an unsuccessful attempt at kindness, or at promoting the **public good**, shall appear as attractive as the most successful one, if it flowed from an equally **strong benevolence**.

II. Because of this, **those** feelings <term>affections</term> which would lead us to do good to our benefactor shall appear **attractive** <note>original: "amiable"</note>, and the contrary feelings **repulsive** <note>original: "odious"</note>, even when our actions cannot possibly be of any advantage or hurt to him. Thus, a **sincere love** and gratitude toward our benefactor, a **cheerful readiness** to do whatever he shall require—no matter how burdensome—a **hearty inclination** to comply with his intentions, and **contentment** with the state he has placed us in, are the strongest evidences of **benevolence** we can show to such a person; and therefore they must appear exceedingly attractive. Under these feelings is included all the **reasoned worship** <note>original: "rational Devotion"</note>, or **religion**, toward a DEITY understood as **good**, which we can possibly perform.

We may here briefly observe one circumstance in the **structure** <note>original: "frame"</note> of our nature which is wonderfully designed to promote benevolence: namely, that just as a benefit granted necessarily raises gratitude in the beneficiary <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the next page likely continues the description of how gratitude functions within human nature.</note>

In our recent debates about *passive obedience* <note>The political doctrine that subjects must never resist their monarch, even if the monarch is a tyrant</note>, and the right of *resistance* in defense of *privileges* <note>In this context, legal rights and civil liberties</note>, the point disputed among sensible people was whether *universal submission* would probably result in *greater natural evils* than *temporary insurrections* would when *privileges* are invaded. But let us leave the disputes of the *scholars*, who might be said to be powerfully influenced by *custom* and *education*; instead, let us consider on what grounds actions are *approved* or *condemned*, *justified* or *excused* in common life. We are universally ashamed to say an action is *just* simply because it tended toward my own *advantage*, or toward the *advantage* of the *person acting*. Likewise, we rarely condemn a *beneficent, kind action* because it was not advantageous to us or to the *person acting*. *Blame* and *criticism* are founded on an action's tendency toward *public evil*, or a principle of *private malice* in the *person acting* <note>original: "Agent"</note>, or at least a neglect of the good of others. They are based on an *inhumane* temperament, or at least such *strong selfishness* that it makes the *person acting* indifferent to the sufferings of others; thus, we *blame* and *criticize* even when the action does not affect us in any way. All the moving and persuasive justifications of actions—which might appear evil because of some *partial* evil tendency—are based on the idea that they were necessary for some greater good that outweighed the [evil...]

All these considerations show what is the *universal foundation of our sense of moral good or evil*: namely, *benevolence* toward others on one hand, and *malice*—or even *laziness* <note>original: "Indolence"; here meaning a lack of concern or effort for the welfare of others</note> and *indifference* about *obvious public harm*—on the other. And let it be noted here that we are so far from imagining that all people are motivated only by *self-interest* <note>original: "Self-Love"</note> that we universally expect in others a regard for the *public good*. We do not look upon the lack of this regard as merely the absence of *moral good* or *virtue*, but even as something *positively evil and hateful*.

# MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

<margin>Section 3.</margin>
Every action appears *evil* if it is imagined to result from *any* degree of *this motive* <note>original: "this Affection"; referring here to a malicious or ill-willed disposition</note>. Perhaps a *violent passion* might drive men into such behavior for a few moments, and our *rash, angry feelings* toward our *enemies* may make us believe they have such *hateful character traits*. However, based on the reasons provided earlier\*, it is very likely that *human nature* does not contain such a level of wickedness that one would, in cold blood, be pleased by the *misery* of others when that misery is not seen as useful to *one's own interests*.

The story of Nero and Paetus <note>Publius Clodius Thrasea Paetus was a Roman Senator known for his integrity and opposition to the tyranny of Emperor Nero, who eventually forced him to commit suicide.</note> might be cited as evidence against this claim, but perhaps unfairly, even if we assume the story is true. Nero was aware that he was hated by those the world called *good men* and that they posed a danger to him. He imagined his best security lay in being terrifying and appearing so at all times by making others miserable whenever he wished. He wanted his enemies to see that they would find no safety in the kind of compassion that a person like Nero would consider a sign of weakness. This unfortunate gentleman's happiness might have been described by some foolish courtier in a way that seemed like a criticism of the tyrant’s unnatural behavior. This could have provoked Nero’s anger to the point of killing a person who was admired and respected more than himself. Any

The temperament of a *tyrant* likely consists of a continuous state of *anger*, *hatred*, and *fear*. To form our judgment, then, of his motives for action—and those of men with similar temperaments in lower social positions—let us reflect upon the perceptions we form of humanity when we are under any of those passions which, to the *tyrant*, are *habitual*. When we are under the fresh impressions of an injury, we clearly find that our minds are completely filled with negative views of the person who injured us, as if he were *absolutely evil* and *delighted* in doing mischief. We overlook the virtues which we could have observed in him when we were calm; we forget that it is perhaps only *self-interest* <note>original: "Self-Love"</note>, and not *malice*, which was his motive, or perhaps even some generous or kind intention toward others. These are likely the opinions a *tyrant* constantly forms concerning humanity; and having greatly weakened all *kind feelings* <note>original: "kind Affections"</note> in himself—however much he may pretend to have them—he judges the temperaments of others by his own. And if *humanity* were really as he perceives them to be...

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that a tyrant’s cruelty stems from a distorted view of human nature. If people were as malicious as a tyrant believes, then his harsh treatment of them would be more understandable.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

<margin>Section 3.</margin>
...his treatment of them would not be very unreasonable. We generally find that our passions arise in accordance with the perceptions we form of others. If these perceptions are formed rashly, based on sudden or superficial observations, it is no surprise if our subsequent attitudes are poorly suited to the *actual state of human nature*.

The usual sources of *vice* among people must, therefore, be understood as either a *misguided self-love* <note>original: "Self-Love"</note> that becomes so intense it overcomes *benevolence*, or *emotions* <note>original: "Affections"</note> arising from *false and rashly formed opinions* of *humanity*, which we fall into because our *benevolence* is weak. When people who previously thought well of each other find themselves with *conflicting interests*, they tend to lose that *good opinion* by imagining that the opposition they face is driven by *deliberate malice*. Without this belief in malicious intent, they can hardly *hate* one another. 

For example, two candidates for the same office might wish each other *dead* because that is a common way people vacate positions for one another. However, if they still respect each other’s virtue—as sometimes happens among those with kind temperaments—their rivalry can exist without *hatred*. If a better position with no competition were given to one of them, the other would actually rejoice for them.

<summary>This page argues that human vice and hatred are not innate but typically arise from a misguided self-interest or a mistaken belief that others are acting out of malice. It illustrates this by showing how even rivals can avoid hatred if they maintain a belief in each other's underlying virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of human actions, specifically how self-interest relates to the greater good. It transitions from discussing how we perceive others' motives to how our own self-preservation fits into a moral framework.</meta>

<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>
Section 3.

V. Actions that arise solely from **self-interest** <note>original: "Self-Love"</note>, yet show no lack of **kindness** <note>original: "Benevolence"</note> and have no harmful effects on others, seem **perfectly** indifferent in a moral **sense**. They provoke neither the **love** nor the **hatred** of an observer. Our reason can indeed identify certain boundaries within which we may act out of self-interest in a way that is consistent with the **greater good** <note>original: "Good of the Whole"</note>. In fact, it is absolutely necessary for every human being to act for their own **benefit** within these boundaries for the sake of the **greater good**; a total lack of such **self-interest** would be **universally** harmful. 

Therefore, a person who pursues their own **private good** with the intention of aligning with that natural order which supports the **greater good**—and even more so, one who promotes their **own good** with the direct goal of making themselves more capable of serving God or doing good to **humanity** <note>original: "Mankind"</note>—acts not only innocently, but also honorably and **virtuously**. In both of these cases, a motive of **kindness** joins with **self-interest** to drive the action. 

In this light, a **neglect** of our **own well-being** may be **morally evil**, as it suggests a lack of **kindness** toward the **whole** community. However, when **self-interest** breaks past the boundaries mentioned above and leads us into actions that are detrimental to **others** and to the **whole**, or makes us numb to **generous, kind emotions** <note>original: "generous kind Affections"</note>;

<summary>This page argues that self-interest is not inherently immoral; in fact, taking care of oneself is necessary for the greater good and becomes virtuous when done to better serve God and humanity.</summary>
<keywords>self-love, benevolence, moral sense, greater good, virtue, self-neglect, social duty</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 160, explaining how self-interest becomes vicious when it overrides our natural kindness or leads to a negative view of others. It then transitions to discussing the moral duty of self-preservation as part of a rational system.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

then it appears **vicious** <note>original: "vitious"</note>, and is **disapproved**. 

<margin>
Section 3.
</margin>

Similarly, when due to any minor **injuries** <note>original: "Injurys"</note>, sudden resentment, or any weak, superstitious suggestions, our **kindness** <note>original: "Benevolence"</note> is so faint that we fall into hateful ideas about humanity—or any part of it—without just cause, as if they were **entirely evil**, or **malicious**, or a worse sort of beings than they really are; these ideas must lead us into **ill-willed emotions** <note>original: "malevolent Affections"</note>, or at least weaken our good ones, and make us **truly immoral** <note>original: "really Vitious"</note>.

**VI.** Here we must also observe that every **moral agent** <note>A person capable of making moral judgments and acting on their own initiative.</note> rightly considers himself a **part** of this **rational system** <note>The interconnected community of intelligent beings.</note>, which may be useful to the **whole**; therefore, he may be, in part, an object of his own **kindness** <note>original: "Benevolence"</note>. Furthermore, as was hinted at above, he may see that the **preservation** of the **system** requires every person to be innocently **solicitous** <note>original: "sollicitous"; meaning concerned or attentive to one's own needs.</note> about himself. 

Hence, he may conclude that an action which brings greater **evil** to the **person acting** <note>original: "Agent"</note> than **good** to others—however much it may show strong **kindness** or a **virtuous character** in that person—nonetheless shows the action to be founded upon a mistaken opinion of how that action affects the **public good**, to which it does not actually contribute. Consequently, a wise man who reasoned correctly and considered the **whole** would not be led to do it, no matter how strong his **kindness** might be.

<margin>Section 3.</margin> ...nor would he recommend it to the practice of others. Furthermore, if some *good* were available for a person to pursue, and he had a *competitor* who was equal to him in every way, even the *highest kindness* <note>original: "highest Benevolence"</note> possible would not lead a wise man to prefer the other person over himself—unless there were *ties of gratitude* or some other *outside circumstance* to move him to yield to that *competitor*. Surely, a man of the *strongest kindness* may treat himself just as he would a *third person* who was a *competitor* of *equal merit* with anyone else. Just as choosing one person over another in such a case would not suggest a *weakness of kindness*, neither would he show weakness by choosing himself over a man of only equal *abilities* <note>original: "Abilitys"</note>.

VII. **THE** *moral beauty* or *ugliness* <note>original: "Deformity"</note> of *actions* is not changed by the *moral qualities* of the **people receiving the action** <note>original: "Objects"</note>, except in how those *qualities* increase or decrease the *kindness* <note>original: "Benevolence"</note> of the *action* or the *public good* intended by it. Therefore, *kindness* toward the *worst characters*, or the *effort* <note>original: "Study"</note> to do them *good*, can be as *admirable* <note>original: "amiable"</note> as any other kindness; indeed, it is often more so than kindness toward the *good*, since it demonstrates such a strong degree of *kindness* that it can <meta>catchword: surmount</meta>

In comparing the *moral qualities* of *actions*—in order to guide our **choice** <note>original: "Election"</note> among various proposed *actions*, or to find which of them has the greatest *moral excellence*—we are led by *our moral sense* of *virtue* to judge this way: in equal degrees of *happiness* expected to result from an *action*, the *virtue* is in proportion to the *number* of persons to whom the happiness shall extend. AGAIN, when the *consequences* of *actions* are of a *mixed nature*—partly *advantageous* and partly *harmful* <note>original: "Pernicious"</note>—that *action* is *good* if its *good effects* outweigh <note>original: "preponderate"</note> the *evil* by being useful to *many* and harmful to *few*. The *action* is *evil* when the opposite is true. Here also, the *moral importance* of *characters* or the *social standing* <note>original: "Dignity"</note> of *persons* may compensate for *numbers*, as may the *degrees* of *happiness* or *misery*. For to procure an *insignificant good* for *many*, but an *immense evil* for a *few*, may be *evil*; and an *immense good* for a *few* may outweigh a *small evil* felt by *many*.

BUT by the *consequences* of *actions*, we are to understand not only the immediate *effects* directly produced by them, but also all those *events* which otherwise would not have happened. Thus, many *actions* which have no immediate *evil effects*—indeed, which actually produce *good effects*—may be *evil* if their universal permission <note>original: "Allowance"</note>...

Or, if there is no fixed *standard* so that people may easily know when such actions are allowed and when prohibited; or when such actions, however useful in specific instances, might serve as precedents for the greatest mischief. For example: the *execution* of justice by *private individuals* on offenders (in any state not entirely corrupted from the goals of government); the *violation* of the rights of *worthless persons* in favor of those with great *moral capacity* and great *benevolence*; or the *protection* of the innocent by *false testimony* in a court of justice. Such acts may be very useful in specific instances, but the permission of such actions would lead to the greatest mischief, since everyone would judge for themselves which cases were proper and which were not. Consequently, there could be no certainty regarding *life*, *possessions*, or *evidence*. For this reason, such practices are universally considered *evil*.

X. From the last two observations, we can see which actions our *moral sense* would most recommend for our *choice* <note>original: "Election"</note> as being the most *perfectly virtuous*: namely, those that appear to have the most universal and unlimited tendency toward the *greatest* and *most extensive happiness* of all the *rational beings* <note>original: "Agents"</note> to whom our influence can reach. All *benevolence*, even toward a *part* <note>original: "Part"; referring to a small group or specific individual rather than the whole of humanity</note>, is admirable <note>original: "amiable"</note>.

XI. To find a *universal rule* <note>original: "Canon"</note> to calculate the *morality* of any actions, with all their circumstances—whether we judge the actions done by ourselves or by others—we must observe the following *propositions* or *axioms*:

1. THE moral *importance* <note>original: "Importance," often meaning the weight or impact of the act</note> of any character, or the *quantity* of public *good* produced by them, is found in a *combined ratio* <note>original: "compound Ratio"</note> of their *benevolence* and *abilities*. <meta>This page continues Francis Hutcheson's "moral calculus," a mathematical attempt to quantify human virtue. On the previous page, he defined the basic relationship between Benevolence (B), the Moment or amount of public good produced (M), and the person's Abilities (A). Here, he introduces "Interest" (I)—the person's own self-interest—to show how it increases or decreases the moral value of an action.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

**Section 3.** These selfish *motives* will be explained further later; <note>See Section 5.</note> here we may generally refer to them by the word *interest*. When interest aligns with *benevolence* in any action that can be increased or decreased, it must produce a greater amount of good than *benevolence* alone would given the same *abilities*. 

Therefore, when the *impact* <note>original: "Moment"</note> of one action (intended partly for the good of the *person acting* <note>original: "Agent"</note>) is only equal to the *impact* of another person's action influenced solely by *benevolence*, the first person is *less virtuous*. In their case, the *interest* must be subtracted to find the true effect of the *benevolence* or *virtue*. 

In the same way, when *interest* opposes *benevolence* and yet is overcome by it, this *interest* must be added to the *impact* to increase the *virtue* of the action or the strength of the *benevolence*. Or, expressed this way: 

In virtuous actions that are also personally advantageous: 
$$B = \frac{M - I}{A}$$ 

And in laborious, painful, dangerous, or expensive *virtue*: 
$$B = \frac{M + I}{A}$$ 

By *interest* in this last case, we mean all the *advantage* the *person acting* might have gained by skipping the action, which serves as a *negative motive* for it; and this value, when subtracted, becomes *positive*. <note>In mathematical terms, subtracting a negative number is the same as adding a positive one (M - (-I) = M + I). Hutcheson is saying that if you suffer a loss to do good, that loss is added to your "virtue score."</note>

---

* See Section 5.

<meta>catchword: BUT</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson refines his moral formulas by accounting for self-interest, arguing that personal gain reduces the moral credit of a good deed, while personal sacrifice increases it.</summary>

BUT here we must observe, that no *advantage* that was not intended—even if it happens by chance or naturally flows back to us from the *action*—affects its *morality* at all or makes it less *admirable* <note>original: "amiable"</note>. Likewise, no *difficulty* or *evil* that was unforeseen or not consciously decided upon makes a kind *action* more *virtuous*; in such cases, *self-love* neither assists nor opposes *benevolence*. In fact, *self-interest* only diminishes *benevolence* when the *action* would not have been undertaken, or so much *good* would not have been produced by the *person acting* <note>original: "Agent"</note>, without this prospect of *interest*. Conversely, self-interest lessens the *vice* of an evil *action* when the *action* would not have been pleasing to the *person acting*, nor so much *evil* produced by them, if that *interest* had not been present. <note>Hutcheson argues that doing something bad purely for personal gain is slightly "less evil" than doing something bad out of pure malice or a desire to cause harm.</note>

THE fifth axiom only explains the external signs by which people must judge others, since we cannot see into each other's hearts. It may often happen that people have enough *benevolence* to have overcome any difficulty, and yet they may face no difficulties at all. In that case, it is certain there is just as much *virtue* in the *person acting*, even though he does not give such proof of it to his fellow creatures as he would have if he had overcome obstacles in his kind actions. <summary>Hutcheson clarifies that moral value is determined by intent: unintended benefits do not decrease virtue, and unforeseen obstacles do not increase it. He also notes that while we judge others by their outward actions (Axiom 5), true virtue exists in the heart even when it isn't tested by difficulty.</summary>

SINCE then *benevolence*, or *virtue* in any person acting <note>original: "Agent"</note>, is expressed as M/A, or as (M+I)/A, and no *being* can act beyond his *natural ability*, *the perfection of virtue must be that point where* M=A. This occurs when the *being* acts to the absolute limit of its power for the *public good*. Consequently, the perfection of *virtue* in this case (the ratio of M/A) is equal to one <note>original: "Unity"</note>. 

This may show us the only foundation for the boasting of the *Stoics* <note>Stoics: A school of ancient Greek philosophers who taught that virtue is the highest good and that a wise person should be free from passion and indifferent to pleasure and pain.</note>, who claimed: "That an innocent creature, by pursuing virtue with its utmost power, may equal the gods in virtue." In their view, if [A], or *ability*, is *infinite*, then unless [M], or the *good* to be produced in the whole of existence, is also infinite, the *virtue* is not *absolutely perfect*; thus, the result of the calculation <note>original: "Quotient"</note> can never exceed one.

XII. THESE same axioms may be applied to calculate the *moral evil* in *actions*. In this case, we will name the disposition that leads us to *evil* as "*hatred*," though it is more often merely *self-love* combined with a lack of attention <term>inadvertence</term> to the consequences. Therefore:

1st. **T**HE **Moment of Evil** <note>The "Moment of Evil" ($\mu$) represents the total quantity or weight of harm produced.</note> produced by any *agent*, is the *product* of his *hatred* multiplied by his *ability*, or $\mu = H \times A$. And,

2dly. **I**N equal *abilities*, $\mu = H \times 1$.

3dly. **W**HEN *hatred* is equal, $\mu = A \times 1$: And,

4thly. **T**HE **degree of moral evil**, or the **degree of vice**, which is equal to the *hatred* or *neglect* of the *public good*, is expressed thus: $H = \frac{\mu}{A}$.

5thly. **T**HE **motives of self-interest** may cooperate with *hatred*, or oppose it, in the same way they do with *benevolence*. Therefore, according to whether *self-interest* may partly incite the *action* (and so diminish the *evil* calculated), or dissuade from it (and so increase the *malice* required to overcome the interest), the *malice* which surmounts it is $H = \frac{\mu + I}{A}$, in the same manner as in the case of *moral good*.

**B**UT we must observe, that not only is *innocence* expected from all mortals, but they are presumed by their *nature* to be, in some measure, inclined toward the *public good*; so that a bare absence of this desire is enough to make an *agent* be considered *evil*. Nor is a direct...

<page-num>174</page-num>

# An INQUIRY concerning MORAL GOOD and EVIL

<margin>Section 3.</margin> ...nor is a direct intention of *public evil* necessary to make an action *evil*; it is enough that it flows from *self-love* with a plain neglect for the good of others.

XIII. From our fourth axiom <note>Referencing the previous formulas where moral value is calculated by dividing the total impact by the agent's ability.</note>, we may form an almost demonstrative conclusion that we have a *sense of goodness* and *moral beauty* in actions that is distinct from *advantage* <note>original: "Advantage"; meaning personal gain or utility</note>. If we had no other foundation for approving of actions but the *advantage* that might come to us if they were done for our benefit, we would not take the *abilities* of the *agent* <note>original: "Agent"; the person performing the action</note> into account; instead, we would value actions solely according to their *moment* <note>original: "Moment"; a technical term here meaning the total weight, quantity, or importance of the result produced</note>. We only consider an agent's *abilities* to determine their degree of *benevolence*, which implies that *benevolence* is inherently lovable.

Who was ever better pleased with a *barren, rocky farm* or an *inconvenient house* because they were told that the poor *farm* yielded as much produce as it possibly could, or that the *house* accommodated its owner as well as it could? And yet, in our judgments of actions where the *moment* (the actual impact) is very insignificant, it wonderfully increases the *beauty* of the act to claim, "That it was all the poor *agent* could do for the *public*, or for his *friend*."

<summary>Hutcheson argues that we value the intentions and benevolence of an agent more than the actual results of their actions, proving that our moral sense is based on a love of virtue rather than just personal gain.</summary>

# MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

<margin>Section 3.</margin> ...intentions of the *public good*, according to their power. We form our judgment of them according to what appears to be their *fixed disposition*, and not according to any particular *outbursts* <note>original: "Sallys"</note> of *unkind passions*. Although these outbursts do diminish the *beauty* of *good characters*, just as the *movements* of *kindly affections* may diminish the *deformity* of *bad ones*. 

What then properly constitutes a *virtuous character* is not a few accidental *movements* of *compassion*, *natural affection*, or *gratitude*. Instead, it is a *fixed humanity*, or a *desire* for the *public good* of everyone to whom our *influence* can extend. This desire uniformly excites us to all acts of *beneficence* <note>original: "Beneficence"; the practice of doing good or being charitable</note>, according to our best *prudence* and *knowledge* of the *interests* of others. Furthermore, a *strong benevolence* <note>original: "Benevolence"; a disposition to do good and promote the happiness of others</note> will not fail to make us careful to inform ourselves correctly concerning the truest methods of serving the interests of mankind. Every movement, indeed, of the *kindly affections* appears to some degree *lovable* <note>original: "amiable"</note>; but we name the *character* based on the *prevailing principle*.

XV. I do not know for what reason some will not allow that to be *virtue* which flows from *instincts* or *passions*. But how do they help their argument? They say, "*Virtue arises from reason*." What is *reason* but that *shrewdness* <note>original: "Sagacity"</note> we have in pursuing any *end*? The *ultimate end* proposed...

<summary>This page argues that a virtuous character is defined by a consistent, fixed disposition toward the public good rather than occasional kind acts. It also begins to critique the view that virtue must be derived purely from reason, suggesting reason is merely a tool for achieving ends determined by our instincts.</summary>

<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>Section 3.</margin> ...proposed <note>The word "proposed" begins on the previous page as "pro-".</note> by common *moralists* is the *happiness* of the *agent* <note>The person performing the action.</note> themselves, and he is certainly determined to pursue this by *instinct*. Now, might not another *instinct* toward the *public*, or the *good* of others, be just as proper a principle of *virtue* as the *instinct* toward *private happiness*? And is there not the same occasion for the exercise of our *reason* in pursuing the former as the latter? 

This much is certain: while we view the *selfish* actions of others with *indifference* at best, we see something lovable <note>original: "amiable"</note> in every action that flows from *kind affections* or *passions* toward others, provided they are conducted with prudence so as to actually achieve their goal. Our *passionate* actions, as we showed above <note>See Section 2, Article 8.</note>, are not generally *self-interested*. This is because our intention is not to free ourselves from the *discomfort* <note>original: "Uneasiness"</note> of the passion, but to alter the *condition* <note>original: "State"</note> of the person we are concerned for <note>original: "Object"</note>. 

If it is said, "That actions arising from *instinct* are not so much the effect of prudence and choice," this objection applies just as strongly against actions that flow from *self-love*. And I see no harm in supposing that humans are *naturally* disposed toward *virtue*, rather than being left merely *indifferent* and only moved to action when it appears to serve their own *private good*. Surely, the supposition of a *benevolent universal instinct* would recommend... <note>The sentence continues on the next page: "...recommend itself."</note>

<summary>The author argues that a natural instinct for the public good is as valid a foundation for virtue as the instinct for self-preservation. He refutes the idea that instinctive actions lack moral value, noting that if instinct disqualified an action, then actions motivated by self-interest would also be disqualified.</summary>

<margin>Section 3.</margin>
...commend *human nature*, and its *author* <note>original: "AU-AUTHOR"; Hutcheson refers here to God as the creator of human nature</note>, more to the *love* of a *good man*, and leave room enough for the *exercise* of our *reason* in contriving and settling *rights, laws, constitutions*; in *inventing* arts, and *practicing* them so as to gratify, in the most effectual manner, that *generous inclination*. And if we must bring in *self-love* to make *virtue rational*, a little reflection will discover—as it may appear later—that this *benevolence* is our *greatest happiness*. From that realization, we may resolve as much as possible to cultivate this *sweet disposition* and to despise every *opposite interest*. Not that we can be *truly virtuous* if we intend only to obtain the pleasure which accompanies *beneficence* <note>The act of doing good or being charitable</note> without the *love of others*. Indeed, this very pleasure is founded on our being conscious of *disinterested love* to others as the *spring* <note>The source or motivating force</note> of our *actions*. But *self-interest* may be our *motive* in choosing to continue in this agreeable state, even though it cannot be the *sole* or principal *motive* of any *action* which appears *virtuous* to our *moral sense*.

The applying of a *mathematical calculation* to *moral subjects* may perhaps appear at first extravagant and wild; but some corollaries <note>Logical consequences or deductions that follow naturally from a proven proposition</note>, which are easily and certainly deduced below,* may show the convenience of this method.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on applying mathematical calculations to morality. It argues that virtue is not reserved for the powerful, but is accessible to everyone regardless of their social standing or natural abilities.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<margin>Section 3. <image-desc>A horizontal calligraphic flourish.</image-desc></margin>...this attempt, if it could be pursued further.

At present, we may take only this one point, which seems the most joyful imaginable even to the lowest rank of mankind: namely, that no external circumstances of fortune, and no involuntary disadvantages, can exclude any mortal from the *most heroic virtue*. For however small the *amount* <note>original: "Moment"; here meaning the magnitude or importance of an effect in a physical or mathematical sense</note> of *public good* anyone can accomplish, if their *abilities* are proportionally small, the *result* <note>original: "Quotient"; Hutcheson is using a mathematical metaphor where Virtue = Benevolence divided by Ability</note> expressing their *degree of virtue* may be as great as any other. 

Thus, it is not only the *prince*, the *statesman*, or the *general* who are capable of *true heroism*, though these are the primary characters whose fame is spread through various nations and ages. Instead, consider when we find in an *honest trader* a *kind friend*, a *faithful and prudent adviser*, a *charitable and hospitable neighbor*, a *tender husband*, and an *affectionate parent*. If we see a *calm yet cheerful companion*, a *generous supporter of merit*, a *careful soother of conflict and debate*, and a *promoter of love and good understanding among acquaintances*—and if we consider that these were *all* the *kind acts* <term>good offices</term> which his *position in the world* <note>original: "Station in the World"</note> gave him an opportunity to perform for humanity—then we must judge *this character* to be just as lovable as those whose external splendor dazzles an unwise world into the opinion that "they are the *only heroes in virtue*."

<summary>Hutcheson argues that virtue is relative to one's ability and opportunity; therefore, a common person performing small acts of kindness is as much a "hero" as a powerful leader performing great ones.</summary>

# S E C T. IV.

*All Mankind agree in this general Foundation of their Approval of moral Actions. The Grounds of the different Opinions about Morals.*

I. To see how far mankind agrees in what we have established as the universal foundation of this *moral sense*, namely BENEVOLENCE, we have already observed that when we are asked the reason for our approval <note>original: "Approbation"</note> of any action, we constantly allege its *usefulness* to the *public*, and not to the *actor* <term>the person performing the deed</term> himself. If we are defending a censured action and maintaining that it is lawful, we always make this one point of our defense: "That it injured nobody, or did more good than *harm*." On the other hand, when we blame any piece of conduct, we show it to be *harmful* <note>original: "prejudicial"</note> to others besides the *actor*; or at least to evidence a *neglect* of their *interests* when it was in our power to serve them, or when *gratitude*, *natural affection*, or some other *unselfish bond* <note>original: "disinterested Tie"</note> should have raised in us a concern for their interest. We all know how great an excuse <note>original: "Extenuation"</note> of

We may perhaps make mistakes in judging actions to tend toward the public good which do not; or be so stupidly careless <note>original: "inadvertent"</note> that, while our attention is fixed on some *partial good effects*, we may quite overlook many *evil consequences* which outweigh <note>original: "counter-ballance"</note> the *good*. The matter is plainly this: men are often mistaken about the tendency of actions to lead to either *public* or *private good*. Indeed, sometimes *violent passions*, while they last, will make us approve of very bad actions in a *moral sense*, or very destructive ones to the *agent* <note>the person performing the action</note> as if they were *advantageous*. “...or that men may be blinded by passion, even to their own *interest*.”

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But to prove that some men lack a *moral sense*, we would need to find examples of *cruel* and *malicious actions* performed and approved in others when there is no *motive* of *self-interest*, whether real or perceived, except for satisfying a sheer *desire* to cause mischief to others. We would have to find a country where *murder* in cold blood, *torture*, and *every malicious thing*—done without any *benefit* <note>original: "Advantage"</note>—is, if not *approved*, at least viewed with *indifference* and causes no *dislike* <note>original: "Aversion"</note> toward the actors from unbiased *spectators*. We would have to find men who hold the *treacherous*, *ungrateful*, and *cruel* in the same regard as the *generous*, *friendly*, *faithful*, and *humane*. Such people would approve of the latter no more than the former in all cases where they themselves are not affected by these behaviors, or when *physical good* or *harm* <note>original: "natural Good or Evil"; in this context, "natural" refers to physical or worldly outcomes rather than moral ones</note> happens to other people. It is doubtful whether the *universe*, although large enough and filled with a significant variety of characters, could provide us with any example—not just of a *nation*, but even of a *club* or a *single person*—holding such views, who would consider all actions indifferent except those that affect his *own self-interest* <note>original: "own Concerns"</note>.

<margin>Section 4.</margin>

This is indeed a good argument against **innate ideas** <note>The theory that humans are born with certain ideas or knowledge already in their minds.</note> or **principles**, but it does not prove that mankind lacks a **moral sense** to perceive **virtue** or **vice** when they observe actions.

The reasons for this **diversity** are mainly these:

1st. Different opinions of **happiness**, or **natural good** <note>original: "natural Good"; here meaning physical or worldly well-being rather than moral virtue</note>, and the most effective means to promote it. Thus, in one country where a **courageous temperament** prevails, where **liberty** is considered a **great good** and **war** a **minor evil**, all insurrections in defense of privileges will appear as a **moral good** to our **sense**, because they appear **benevolent**. And yet, the **same sense** of **moral good** found in **benevolence** shall make the same action appear hateful <note>original: "odious"</note> in another country where the spirits of men are more **dejected** and **fearful**, where **civil war** appears to be the **greatest natural evil**, and **liberty** is not considered **worth much** <note>original: "no great Purchase"</note>. So in SPARTA—where, through contempt for wealth, the security of possessions was not highly regarded—the thing chiefly desired as **naturally good** for the state was to have an abundance of **tough and resourceful** <note>original: "hardy, shifting"</note> **youth**. Therefore, **theft**, if skillfully...

For we should not imagine that this *sense* <note>The "moral sense," which Hutcheson argues is an innate human faculty.</note> provides us, prior to observation, with ideas of complex *actions* or of their natural *tendencies* toward *good* or *evil*. It only inclines us to approve of *benevolence* whenever it appears in any *action*, and to hate *the opposite*.

In the same way, our *sense of beauty* does not—without reflection, instruction, or observation—give us the ideas of *regular solids* <note>Geometric shapes such as cubes, spheres, or pyramids.</note>, *temples*, *cirques* <note>original: "Cirques"; ancient Roman arenas for public spectacles.</note>, and *theaters*. Instead, it leads us to approve of and delight in *uniformity* amidst *variety* wherever we observe it. Let us read the preambles of any laws we consider unjust, or the justifications of any disputed practice by the *moralists* <note>Philosophers or writers who specialize in ethics and human conduct.</note>, and we will find, without doubt, that men are often mistaken in calculating the balance of *natural good* or *evil consequences* resulting from certain actions. However, the ground on which any action is approved is still some perceived tendency toward the greater *natural good* of others, as understood by those who approve it.

The same reasoning may also remove objections against the *universality* of this *moral sense* that arise from certain stories told by travelers.

<margin>Section 4.</margin>
...concerning *strange cruelties* practiced toward the *elderly* or *children* in certain countries. If such actions are done in sudden fits of angry passion, they only prove that other motives or drivers of action <note>original: "springs of action"</note> may overpower *benevolence* even in its *strongest bonds*. And if these actions are truly universally allowed, looked upon as innocent, and justified, it is certainly under some appearance of *benevolence*. Examples might include securing them from the insults of enemies, avoiding the infirmities of age (which perhaps appear to be greater evils than death), or freeing vigorous and useful citizens from the expense of maintaining them or the trouble of caring for them. A love of pleasure and ease may, in those performing the acts <note>original: "immediate agents"</note>, be stronger in some instances than *gratitude* toward parents or *natural affection* for children. 

But the fact that such nations continue to exist, despite all the toil involved in educating the young, is still a sufficient proof of *natural affection*. For I imagine we are not to think there are any sophisticated <note>original: "nice"</note> laws in such places compelling parents to provide a proper education for a certain number of their offspring. We know very well that a perceived *public good* was the basis for laws—equally barbarous—enacted by Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta.</note> and Solon <note>An Athenian statesman and lawmaker.</note>, which allowed for killing the deformed or weak to prevent a burdensome crowd of useless citizens.

<page-num>186</page-num>

<margin>Section 4.</margin>

Men are given *reason* to judge and compare the tendencies of actions so that they do not blindly follow the first appearance of the *public good*. However, it is still some appearance of *good* that they pursue. It is strange that *reason* is universally granted to belong to men, despite all the stupid and ridiculous opinions held in many places; yet, when people see absurd practices founded upon those very opinions, they take it as an argument against the existence of any *moral sense*. In reality, bad conduct is not caused by a defect in the *moral sense*, but by a mistake in *judgment* or *opinion*. 

If putting the *elderly* to *death* (considering all the consequences) truly tended toward the *public good* and resulted in the *lesser misery* of the *elderly*, it would no doubt be *justifiable*. Indeed, *perhaps* the *elderly* choose it themselves in hopes of a *future state* <note>original: "future State"; referring to the afterlife.</note>. If a *deformed* or *weak group* <note>original: "Race"</note> could never, through ingenuity or skill, make themselves useful to mankind, but instead became an absolutely unbearable burden that involved a whole state in misery, it would be *just* to put them to death. Everyone admits this is *just* in the case of an overloaded boat in a storm. 

As for the *killing* of children when parents already have enough <note>original: "sufficiently stocked"</note>, this is perhaps practiced and allowed out of *self-love*; but I can hardly believe such an *action* is considered "good" or virtuous anywhere. If *wood*, *stone*, or *metal* is considered a deity, or if they have government...

If he delights in sacrifices, penances, ceremonies, and servile bowing <note>original: "Cringings"</note>, they are all praiseworthy <note>original: "laudable"</note>. Our sense of virtue generally leads us accurately enough according to our opinions; therefore, the absurd practices that prevail in the world are much better arguments that men lack reason than that they lack a moral sense of beauty in actions.

IV. The next cause <note>original: "Ground"</note> of diversity in sentiments is the diversity of systems to which men, due to foolish opinions, confine their benevolence. We suggested <note>original: "insinuated"</note> above* that it is orderly <note>original: "regular"</note> and beautiful to have a stronger benevolence toward the morally good parts of mankind, who are useful to the whole, than toward those who are useless or harmful <note>original: "pernicious"</note>. Now, if men adopt a low or base opinion of any group <note>original: "Body"</note> or sect of men—if they imagine them to be intent on the destruction of more valuable people, or merely useless burdens upon the earth—then benevolence itself will lead us to neglect the in-

If we were freely conversant with *robbers*, who show a *moral sense* in the *equal* or *proportional division* of their prey and in *loyalty* <note>original: "Faith"</note> to each other, we would find that they have their own sublime *moral ideas* of their group, seeing themselves as *generous*, *courageous*, *trustworthy*, and even *honest*. On the other hand, those we call *honest* and *industrious* are imagined by them to be *mean-spirited*, *selfish*, *ill-tempered* <note>original: "Churlish"</note>, or *extravagant* <note>original: "Luxurious"</note>. They believe wealth is poorly bestowed on such people, and therefore they would apply it to better uses: to maintain more gallant men, who have as much right to a living as their neighbors, even if those neighbors are their professed enemies. Indeed, if we observe the talk of our *professed debauchees*, our most dissolute *rakes*, we shall find their vices clothed, in their imaginations, in some attractive <note>original: "amiable"</note> dress of *liberty*, *generosity*, and just *resentment* against the inventors of clever rules designed to enslave men and rob them of their pleasures. The idea of an *ill-natured villain* is too frightful to ever become familiar to any mortal: and hence we shall find that the *basest actions* are dressed in some *tolerable mask*. What others call *avarice* appears to the *agent* as a *prudent care* for a *family* or *friends*; *fraud* as *artful conduct*; *malice* and *revenge* as a *just sense* of *honor* and a *vindication* of our *right* in *possessions* or *fame*; *fire* and *sword* and *desolation* among *enemies* as a *just, thorough defense* of our *country*; *persecution* as a *zeal* for the *truth* and for the *eternal happiness* of men, which *heretics* oppose. In all these instances, men generally act from a *sense* of *virtue* based on *false opinions* and *mistaken benevolence*; upon *wrong* or *partial views* of *public good* and the *means* to *promote* it; or upon very narrow *systems* formed by similarly *foolish opinions*. It is not a *delight* in the *misery* of others, or *malice*, which causes the horrid crimes that fill our *histories*, but generally an *injudicious, unreasonable enthusiasm* for some kind of *limited virtue*.

V. THE last ground of *diversity* that occurs is the *false opinions* regarding the *will* or *laws* of the DEITY. We are determined to obey these out of *gratitude* and a *sense* of *right* imagined to exist in the DEITY to dispose of the fortunes of his creatures at his pleasure. This is so widely known to have produced *follies*, *superstitions*, *murders*, and the *devastations* of kingdoms—all from a sense of *virtue* and *duty*—that it is unnecessary to mention specific instances. We may only observe, "That all those *follies* or *barbarities* actually confirm rather than destroy the opinion of a *moral sense*; since the DEITY is believed to have a *right* to dispose of his creatures, and *gratitude* toward him, if he is conceived as *good*, must move us to obey his will; if he is not conceived as *good*, *self-love* may overcome our *moral sense* of the action which we undertake to avoid his fury."

As for the vices which are commonly observed to proceed from the *love* of *pleasure* or any *violent passion*, since the *agent* is generally soon aware of their *evil*; and

VI. BEFORE we leave this subject, it is necessary to remove one of the strongest objections against what has been said so often, namely: "That this *sense is natural and independent of custom and education*." The objection is this: "That we shall find *some actions* always accompanied by the strongest abhorrence, even at first view, in some whole nations, where yet there is nothing contrary to *benevolence* apparent in the actions; and that the *same actions* shall in another nation be counted innocent or honorable. Now, we generally allow that what is from *nature* in one nation would be so in all. Thus *incest* among *Christians* is abhorred at first appearance as much as *murder*; and yet we cannot find any necessary tendency of it to the detriment of mankind, at least among *collaterals*. Now, this abhorrence cannot be from *nature*, since in GREECE the marrying of half-sisters was counted *honorable*; and among the *Persian* MAGI, the marrying of mothers. THE answer to this may be easily found from what has already been said: had we no *moral sense natural to us*, we would only look upon *incest* as hurtful to ourselves and shun it, and we would never hate other *incestuous persons* more than we do a bankrupt merchant; so that this abhorrence still supposes a *sense of moral good*. And further, it is true that it is very hard to show any natural tendency of some sorts of *incest* to lead to public *detriment*; but wherever it is hated, it is understood as offensive to the DEITY, and as exposing the persons concerned to his just vengeance. Now it is universally acknowledged to be the grossest ingratitude and baseness in any creature to counteract the will of the DEITY, to whom it is under such obligations; and this is plainly a *moral evil quality* perceived in *incest*, and reducible to the general foundation of a lack of *benevolence*, or to *malice*. Indeed, where this opinion—that *incest* is offensive to the DEITY—prevails, *incest* must have another direct opposition to *benevolence*; since we must perceive the *incestuous person* as exposing an associate, who should be dear to him by the ties of nature, to the lowest state of misery and baseness, infamy and punishment.

But in those countries where no such opinion prevails that the DEITY abhors *incest*, or prohibits it; since it does not appear that any obvious *natural evils* follow from it, it may be looked upon as innocent. And further, as men who have the *sense of tasting* may, through *company* and *education*, have prejudices against meats they have never tasted, regarding them as *unsavory*; so may men who have a *moral sense* acquire an opinion by implicit faith regarding the *moral evil* of actions, although they do not themselves discern in them any tendency toward *natural evil*, by imagining that others do so. But without a *moral sense*, we could receive no prejudice against actions under any other view than as being *naturally disadvantageous* to ourselves.

## SECT. V.

### *A further confirmation that we have practical dispositions to virtue implanted in our nature, with a further explanation of our instinct for benevolence in its various degrees; with the additional motives of self-interest, namely honor, shame, and pity.*

I. WE have already endeavored to prove,
> “That there is a universal determination toward benevolence in mankind, even toward the most distant parts of the species:”

But we are not to imagine that this *benevolence* is equal, or in the same degree toward everyone; there are some closer and stronger degrees of *benevolence* when the objects of it stand in closer relations to ourselves, which have obtained distinct names, such as *natural affection* and *gratitude*; or when *benevolence* is increased by a greater *love of esteem*.

Now, it is certain that *ambition* is founded on *self-love*; but then our *determination* to love *honor* presupposes a *sense of moral virtue*, both in the *people* who confer the *honor* and in him who pursues it. And let it be observed that if we knew an *agent* had no other *motive for action* but *ambition*, we would perceive no *virtue* even in his most useful *actions*, since they did not flow from any *love* for others or *desire* for their *happiness*. **Shame** is in the same manner constituted an *immediate evil*, and influences us the same way to abstain from *moral evil*; not that any *action* or omission would appear *virtuous*, where the sole *motive* was fear of *shame*.

AGAIN, we shall be *ashamed* of every evidence of *moral incapacity*, or lack of *ability*; and with good reason, when this lack is occasioned by our own negligence. Nay further, if any circumstance be looked upon as *indecent* in any country, *offensive* to others, and *deformed*; we shall, out of our love for the good opinions of others, be *ashamed* to be found in such circumstances, even when we are aware that this indecency or offense is not founded on *nature*, but is merely the effect of *custom*. Thus, being observed in some of the functions of nature, which are counted *indecent* and *offensive*, will make us *uneasy*, although we are aware that they really do not indicate any *vice* or *weakness*. But on the contrary, since *moral abilities* of any kind, upon the general presumption of a good application, procure the esteem of others, we shall value ourselves upon them, or grow proud of them, and be ashamed of any discovery of our lack of such *abilities*. So too are all other kinds of generous acts between *married persons*, where there is no suspicion of *self-love* in the *agent*, but he is imagined as acting from love for his *associate*. This, I suspect, first introduced ideas of *modesty* in polite nations, and *custom* has strengthened them wonderfully; so that we are now ashamed of many things based on some confused, implicit opinions of *moral evil*, though we do not know for what reason.

HERE too we may see the reason why we are not ashamed of any of the methods of *grandeur* or *lavish living*. There is such a mixture of *moral ideas*, of *benevolence*, and of *abilities* kindly employed—so many dependents *supported*, so many friends *entertained*, *assisted*, and *protected*; such a *capacity* imagined for great and lovable *actions*—that we are never ashamed, but rather

This view of *honor* and *shame* may also let us see the reason why most people are uneasy at being praised when they themselves are present. Everyone is delighted with the esteem of others, and must enjoy great pleasure when he hears himself commended; but we are unwilling for others to observe our enjoyment of this pleasure, which is really *selfish*; or that they should imagine us fond of it, or influenced by hopes of it in our good actions; and therefore we choose secrecy for the enjoyment of it, as we do with respect to other pleasures in which others do not share with us.

<margin>Sect. 5.</margin>and that is COMPASSION; by which we are disposed to study the *interest* of others, without any views of *private advantage*: This needs little illustration. Every mortal is made uneasy by any grievous misery he sees another involved in, unless the person is imagined to be *evil* in a *moral sense*. Indeed, it is almost impossible for us to be unmoved, even in that case. *Advantage* may make us do a cruel action, or may overcome *pity*, but it scarcely ever extinguishes it. A sudden passion of *hatred* or *anger* may represent a person as *absolutely evil*, and so extinguish *pity*; but when the passion is over, it often returns. Another *disinterested view* may even in cold blood overcome *pity*, such as *love for our country* or *zeal for religion*. *Persecution* is generally occasioned by the *love of virtue* and the *desire for the eternal happiness of mankind*, although our *folly* makes us choose absurd means; and it is often accompanied with enough *pity* to make the *persecutor* uneasy in what he chooses for prevailing reasons, unless his *opinion* leads him to look upon the *heretic* as *absolutely* and *entirely evil*.

WE may here observe how wonderfully the constitution of *human nature* is adapted to move *compassion*. Our *misery* and *distress* immediately appear in our *countenance*, if we do not study to prevent it, and propa-

propagates some pain to all spectators, who from observation universally understand the meaning of those dismal *expressions*. We *mechanically* send forth *shrieks* and *groans* upon any surprising perception of *evil*, so that sometimes no regard for decency can restrain them. Thus, all who are present are aroused to our assistance, and sometimes our *injurious enemy* is made to relent.

WE observed above<note>* See Section 2, Article VIII.</note>, that we are not immediately motivated by *compassion* to desire the *removal* of our own pain; we think it right to be so affected on such an occasion, and dislike those who are not. But we are motivated directly to desire the *relief* of the miserable; and if we see this is impossible, we may by *reflection* discern it to be useless for us to indulge our *compassion* any further; and then, out of *self-love*, we retire from the object which causes our pain and study how to divert our thoughts. But where there is no such *reflection*, people are hurried by a *natural, kind instinct* to see objects of *compassion* and expose themselves to this pain when they can give no reason for it, as in the instance of *public executions*.
