## SECT. VI.

### Concerning the Importance of this *moral Sense* to the present *Happiness* of *Mankind*, and its *Influence* on *human Affairs*.

I. IT may now probably appear that, despite the corruption of manners so justly complained of everywhere, this *moral sense* has a greater influence on *mankind* than is generally imagined, although it is often directed by very partial, imperfect views of *public good*, and often overcome by *self-love*. But we shall offer some further considerations to prove "that it gives us more *pleasure* and *pain* than all our other *faculties*." And to prevent repetitions, let us observe that wherever it appears that any *morally good quality* gives pleasure from *reflection* or from *honor*, the contrary *evil one* will give proportionate pain from *remorse* and *shame*. Now we shall consider the *moral pleasures*, not only separately, but as they are the *most delightful ingredient* in the ordinary pleasures of life.

<summary>The author argues that the moral sense exerts a greater influence on humanity than typically recognized, despite the existence of corruption. He asserts that the moral sense provides more significant pleasure and pain than any other human faculty and is a primary ingredient in overall life satisfaction.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, happiness, public good, self-love, pleasure, pain, faculties, moral quality, honor, remorse, shame</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---

It suggests that our true nature is best observed when we judge the lives of others impartially.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

### Sect. 6.
---

**ALL MANKIND** seem persuaded of some *excellence* in the possession of *good moral qualities*, which is superior to all other enjoyments; and on the contrary, look upon a state of *moral evil* as worse and more wretched than any other whatsoever. We must not form our judgment in this matter from the actions of men; for however they may be influenced by *moral sentiments*, yet it is certain that *self-interested passions* frequently overcome them, and *partial views* of the tendency of actions make us do what is really *morally evil*, apprehending it to be *good*. But let us examine the sentiments which men universally form of the state of others, when they are in no way immediately concerned; for in these sentiments *human nature* is calm and undisturbed, and shows its true face.

Now would we imagine a rational *creature* in a sufficiently happy state, when his mind was without interruption wholly occupied with pleasant sensations of *smell*, *taste*, *touch*, *etc.*, if at the same time all other ideas were excluded? Would we not think the state *low*, *mean*, and *sordid* if there were no *society*, no *love* or *friendship*, no *kind acts*? What then must that state be wherein there are no pleasures but those of the external *Senses*, with such long intervals as *human nature* at present

This *frame* of our *nature*, so incapable of long enjoyments of the *external senses*, points out to us "that there must be some other more *durable pleasure*, without such *tedious interruptions* and *nauseous reflections*."

Let us even join with the pleasures of the external senses the perceptions of *beauty*, *order*, and *harmony*: these are no doubt more *noble pleasures* and seem to enlarge the *mind*; and yet how *cold* and *joyless* are they if there are no *moral pleasures* of *friendship*, *love*, and *beneficence*? Now if the bare absence of *moral good* makes the state of a rational agent contemptible in our judgment, the presence of *contrary dispositions* is always imagined by us to sink him into a degree of misery from which no other pleasures can relieve him. Would we ever wish to be in the same condition as a *wrathful*, *malicious*, *revengeful*, or *envious being*, even if we were at the same time to enjoy all the external sensations of pleasure, or all the opportunities of seeing the most *beautiful, regular prospects* and hearing the most *harmonious sounds*, or

### **Sect. 6.** obtaining the most extensive *Knowledge*?
---

These internal pleasures of *beauty* and *harmony* have a great power to soothe the mind into a forgetfulness of *wrath*, *malice*, or *revenge*; and they must do so before we can have any tolerable delight or enjoyment: for while these *affections* possess the mind, there is nothing but *torment* and *misery*.

What *castle-builder* <note>One who constructs elaborate daydreams or fantasies.</note>, who forms for himself imaginary scenes of life in which he thinks he would be happy, ever made *acknowledged treachery*, *cruelty*, or *ingratitude* the steps by which he mounted to his wished-for elevation, or parts of his character once he had attained it? In such reveries, we always conduct ourselves according to the *dictates* of *honor*, *faith*, *generosity*, and *courage*; and the lowest we can sink is hoping we may be enriched by some innocent accident:

> *O si urnam Argenti* FORS *quà mihi monstret!* <note>Horace, Satires, Book 2, Satire 6, line 10: "O if only some lucky chance would show me a jar of silver!"</note>

Where there is no *virtue*, there is nothing worth desire or contemplation; the *romance* or *epos* <note>An epic poem or narrative.</note> must end. Indeed, the *difficulty* \* or *natural evil* <note>Physical suffering or misfortune, as opposed to moral wrongdoing.</note> increases the *virtue* of the good action which it accompanies so much that we cannot easily endure these works after the distress is over; and if we do continue the work, it must be by presenting a new scene of *benevolence* in a prosperous fortune. A scene of *external prosperity* or *natural good*, without anything *moral* or *virtuous*, cannot entertain a person of even the dullest imagination, no matter how much he had interested himself in the fortunes of his hero. For where *virtue* ceases, there remains nothing worth wishing for our favorite, or which we can be delighted to view his possession of, even when we are most concerned for his happiness.

LET us take a particular instance to see how much we prefer the possession of *virtue* to all other enjoyments, and how we look upon *vice* as worse than any other misery. Now, may this not teach us that we are indeed determined to judge *virtue* with peace and safety as preferable to *virtue* with distress; but that at the same time we look upon the state of the *virtuous*, the *public-spirited*, even in the utmost natural distress, as preferable to all the affluence of other enjoyments? For this is what we choose to have our favorite hero in, notwithstanding all its pains and natural evils. II. Let us in the same manner examine our sentiments regarding the happiness of others in common life. Wealth and external pleasures carry no small weight in our imaginations; but does there not always accompany this opinion of happiness in *wealth* some supposed *beneficent intention* of doing good deeds for persons dear to *us*, or at least to our *families* or *kinsmen*? And in our imagined happiness of *external pleasure*, some ideas are always included of some *moral enjoyments* of *society*, some *communication* of pleasure, and something of *love*, *friendship*, *esteem*, or *gratitude*. Who ever pretended to have a *taste* for pleasures without *society*? Or if any seem violent in the pursuit of such pleasures, how base and contemptible do they appear to all people—even to those who could have no expectation of advantage from their having a more generous notion of pleasure? Now, if there were no *moral sense*, no happiness in *benevolence*, and if we acted from no other principle than *self-love*, surely there is no pleasure of the external senses whose solitary perceptions we could not obtain

And yet *love*, *friendship*, *society*, and *humanity*, though accompanied by *poverty* and *toil*, or even by smaller degrees of *pain* that do not wholly occupy the mind, are not only the object of love from others, but even of a sort of emulation; which plainly shows "that *virtue* is the chief *happiness* in the universal judgment of *mankind*."

III. THERE is a further consideration which must not be passed over concerning the EXTERNAL BEAUTY of persons, which all allow to have great power over human minds. Now, it is some perceived morality, some natural or imagined indication of accompanying *virtue*, which gives it this powerful *charm* above all other kinds of *beauty*. Let us consider the characteristics of *beauty* which are commonly admired in countenances, and we shall find them to be *sweetness*, *mildness*, *majesty*, *dignity*, *vivacity*, *humility*, *tenderness*, and *good nature*; that is, that certain *airs*, *proportions*, and *je ne sais quois* are natural indications of such *virtues*, or of abilities or dispositions toward them. As we observed above <margin>* See *Section* 5. *Article* VIII. *Paragraph* 2.</margin> regarding *misery* or *distress* appearing in countenances; so it is certain that almost all habitual *dispositions of mind* shape the countenance in such a manner as to give some indications of them to the spectator. Our violent passions are obvious at first view in the countenance, so that sometimes no art can conceal them; and smaller degrees of them give some less obvious turns to the face, which an accurate eye will observe. Now, when the natural *air* of any face approaches to that which any passion would form it into, we make a

Section 6. Conjecture from this concerning the *leading disposition of the person's mind.*

Regarding those preferences which prevail in certain countries for *large lips, small noses, and narrow eyes*, unless we knew from the people themselves under what idea such forms are admired—whether as *naturally beautiful* in form or proportion to the rest of the face, or as presumed indications of some *moral qualities*—we may more probably conclude that it is the *latter*. This is because such indications are so often the basis for approval or dislike of faces among ourselves. And as for those irregularities of features which we consider *naturally disagreeable* in form, we know that the aversion on this account is so weak that *moral qualities* can produce a liking, even for that face, in people who are aware of the *irregularity* or the lack of that *regularity* which is common in others. Among us, certain features are imagined to denote *dullness*, such as *hollow eyes* or *large lips*, and a certain color of hair to denote *wantonness*. May we not conclude that a similar *association of ideas*—perhaps in both cases without any foundation in *nature*—is the basis for those preferences that appear unaccountable to us?

In the same manner, when there is nothing grossly disproportionate in any face, what is it we criticize? It is *pride, haugh-*

IV. THIS same consideration may be extended to the whole *air* and *motion* of any person. Everything we count agreeable in some way denotes *cheerfulness, ease*, a *condescension*, and *readiness* to oblige, a *love of company*, with a *freedom* and *boldness* which always accompanies an honest, undesigning heart. On the contrary, what is shocking in *air* or *motion* is *roughness, ill-nature*, a *disregard* for others, or a *foolish shamefacedness*, which evidences a person to be inexperienced in society or the duties of humanity.

WITH relation to these *airs, motions, gestures*, we may observe that, considering the different *ceremonies* and *modes* of showing respect which are practiced in different nations, we may indeed probably conclude that there is no *natural connection* between any of these *gestures* or *motions* and the *affections of the mind* which they are by *custom* made to express; but when *custom* has made any of them pass for *expressions* of such *affections*, by a constant *association of ideas*, some shall become *agreeable* and *lovely*, and others *extremely offensive*, although they were both, in their own nature, *perfectly indifferent*.

We might have been driven to the propagation of our species by such an uneasy sensation that it would have effectively compelled us to it without any great prospect of happiness; just as we see *hunger* and *thirst* compel us to preserve our bodies, though few regard eating and drinking as a significant source of happiness. *The sexes* might have been brought together, just as we imagine the *animals* are, by *desire* alone, or by a *love of sensual pleasure*. But how dull and tasteless life would have been if there were nothing more to **MARRIAGE**? Who would have had enough resolution to bear all the cares of a *family* and the *education of children*? Or who, from a general motive of *benevolence*, would have chosen to subject himself to natural *affection* toward an *offspring* when he could foresee all the troubles it might cause? This inclination of the *sexes*, therefore, is founded on something stronger, and more effective and joyful, than the promptings of *uneasiness* or the mere *desire for physical pleasure*.

And this may provide us with a reason for what appears quite unaccountable: namely, that *Chastity* itself has a powerful charm in the eyes of the *Dissolute*, even when they are attempting to destroy it.

As soon as a heart, previously *hard* and *stubborn*, is softened in this flame, we shall observe a *love* of *poetry*, *music*, the *beauty* of *nature* in *rural scenes*, a *disdain* for other selfish pleasures of the external senses, a *neat dress*, a *humane behavior*, and a *delight* in and *imitation* of everything which is *gallant*, *generous*, and *friendly*.

**I**N the same manner, we are determined toward common friendships and acquaintances, not by the somber perceptions of our *necessities*, or *prospects* of *self-interest*; but by an incredible variety of small, agreeable, and engaging signs of *love*, *good nature*, and other *morally attractive qualities* in those we converse with: And among the rest, one of the most significant is an inclination toward *cheerfulness*, and a *delight* in raising *mirth* in others, which earns a secret approval and gratitude toward the person who puts us in such an *agreeable*, *innocent*,

VI. UPON this *moral sense* is founded all the power of the ORATOR. The various figures of speech are the several manners which a lively genius, warmed with passions suitable to the occasion, naturally runs into, only a little diversified by *custom*: and they only move the *hearers* by giving a lively representation of the passions of the *speaker*, which are communicated to the *hearers*, as we *observed* above of one passion, namely *pity*.

NOW the passions which the *orator* attempts to raise are all founded on *moral qualities*. All the bold *metaphors* or *descriptions*, all the artificial manners of *expostulation*, *arguing*, and *addressing* the *audience*, all the *appeals to mankind*, are but more lively methods of giving the *audience* a stronger impression of the *moral qualities* of the person *accused* or *defended*, of the *action advised* or *dissuaded*: and all the *antitheses* or *witticisms*, all the *cadences* of sonorous *periods* <note>In traditional rhetoric, a "period" is a complex sentence structured with several balanced clauses to achieve a persuasive or rhythmic effect.</note>, whatever inferior kind of beauty they may have separately, are of no consequence to persuade,

if we neglect moving the passions by some kind of *morality*. They may perhaps raise a little admiration for the *speaker* among those who already favor his party, but they more often raise contempt in his *adversaries*. But when you display the *beneficence* of any action, the *good effect* it shall have on the *public* in promoting the welfare of the *innocent*, and relieving the *unjustly distressed*; if you prove your allegations, you make every mortal approve of undertaking it. When any person is to be recommended, display his *humanity, generosity, pursuit* of the *public good*, and *capacity* to promote it, his *contempt of dangers*, and private pleasures; and you are sure to procure him *love* and *esteem*. If at the same time you show his *distress*, or the *injuries* he has suffered, you raise *pity*, and every tender affection.

**O**n the contrary, represent the *barbarity*, or *cruelty* of any action, the *misery* it shall cause for the *kind*, the *faithful*, the *generous*, or only to the *innocent*; and you raise an abhorrence of it in the breasts of the *audience*, though they were not the persons who would have suffered by it. Now, are the *learned* and *refined* the only persons who are moved by such speeches? Must men know the schemes of the *moralists* and *politicians*, or the *art of rhetoric*, to be capable of being persuaded? Must they be thoroughly familiar with all the methods of promoting *self-interest*? No; do we not see on the contrary, the *unrefined, undisciplined multitude* most affected? Where had *oratory* so much power as in *popular states*, and that too before the perfection of the sciences? *Reflection* and *study* may raise in men a suspicion of design and a caution in giving assent, once they have some knowledge of the various topics of argument and find them employed upon themselves; but *unrefined nature* is still open to every *moral impression* and carried furiously along without caution or hesitation. VII. WE shall find the same *moral sense* to be the foundation of the chief pleasures of POETRY. We hinted, in the former treatise, at the foundation of delight in *numbers, measures, metaphors, and similitudes* <note>* See *Treatise* I. *Sect.* 2. *Art.* XIII. *Sect.* 4. *Art.* III.</note>. But as the contemplation of *moral objects*, either of *vice* or *virtue*, affects us more strongly, and moves our passions in a quite different and more powerful manner than *natural beauty*, or (what we commonly call) *deformity*; so the most moving beauties bear a relation to our *moral sense*, and affect us more vehemently than the representation of *natural objects* in the liveliest descriptions. *Dramatic* and *epic poetry* are entirely addressed to this *sense*, and raise our passions by the *fortunes of characters* distinctly represented as *morally good* or *evil*; as might be seen more fully, were we to consider the passions separately.

<summary>The author concludes that a sense of morality exists prior to formal instruction and then argues that this same moral sense is the primary source of pleasure in poetry, particularly in dramatic and epic forms.</summary>

When we are studying to raise any *desire* or *admiration* of an *object* that is really *beautiful* <image-desc>decorative flourish</image-desc>, we are not content with a bare *narration*, but endeavor, if we can, to present the *object* itself, or the most *vivid image* of it. And hence it is that the *epic poem* or *tragedy* provides a vastly greater pleasure than the writings of *philosophers*, though both aim at recommending *virtue*. Representing the actions themselves—if the representation is *judicious*, *natural*, and *vivid*—will make us admire the *good* and detest the *vicious*, the *inhuman*, the *treacherous*, and the *cruel*, by means of our *moral sense*, without any reflections from the *poet* to guide our sentiments. It is for this reason that HORACE has justly made knowledge in *ethics* so necessary to a good poet:

> *Scribendi rectè* SAPERE *est & principium & fons* <note>* Horace, *The Art of Poetry*, v. 309.</note>.

And again:

> *Qui didicit Patriæ quid debeat, & quid Amicis,*
> *Quo sit amore Parens, quo Frater amandus, & Hospes,*
> *Quod sit Conscripti, quod Judicis officium, quæ*

UPON this same *sense* is founded the power of that great *beauty* in poetry, the <term>PROSOPOPOEIA</term>, by which *every affection* is made a *person*; every *natural event*, *cause*, and *object* is animated by *moral epithets*: for we join the contemplation of *moral circumstances* and *qualities* along with *natural objects*, to increase the *beauty* or *deformity*, and affect the *hearer* in a more lively manner with the *affections* described, by representing them as *persons*. Thus a *shady wood* must have its *solemn, venerable genius*, and proper *rural gods*; every *clear fountain*, its *sacred, chaste nymph*; and *river*, its *bountiful god*, with his *urn*, and perhaps a *cornucopia* diffusing *plenty* and *fruitfulness* along his banks. The *daylight* is *holy*, *benign*, and *powerful* to banish the pernicious spirits of the *night*. Nay, the *most sacred Poets* are often led into this *Imagery*, and represent *Justice* and *Judgment* as supporting the ALMIGHTY's Throne, and *Mercy* and *Truth* going before his Face: They show us *Peace* as *springing* up from the Earth, and *Mercy* *looking* down from Heaven.

HISTORY derives its chief excellence from representing *manners* and *characters*; since the contemplation of these in *nature* is very affecting, they must necessarily give pleasure when well related. It is also well known that a collection of the best pieces of *portrait painting* is but a poor entertainment when compared with those pieces which represent *moral actions, passions, and characters.*

IT is perhaps true, that *Reflection* and *Reason* might lead us to approve the same Actions as *advantageous*. But would not the same *Reflection* and *Reason* likewise generally recommend the same *foods* to us which our *Taste* represents as pleasant? And shall we thence conclude that we have no *Sense* of *Tasting*? or that such a *Sense* is *useless*? No: The use is plain in both Cases. Notwithstanding the mighty *Reason* we boast of above other Animals, its Processes are too slow, too full of doubt and hesitation, to serve us in every exigency, either for our own preservation, without the external Senses, or to direct our Actions for the Good of the *Whole*, without this *moral Sense*. Nor could we be so strongly determined at all times to what were most conducive to either of these ends, without these prompt *Monitors*, and *urgent Solicitors*; nor so nobly rewarded when we act vigorously in pursuit of these ends, by the calm dull reflections of *Self-Interest*, as by those delightful sensations.

<summary>The author argues that while reason and self-interest might eventually recommend virtuous actions, they are too slow and hesitant to guide us in every urgent situation. The moral sense serves as a necessary and immediate prompt for both self-preservation and the common good, providing a more immediate delight than the dull calculations of self-interest.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 245, which posits that a moral sense is as necessary as our physical senses because reason is too slow for immediate moral judgment. This page specifically addresses the criticism that such a "moral sense" is an unexplained or "occult" quality.</meta>

<page-num>246</page-num>

<margin>Sect. 6.
<image-desc>A decorative horizontal flourish or squiggle.</image-desc></margin>

This *natural determination* to approve and admire, or hate and dislike actions, is no doubt an *occult quality*. But is it in any way more mysterious that the idea of an action should raise *esteem* or *contempt*, than that the motion or tearing of flesh should give *pleasure* or *pain*, or the act of volition should move *flesh* and *bones*? In the latter case, we have the brain, elastic fibers, animal spirits, and elastic fluids—like the *Indian's* elephant and tortoise—to bear the burden of the difficulty; but go one step further, and you find the whole matter as difficult as it was at first, and equally a mystery with *this determination* to love and approve, or hate and despise *actions* and *agents*, without any views of *self-interest*, as they appear *benevolent* or the contrary.

When they offer it as a presumption that there can be no such *sense* prior to all prospect of *interest*,

> “state may be some way made better;”

may we not ask—supposing the Deity intended to instill such a *sense* of something lovable in actions (which is no impossible supposition)—what sort of actions would

a *good* GOD cause us to *approve*? Must we deny the possibility of such a predisposition if it did not lead us to admire *actions* of no *advantage* to *mankind*, or to love *agents* for being *distinguished* *triflers*? If, then, the actions which a *wise* and *good* GOD must predispose us to approve—if He gives us any such *sense* at all—must be actions *useful* to the *public*, this *advantage* can never be an argument against the *sense* itself. In the same way, we would have to deny all *revelation* that taught us *good sense*, *humanity*, *justice*, and *rational worship* simply because *reason* and *self-interest* confirm and recommend such *principles* and *services*; and we would have to greedily embrace every *contradiction*, *absurdity*, and *empty display* as a truly *divine institution* simply because it contained nothing *humane* or *useful* to *mankind*.

IX. THE writers of opposing systems—who derive all ideas of *good* and *evil* from the *private advantage* of the *actor*, or from a relationship to a *law* and its *sanctions* (known either through *reason* or *revelation*)—are continually returning to this *moral sense* which they deny. They do this not only by calling the *laws* of the DEITY *just* and *good*, and claiming that *justice* and *right* allow the DEITY to govern us, but also by using a set of words that mean something different from what they claim is their only

<summary>The author argues that the usefulness of virtuous actions does not disprove the existence of an innate moral sense, as a wise God would naturally design such a sense to favor the public good. # S E C T. VII.

## *A Deduction of some Complex moral Ideas, namely, of Obligation, and Right, Perfect, Imperfect, and External, Alienable, and Unalienable, from this moral Sense.*

I. To conclude this subject, we may, from what has been said, see the true *origin of moral ideas, namely:* "This *moral sense of excellence in every appearance, or evidence of benevolence;*" and that we have ideas of *virtue* and *vice*, independently of any *law, human* or *divine*.

If anyone asks, "Can we have any sense of **OBLIGATION** independently of the *laws* of a *superior*?" We must answer according to the various meanings of the word *obligation*. If by **OBLIGATION** we understand a *determination, without regard to our own interest, to approve actions, and to perform them; which determination shall also make us displeased with ourselves, and uneasy upon having acted contrary to this sense*; in this meaning of the word *obligation*, there is naturally an *obliga-*

<summary>The author concludes that moral ideas of virtue and vice arise from an innate sense of excellence in benevolence rather than from external laws. He begins to define "obligation" as an internal determination to act rightly that exists independently of a superior's commands or personal self-interest.</summary>
<keywords>obligation, moral sense, benevolence, virtue, vice, ethics, moral philosophy, origin of ideas</keywords>

Or, which is to the same purpose, this *internal sense* and *instinct* toward *benevolence* will either influence our actions or else make us very uneasy and dissatisfied; and we shall be conscious that we are in a base, unhappy state, even without considering any *law* whatsoever, or any lost external advantages or impending disadvantages from its sanctions. It must then first be supposed that there is something in actions which is apprehended as *absolutely good*; and this is *benevolence*, or a *tendency toward the public natural happiness of rational agents*; and that our *moral sense* perceives this *excellence*. IV. From this sense too we derive our ideas of RIGHTS. Whenever it appears to us that *a faculty of doing, demanding, or possessing anything, universally allowed in certain circumstances, would on the whole tend toward the general good*, we say that any person in such circumstances has a *right* to *do, possess, or demand* that *thing*. And according as this *tendency* toward the *public good* is greater or less, the *right* is greater or less.

The *rights* called *perfect* are of such necessity to the public good that the *universal violation* of them would make *human life intolerable*, and it actually makes those miserable whose *rights* are thus *violated*. And on the contrary, to fulfill these *rights* in every instance tends toward the *public good*, either *directly* or by promoting the innocent advantage of a *part*. And as to the general consequences, the universal use of force in a *state of nature*, in pursuit of *perfect rights*, seems exceedingly *advantageous* to the *whole*, by making everyone fear any attempts against the *perfect rights* of others. This is the *moral effect* which follows *actual injury*, or a *violation* of the *perfect rights of others*: namely, a right to *war*, and *all violence* which is necessary to compel the injurious party to repair the damage and provide security against such offenses in the future. Instances of *perfect rights* are those to our *lives*; to the *fruits of our labor*; to demand the performance of *contracts* based on valuable considerations from those capable of performing them; to *direct* our own *actions* for either the *public* or *innocent private good*, before we have submitted them to the direction of others in any way; and many others of a similar nature.

**IMPERFECT** *rights* are *those that, when universally violated, would not necessarily make people miserable.* These *rights* tend toward the improvement and increase of *positive good* in any society, but are not absolute-

The violation of them only disappoints people of the happiness expected from the humanity or gratitude of others, but does not deprive people of any *good* which they had before. From this description it appears, "that a violent prosecution of such *rights* would generally occasion greater *evil* than the violation of them." Besides, the allowing of force in such cases would deprive people of the greatest pleasure in actions of *kindness*, *humanity*, and *gratitude*, which would cease to appear amiable when people could be constrained to perform them. Instances of *imperfect rights* are those which the *poor* have to the charity of the *wealthy*; which all *people* have to services of no trouble or expense to the performer; which *benefactors* have to returns of gratitude, and such like. The violation of *imperfect rights* only suggests a person has such weak *benevolence* that they do not study advancing the *positive good* of others when it is in the least opposite to their own; but the violation of *perfect rights* suggests the *injurious person* is *positively evil* or *cruel*, or at least so *immoderately selfish* as to be indifferent about the *positive misery* and *ruin* of others when he imagines he can find his own *interest* in it. In violating the *former*, we show a weak desire for *public happiness*, which every small view of *private interest* overbalances;

Now as the absence of *good* is more easily borne than the presence of *misery*, so the power of our *good wishes* toward the *positive good* of others, is weaker than that of *compassion* toward their *misery*. He then who violates *imperfect rights*, shows that his *self-love* overcomes only the desire of *positive good* to others; but he who violates *perfect rights*, betrays such a *selfish desire* of advancing his own *positive good*, as overcomes all the power of *compassion* toward the *misery* of others.

BESIDE these two sorts of *rights*, there is a third called *external*; as when the *doing*, *possessing*, or *demanding* of anything is really detrimental to the *public* in any particular instance, as being contrary to the *imperfect right* of another: but yet the *universal* denying men this *faculty* of *doing*, *possessing*, or *demanding* that *thing*, or of *using force* in pursuit of it, would do more mischief than all the evils to be feared from the *use* of this *faculty*. And hence it appears, "That
" there can be no right to use force in
" opposition even to external rights, since
" it tends to the *universal good* to allow
" force in pursuit of them."

INSTANCES of external *rights* are these: that of a *wealthy miser* to recall his *loan* from the most industrious poor *tradesman* at any time; that of demanding the performance of a *covenant* too burdensome on one side; the right of a *wealthy heir* to refuse payment of any debts which were contracted by him while under age, even without fraud in the lender; the right of taking advantage of a *positive law*, contrary to what was *equity* prior to that law—as when a *registered deed* takes precedence over one not registered, although the unregistered one was earlier and known to be so before the second contract.

V. THERE is another important difference of *rights*, according to whether they are *alienable* or *unalienable*. To determine what *rights* are alienable and what are not, we must use these two markers:

1st. IF the alienation is within our natural power, so that it is possible for us in fact to transfer our *right*; and if it is so, then,

2ndly. IT must appear that a power to transfer such *rights* may serve some valuable purpose.

either we ourselves, or any other **Person** pleases; and so too are our *internal Affections*, which necessarily arise according to our **Opinions** of their **Objects**. By the *second Mark* it appears, “That our *Right* of *serving* **GOD** in the manner which we *think acceptable*, is not *alienable*;” because it can never serve any valuable purpose to make **Men** serve him in a way which seems to them displeasing to him. In the same way, a *direct Right* over our *Lives* or *Limbs* is not *alienable* to any *Person*, so that he might put us to death or maim us at his pleasure. We have indeed a *Right* to hazard our **Lives** in any good **Action** which is of importance to *the Public*; and it may often serve a most valuable end to subject the direction of such perilous **Actions** to the **Prudence** of others in pursuing a *public Good*; as *Soldiers* do to their *General*, or to a *Council of War*: and to this extent, this *Right* is *alienable*. These may serve as **Instances** to show the **Use** of the two **Marks** of *alienable Rights*, which must both concur to make them so, and will explain the manner of applying them in other **Cases**.

VI. **THAT** we may see the **Foundation** of some of the more *important Rights* of **Mankind**, let us observe **that** probably nine-**tenths**, at least, of the things which are useful to **Mankind** are owing to their *Labour and Industry*; and consequently all

People are obliged to observe such a tenor of action as shall most effectively promote *industry*; and to abstain from all actions which would have the contrary effect. It is well known that *general benevolence* alone is not a motive strong enough for *industry*, to bear *labor* and *toil*, and many other difficulties which we are averse to because of *self-love*. For the strengthening, therefore, of our motives to *industry*, we have the strongest attractions of *blood*, of *friendship*, of *gratitude*, and the additional motives of *honor*, and even of *external interest*. *Self-love* is really as necessary to the *good* of the *whole* as *benevolence*; just as that *attraction* which causes the *cohesion* of the parts is as necessary to the *regular state* of the *whole* as *gravitation*. Without these additional motives, *self-love* would generally oppose the motions of *benevolence* and concur with *malice*, or influence us toward the same actions which *malice* would.

> “Whatever tenor of action then would
> “take away the stronger ties of *benevolence*, or the additional motives of *honor* and *advantage* from our minds,
> “and so hinder us from pursuing *industriously* that course which really increases the *good* of the *whole*, is evil;
> “and we are obliged to shun it.”

And from this appears the *right* which men have to lay up for the *future* those goods which will not be spoiled by time; to alienate them in *trade*; and to donate them to *friends*, *children*, and *relations*. Otherwise, we deprive *industry* of all the motives of *self-love*, *friendship*, *gratitude*, and *natural affection*. There is the same foundation for the *right* of disposition by *testament*. The presumption of this disposition is the ground of the right of succession for the *intestate*.

1. THE failure, in whole or in part, of any attempt, *good* or *evil*, if it is caused only by external force or any unforeseen accident, does not change the *moral good* or *evil*; for just as in *good* attempts, the *moment of good*, or [M], is diminished or vanishes in such a case, so does the *ability*, or [A], likewise. The *quotient* then may still be the same, and the same applies to *evil* attempts. Therefore, actions are not to be judged *good* or *evil* by their outcomes, any further than those outcomes might have been foreseen by the *agent* in *evil* attempts, or were actually intended in *good actions*; for only then do they demonstrate either *love* or *hatred* in the *agent*.

2. SECULAR *rewards* attached to *virtue*, which actually influence the *agent* further than his *benevolence* would, diminish the *moral good* to the extent that they were necessary to move the *agent* to the action, or to make him do more *good* than he otherwise would have done; for by increasing the *interest*, or [I *positive*], to be subtracted, they diminish the *benevolence*. However, *additional interests* which were not necessary to have moved the *agent*—such as the *rewards* of a *good Being* for actions which he would have undertaken without a reward—do not diminish the *virtue*. In this matter, however, no mortal is capable of judging.

<summary>Hutcheson argues that the moral value of an action is determined by intention and ability rather than the final outcome, and that external rewards only reduce the moral credit of an action if they were the primary motivation.</summary>

another. Nor do the prospects of *grateful* <margin>Section 7.</margin> *returns* for benefits which we would have conferred freely diminish the *generosity*. This corollary may be applied to the *rewards of a future state*, if any person conceives them as distinct from the pleasures of *virtue* itself; if they are not conceived as something distinct from those pleasures, then the very *desire* for them is strong evidence of a *virtuous disposition*.

3. EXTERNAL *advantage* that excites us to actions with an *evil tendency* toward others diminishes the *evil* of the action, if without this prospect of *advantage* we would not have undertaken them. Such advantages include the prospects of *great rewards*, the avoiding of *tortures*, or even the *unsettling solicitations* of violent *selfish passions*: and this is commonly called the *greatness of temptation*. The reason for this is the same as in the former case, since H = $\frac{M-I}{A}$ <note>In this formula, H represents the degree of moral evil or hatred, M is the moment of evil produced, I is the self-interest or external pressure, and A is the agent's ability.</note>. We may also remember here that we are more uneasy in the presence of *pain* than we are at the absence of *good*; and therefore *torture* is a more extenuating circumstance than *bribes* leading us to *evil*, because [I] is greater.

Thus, in cooperating with *gratitude*, *natural affection*, or *friendship*, we demonstrate less *virtue* in any given amount of good produced than in equally important actions of *general benevolence*: But *ingratitude* to a *benefactor*, *negligence* of the *interests* of a *friend* or *relative*, or returning evil actions, are vastly more odious than equal negligence or evil actions toward *strangers*.

14. When we cannot follow two different inclinations of *benevolence* at once, we are to prefer gratifying the stronger inclination according to the wise order of nature, which has established these attachments: thus, we are to be *grateful* rather than *liberal*, and serve a *friend* or *kinsman* rather than a stranger of only equal *virtue*, when we cannot do both.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of divine authority, moving from the concept of God’s right to rule based on benevolence to considering whether his right is instead based on his status as Creator and owner of his works.</meta>

<page-num>273</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

IX. <margin>Section 7.</margin> SOME imagine that the *property* the *Creator* has in all his *works* must be the *true foundation* of his *right* to govern. Among men, indeed, we find it necessary for the *public good* that no one should arbitrarily dispose of the goods acquired by the *labor* of another, which we call his *property*; and hence we imagine that *creation* is the *only* foundation of God's *dominion*. But if the reason for establishing the *rights of property* does not hold against a *perfectly wise* and *benevolent being*, I see no reason why *property* should be necessary to his *dominion*; and the reason does not hold: for an *infinitely wise* and *good being* could never (by assuming authority) counteract the *universal good*. The tie of *gratitude* is stronger, indeed, than bare *benevolence*; and therefore, supposing two *equally wise* and *good beings*, the one being our *creator* and the other not, we would think ourselves more obliged to obey our *creator*. But supposing our *creator* were *malicious*, and a *good being* condescended to rescue us, or govern us better, with sufficient power to accomplish his kind intentions, his *right* to govern would be perfectly good. But this is rather a matter of *curious speculation* than *utility*, since both titles of *benevolence* and *property* coincide in the *one and only* true DEITY, as far as we can know, joined with *infinite wisdom* and *power*.

X. IF it is here asked, “Could not the DEITY have given us a *different* or *contrary determination of mind, namely,* to *approve actions upon another foundation* than *benevolence?*” It is certain there is nothing in this surpassing the natural power of the DEITY. But as in the first treatise <note>Section 8, Article 2, Paragraph 5.</note> we resolved the constitution of our present *sense* of *beauty* into the *divine goodness*, so with much more obvious reason may we ascribe the present constitution of our *moral sense* to his *goodness*. For if the DEITY is really *benevolent*, or *delights* in the happiness of others, he could not rationally act otherwise, or give us a *moral sense* upon another foundation, without counteracting his own *benevolent intentions*. For, upon the supposition of a contrary *sense*, every rational *being* must have been concerned in some degree about his own external happiness: reflection on the circumstances of mankind in this world would have suggested, that a *universal benevolence* and *social temper*, or a course of such external actions, would most effectively promote the external good of everyone, according to the reasonings of CUMBERLAND and PUFFENDORF; while at the same time this *perverted sense of morality* would have made us un-

<summary>The author argues that God gave humans a moral sense based on benevolence because it aligns with His own benevolent nature and promotes universal happiness. He suggests that any other moral foundation would have contradicted God's goodness and created a conflict between human happiness and moral approval.</summary>
<keywords>Deity, benevolence, moral sense, divine goodness, Cumberland, Puffendorf, human happiness, determination of mind</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that a perverted moral sense would have left humans perpetually conflicted between their self-interest and a hypothetical approval of harmful behavior.</meta>

<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

<margin>
Section 7.
</margin>
easy in such a course, and inclined us to the quite contrary, *namely, barbarity, cruelty,* and *fraud;* and *universal war,* according to Mr. Hobbes, would really have been our *natural state;* so that in every action we must have been distracted by two contrary principles, and perpetually miserable and dissatisfied when we followed the directions of either.

XI. IT has been often taken for granted in *these papers*, "That the DEITY is *morally good;*" though the reasoning is not at all built upon this supposition. If we would know the reason for the great agreement of mankind in this opinion, we shall perhaps find no demonstrative arguments *a priori*, from the idea of an *Independent Being*, to prove his *goodness:* but there is abundant probability deduced from the whole frame of nature, which seems, as far as we know, plainly contrived for the *good* of the *whole;* and the casual evils seem the necessary concomitants of some mechanism designed for vastly prepollent <note>Predominant or superior in power or influence.</note> good. Indeed, this very *moral sense*, implanted in *rational agents*, to delight in and admire whatever actions flow from a study of the *good of others*, is one of the strongest evidences of *goodness* in the AUTHOR of Nature. But these reflections are in no way as universal as the opinion, nor are they often instilled by anyone.

<summary>The author argues that without a moral sense rooted in benevolence, humans would be perpetually conflicted between self-interest and destructive impulses. He also suggests that while God's goodness is widely accepted, it is most clearly evidenced by the benevolent design of the universe and the existence of our own moral sense.</summary>

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY, &c.</header>

### Sect. 7.
What, then, more probably leads *mankind* into that opinion is this: the obvious *structure* of the *world* gives us ideas of *boundless wisdom* and *power* in its AUTHOR. We cannot conceive of such a *Being* as *needy*, and we must conclude him to be *happy* and in the best state possible, since he can always gratify himself. We are necessarily led to imagine that the *best state* of rational *agents*, and their greatest and most *worthy happiness*, must consist in *universal, effective benevolence*; and from this, we conclude that the DEITY is benevolent in the most *universal and impartial manner*. Nor can we easily imagine what else deserves the name of *perfection* other than *benevolence* and those *capacities* or *abilities* which are necessary to make it effective—such as *wisdom* and *power*—at least, we can have no other valuable conception of it.
